# SAIRN Open Work Index

**One place to see everything that is open across the whole platform.**
Last rebuilt: **2026-08-23**. Last updated: **2026-09-05** (sd-data's anonymous 268-name disclosure closed and independently reviewed, five residual rows opened; SAIRNscape's `scpLd()` fixed, 7 silent loaders left; StoneDesk's public surface has no tables; SAIRNroofing's published-PIN row closed and re-verified live).

Before this file existed, open work lived in `SAIRN-BACKLOG.md`, in
`SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK.md`, in ~40 per-app handoffs, and in skill files — so real
items got missed and sessions sat idle while work existed. This is the index.
It does not replace those files; each row points back at the source of truth.

**Note, 2026-08-24:** `SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK.md` was split per session to stop the
recurring merge conflicts. Its historical entries are still there (unchanged),
but new active-work entries go in `SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK-hank.md`,
`SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK-cc.md`, `SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK-cody.md`, and
`SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK-fourth.md` (one per clone). **The next rebuild of this index
must read all four, not just the shared file.**

---

## How to use this in 30 seconds

1. Filter by **Owner** — `unassigned` rows are the ones a free session can take.
2. Skip anything whose **Blocked by** is not empty unless you can clear the block.
3. Do what **Next action** says. If it says *verify*, the row's status is a claim
   that has not been re-checked; verify before building on it.

**Every row is a claim, not a fact.** Same standard as a handoff: re-verify
before you act on one. Rows marked ⚠️ are ones this rebuild could not confirm.

The tool reports and never edits: what a component is trusted FOR is a judgement, and a regenerator would delete exactly the part that matters. | M |
| **Platform** | ~~Two CDN scripts load with NO Subresource Integrity, and one is a floating major~~ &mdash; **there were FOUR, and all four are now pinned and hashed** | **CLOSED 2026-09-10** &mdash; three static tags by `799d78db`, the fourth (JS-injected) by Hank the same day; `soup_register_check` now FAILS rather than notes on a regression | &mdash; | &mdash; | **Found 2026-09-10 (Hank) while building the SOUP register; closed the same day in two parts, and the second part is the interesting one.** `799d78db` pinned and hashed the three **static** script tags &mdash; `@supabase/supabase-js@2` to 2.116.0 on `sairnbiz.html` and `stonedesk.html`, and `qrcodejs@1.0.0` on `stonedesk.html` &mdash; each with `crossorigin=anonymous`, without which the browser does not check the hash at all. That commit also **removed StoneDesk's Layer 12 trusted-host allowlist**, which named the only two hosts the page loads scripts from, so a check for missing integrity had reported clean for its entire existence while the condition it tests for was true twice in the same file. **A FOURTH was still running and the scan that found the other three could not see it:** `sairnlaw.html` loaded `tesseract.js@5` &mdash; floating major, no hash &mdash; **injected from JS**, so Semgrep's `missing-integrity`, Layer 12, and `soup_register_check` all walked past it. Pinned to 5.1.1 with a `sha384` hash cross-checked two ways (computed locally twice, and jsDelivr's published sha256 for the same file recomputed locally and matched). **The honest limit, recorded in the register rather than glossed:** the hash covers **one of four artefacts tesseract fetches at run time**. The entry script is hashed; the worker, the wasm core and the language traineddata are fetched by the library itself and there is no supported way to hash them. The covered one is the one that runs in the page's own context with the session token &mdash; a reduction in reach, not a boundary. The OCR failure message no longer blames the connection, since an integrity mismatch now fails down that same path. **Standing lesson:** *grep for the defective SHAPE, not the defective line* &mdash; the same rule that caught the hook carrying its own copy of the `git checkout origin/main` bug. | S |
| **SAIRNlaw** | ~~**Three of tesseract's four run-time fetches cannot be hashed, and `sairnlaw.html` has no Content-Security-Policy to bound where they come from**~~ &mdash; **CLOSED: a CSP now bounds them, verified in a real browser on the live page** | **CLOSED 2026-09-10 (CC)** &mdash; `1b6c3746`. Found 2026-09-10 (Hank) while closing the SRI row | CC (fix) / Hank (finding) | &mdash; | **SRI answers *"is this the file I expected"*; only a CSP answers *"which hosts may this page talk to at all"*, and that was the half missing.** `tesseract.min.js` is pinned and hashed &mdash; one of four artefacts. It loads `worker.min.js`, which fetches `tesseract.js-core` (wasm) and the `@tesseract.js-data` traineddata, all from jsDelivr, none able to carry an integrity attribute. A hostile worker receives every client document put through OCR &mdash; privileged material &mdash; and can reach the network. **DELIVERED AS A META TAG, a deliberate difference from `stonedesk.html`,** which assembles its policy in script at run time: a meta CSP binds from the moment the parser reaches it, a JS-assembled one cannot bind anything the page did before that script ran. Same policy, strictly earlier; the probe asserts this file does not acquire a run-time assembly later. **EVERY SOURCE DERIVED FROM WHAT THIS FILE USES, not copied.** `connect-src` is the seven `sairn.vercel.app` endpoints plus `cdn.jsdelivr.net`; **`law.cornell.edu` is deliberately NOT listed** &mdash; it appears four times and every one is a `source_url` rendered into an href, link text and never a fetch. `'wasm-unsafe-eval'` is required or the OCR core cannot instantiate &mdash; **omitting it is the failure mode that reads as a stricter policy and silently breaks OCR.** `'unsafe-inline'` is required and MEASURED (177 inline handlers), not shrugged at. **No `'unsafe-eval'`** &mdash; zero `eval()`/`new Function()`, counted with comments stripped. `object-src`/`frame-ancestors` `none`, `base-uri`/`form-action` `self`. **VERIFIED IN A REAL BROWSER, TWICE, not parsed and assumed.** Locally over http from `127.0.0.1`: zero violations on load, `loadTesseract()` 793ms, `Tesseract.recognize()` returned *"MOTION"* in 8.7s &mdash; which exercises the worker, the wasm core AND the traineddata, exactly the three fetches SRI cannot cover. **Negative controls, because zero violations proves nothing unless the policy can refuse:** `fetch` to example.com blocked, `<script src=example.com>` blocked, `new Function()` blocked. The local API failure was **CORS, not CSP** &mdash; proven by a `no-cors` request to the same host going out with no violation, rather than inferred from the error text. **Then on the LIVE page after deploy:** CSP present, gate renders, `/api/sd-data` reached (401 as expected unauthenticated), OCR returned *"AFFIDAVIT"*, zero violations. **MY OWN PROBE WAS WRONG FIRST, IN THE SAME WAY AS THAT MORNING'S LAYER 30 PROBE:** it substring-counted `eval(` over the whole file and matched the new CSP comment, which says *"zero `eval()`"* &mdash; failing a correct file. It strips comments now. **A check that cannot tell a quoted example from a live call is the same class of wrong as the thing it tests, and that is twice in one day.** Held by `tests/sairnlaw_csp.js`, 16 arms. | M |
| **Platform** | ~~Two moderate Dependabot alerts open and untriaged~~ &mdash; **both were `qs`, fixed by a lockfile-only bump, and the REASON they sat untriaged was assumed rather than tested** | **CLOSED 2026-09-10 (Hank)** &mdash; `npm audit` now reports 0 vulnerabilities; `tools/npm_audit_check.py` built and WIRED as report-only checker 18 | Hank | &mdash; | **Found 2026-09-10 (Hank). The interesting half is why they sat.** The SOUP register recorded them as untriaged with a stated reason: *"the alert detail needs the GitHub UI or an authenticated API call, and `gh` is not installed in this clone."* **That reason was assumed and it was wrong.** `npm audit --json` reads the committed lockfile and the public npm registry and needs no GitHub credential at all. One command named both advisories. **The blocker was never the missing tool &mdash; it was that nothing ran the check that did not need one.** **Both alerts were the same package:** `qs@6.15.3`, transitive via `stripe@17.7.0`'s `^6.11.0`. `GHSA-x5fp-wj9c-mxmx` (array-limit bypass, `qs.parse`) and `GHSA-4mjr-xmp4-gh2g` (DoS via a non-callable `constructor.isBuffer`). 6.16.0 satisfies the existing range, so the fix is **lockfile-only** &mdash; one package changed, `package.json` untouched, still three direct dependencies. Done with `npm update qs --package-lock-only` after `npm install qs@6.16.0` was tried first and **added `qs` as a fourth DIRECT dependency**, which would have put a register entry on a package no SAIRN file imports. **Reachability read rather than assumed, because it changes what the fix is worth.** `GHSA-x5fp` was **never reachable**: the published `stripe@17.7.0` tarball references `qs` exactly once, in `cjs/utils.js`, and calls **`qs.stringify` only** &mdash; never `parse`. `GHSA-4mjr` **was** reachable, and by a wider door than its own advisory describes: the defect is one line, `obj.constructor.isBuffer && obj.constructor.isBuffer(obj)` (truthy, not callable) becoming `typeof ... === 'function'` in 6.16.0, so **any** object with an own truthy non-function `constructor.isBuffer` hits it and `JSON.parse` sets such a key freely. `api/sairncash/checkout.js` passes `req.body.email` straight into `stripe.checkout.sessions.create`, so `{"email":{"constructor":{"isBuffer":"x"}}}` on an unauthenticated POST is that shape &mdash; **no `qs.parse` required**. What bounded it was incidental, not designed: every Stripe call sits in a `try/catch` answering a 500, and `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` is unset so `checkout.js` returns *"Stripe not configured"* first. **Two accidental bounds is why the bump is the fix and the analysis is not.** **WIRED, and the near-miss is worth recording:** it was nearly held back on an assumed ~30s network cost. **Measured instead: 8.1s and 8.5s, against a sweep that already takes 146s** &mdash; about 5%. Network failure is exit 3 and lands in UNRUN, never in findings and never quietly in a pass. **Its first version reported `SKIPPED: npm is not on PATH` on this machine**, because npm is `npm.cmd` on Windows and `subprocess.run(['npm', ...])` raises &mdash; an honest exit 3 that would have printed on every push in every clone forever. Caught by running it, not reading it. **Real negative control:** against the pre-bump lockfile it exits 1 and names both advisories by URL. | S |
| **Platform** | ~~**The server scoped the read; the LOCAL CACHE outlived the person it was fetched for**~~ &mdash; **CLOSED for every app that carries a scoped read except one, and the scope is DERIVED rather than estimated** | **CLOSED 2026-09-10 (CC)** &mdash; `b50c2e2f` (care, senior, dental) then ``08e7c8d5`` (build, stonedesk, roofing), all live-verified. **`sairndesign` remains &mdash; flagged BY NAME for Fourth, who holds that file** | CC | **Fourth: `sdn_clients`** | **THE DEFECT.** Every hydrate in these apps is an **additive merge** &mdash; it pushes server rows the cache lacks and never removes one it has &mdash; and each logout cleared only the session key. On a device where two people sign in, which is the **ordinary** case in these industries, the rows the first person hydrated were still in localStorage for the second, feeding both the on-screen panels and (in two apps) the outbound AI prompt &mdash; a prompt that told the model the list was *"already scoped server-side"*. **MY OWN EARLIER ROW SAID "TWELVE APPS, UNTRIAGED" AND THAT WAS A GUESS FROM THE WRONG SIGNAL.** It counted apps with per-employee auth. The right signal is the READ ITSELF: `api/sd-data.js` contains **exactly nine branches** that filter rows by the caller's own employee id, and they belong to **six apps, not twelve** &mdash; `alf_clients`, `alf_mar` (SAIRNcare); `sen_clients`, `sen_visits` (SAIRNsenior); `bld_bids`, `bld_tna` (SAIRNbuild); `sd_crm` (StoneDesk); `rf_jobs` (SAIRNroofing); `sdn_clients` (SAIRNdesign). SAIRNdental is a seventh by a different mechanism (`DNT_FINANCIAL_RESOURCES` + `DNT_PATIENT_SCOPED_RESOURCES`). **The other six apps with per-employee auth have NO scoped read at all**, so there was nothing to leak &mdash; `sairnbiz`, `sairncode`, `sairnlaw`, `sairnlegacy`, `sairnmechanical`, `sairnscape`, `sairngrounds`. Counting the gate instead of the login turned an open-ended sweep into a finite list, and **arm 0 of the probe asserts that count is still 9** so a new scoped read cannot quietly land outside it. **THREE CLOSED IN THE SECOND PASS, and two of them did not fit the first pass's shape, which is why they are asserted separately rather than bent into it.** **SAIRNbuild** is the same shape exactly &mdash; `bld_bids` is assignment-scoped and `bld_tna` is an employee's OWN training-needs assessment, both additively hydrated into localStorage. **StoneDesk** has ONE scoped key (`sd_crm`) among many practice-wide ones and **three sign-in paths**, not one enter function &mdash; wiring a guard into only the PIN path would have left passkey and bootstrap unprotected while reading, from the diff, as if the job were done; the probe asserts all three. **SAIRNroofing persists NOTHING scoped** &mdash; checked, not assumed: it writes only `rf_license_key` and `rf_session` &mdash; so its exposure was the module variable `currentJobs` surviving a same-tab sign-out and sign-in until `rfLoadJobs()` returned. Transient and self-correcting, and still the previous user's board on screen. One line. **THE TWO-LIST RULE HELD AGAIN AND CAUGHT ANOTHER PHANTOM:** SAIRNbuild's first draft named `bld_tna_assessments`, which is the SERVER resource name and not a key the app writes &mdash; same mistake as `alf_careplans` in the first pass, caught the same way. A stale floor after growth is cosmetic and gets bumped as housekeeping; friction on every rebase is what gets a gate switched off, and a switched-off gate protects nothing. **THE FIRST VERSION OF THAT PIN WAS WRONG IN BOTH WAYS THIS ROW IS ABOUT, and neither showed in its diff: it said 163 while `discover()` returned 180** (seventeen files deletable in silence -- 85% of its own twenty-file example), **and it was added to `_main_body()` and NOT to `_hook_body()`, the copy that runs UNATTENDED after every push in every clone.** That is the half that mattered: your own deletion is in your own diff, a deletion arriving by REBASE from another clone was visible to nothing else. CLAUDE.md's `sairn_claim_hook.py` lesson, hit again without being recognised. The probe now asserts ONE definition of the constant and exactly TWO comparisons against it.<br><br>**2. `report_only_checks.py` REPORTED A CLEAN PLATFORM AFTER SCANNING ZERO APPS.** Six of its 21 promoted entries run per-app over 22 apps -- 132 checker runs -- and when the target list came back empty the loop never executed, `findings` and `unrun` both stayed `[]`, and the hook printed **exactly what a clean sweep of all 22 prints**. Proved by forcing `app_files()` to `[]`, not argued. Two ways in, both closed: **`git ls-files` failing was read as "no apps"** (return code never checked -- git off the hook's PATH, an index locked by one of the four clones mid-rebase), and **zero targets now lands in `unrun`**. A DELETED app is deliberately not what this guards -- removing an app removes the risk with it; what it guards is the list emptying for a reason **nobody chose**.<br><br>**3. `preauth_oracle_check.py`** already audited its accepted-exemptions file for entries that matched nothing, on its own reasoning that a suppression outlives what it suppressed; the **declarations** file had no equivalent, so a public-by-design declaration could outlive its handler silently. `STALE DECLARATION` closes it, mutation-proved. It also printed four counts and never said how many handlers it LOOKED AT -- `HANDLERS_SCANNED` closes that.<br><br>**THE CWD-RELATIVE CLASS WAS MEASURED, NOT REASONED ABOUT:** four checkers defaulting to a glob relative to the current directory were run from `docs/` and their real output read. Held by `tests/checker_cwd_anchoring_probe.py`, whose assertion is **DIFFERENTIAL**: the same checker run from the repo root and from `docs/` must produce **byte-identical** output, paired with a non-triviality arm because identical-but-empty would satisfy equality while meaning the opposite. **`sairn_seam_check.py` took TWO passes** -- anchoring its glob left a second `os.path.isfile()` on a repo-relative name still asking the current directory, so every delegate resolved False and it still printed three zeros; **two places, one assumption, and fixing the loud one and stopping is the shape this repo keeps recording**. `report_only_checks.py` was never exposed because it passes `cwd=REPO` to every subprocess -- the same fix one layer up. **The non-triviality arm ALSO got this wrong first**: one loose regex hunting the word *file* failed `sairn_seam_check.py` for the wrong reason, since it discloses coverage as `75 clean, ...` and names no files at all.<br><br>**NOT DONE, SAID PLAINLY: the remaining ~75 files in `tools/` were not individually read** -- candidates came from grepping for filesystem discovery, so a checker deriving its subject by parsing or importing would not have appeared. | **Tooling** | **The defect register pointed at commits that never existed on `main`, and the cause was STRUCTURAL rather than anybody's mistake** | **CLOSED 2026-09-11 (Fourth)** &mdash; `tools/defect_register.py` + `docs/defect-density-register.json` + `tests/run_defect_register_probe.py` (37 arms, was 24) | Fourth | &mdash; | **IT GREW FROM 5 PROBLEMS TO 12 IN THE TWELVE HOURS BETWEEN NOTICING IT AND FIXING IT**, which is what turned this from housekeeping into a class. `--add` derives the SHA from the commit in front of it; with four clones pushing, most commits are rebased onto somebody else's work before they reach origin, so **the SHA recorded is the pre-rebase one and the commit it names never existed on `main`.** Every record written-then-rebased goes stale on its own. **The consequence was not cosmetic: this is one of the promoted report-only checkers, so EVERY post-push sweep in EVERY clone carried a finding** &mdash; exactly how a report-only checker earns the reputation that gets it switched off before it is ever promoted to a gate. **THE FIX IS TO TREAT THE SHA AS WHAT IT ACTUALLY IS, a convenience pointer**, and the record's identity as its SUBJECT, which a rebase preserves. `--check` now re-resolves by subject and REPORTS the drift instead of failing; it still FAILS when neither half resolves, which is the real *"a register pointing at nothing, whose length reads as evidence"* case, and it FAILS on an AMBIGUOUS subject rather than guessing which commit a record meant. `--reseat` writes them back, as a separate command because a checker that edits what it checks is not a checker &mdash; and deliberately **does not re-sort**, since the sort key is `(date, commit)` and re-seating twelve SHAs produced a **157-line diff for a 12-line change** (measured, the first version did exactly that; a repair nobody can review is a repair nobody checks). **A SECOND, QUIETER HOLE UNDERNEATH THE LOUD ONE:** the vocabulary checks sat after a `continue`, so the moment a SHA went stale the rest of that record stopped being checked at all &mdash; it would have outlived the fix for the loud one. All 12 re-seated; `--check` clean; **`run_report_only_checks_probe.py` H6 ("a clean sweep stays silent") passes for the first time**. The probe's ambiguity arm MANUFACTURES its fixture with two empty commits in the detached throwaway worktree rather than hoping a duplicate subject exists &mdash; **a skipped arm is an untested branch wearing a green tick**, which is what the first version was | S |

Measured today with the clone behind origin: `outgoing_files(repo, None, "HEAD")` and `outgoing_subjects(repo, None, "HEAD")` both return `[]` with HEAD an ancestor of `origin/main`. **Reachable by ordinary pushes that send real content** &mdash; `git push origin HEAD:some-branch` while behind, an older-sha push, a force-push after origin moved back. **NOT demonstrated end to end** (a real denied seed change slipping through) and `outgoing_subjects()` is UNVERIFIED beyond that one measurement though it feeds check 8 at `:1288` &mdash; both stated as open rather than implied closed. **FAILING CLOSED IS NOT FREE:** the gate BLOCKS, and the cases above are legitimate pushes, so a naive inversion denies real work and teaches `SAIRN_SEED_GATE=off` | M |
| **Tooling** | **One `cd` into a subdirectory bricks every Bash call for the rest of the session, and the command that would undo it is blocked too** | **CLOSED 2026-09-11 (Fourth)** &mdash; all 9 hook commands anchored to `${CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR:-.}`, plus the one script the fix would otherwise have EXPOSED; `tests/hook_command_anchoring_probe.py` | Fourth | &mdash; | Every hook command in `.claude/settings.json` is a RELATIVE path (`python tools/sairn_push_gate_hook.py`) and hooks run in the **Bash tool's persisted working directory**. | **Process** | ~~29 rows claim negative controls and before 2026-09-08 NOT ONE app-fix suite had any~~ &mdash; **THAT HEADLINE WAS MINE AND IT WAS WRONG. Corrected within the hour: controls are common, they just live INSIDE the suites** | Open (decision), **and the first version of this row is retracted** &mdash; swept 2026-09-08 (CC) on Michael's direction, then corrected by CC the same session | **Michael** &mdash; a much smaller scope call than the first version implied | &mdash; | **WHAT I GOT WRONG, FIRST, BECAUSE IT IS THE POINT.** I swept for negative controls by looking for separate PROBE FILES, found 27 of them, dated every one, and reported that *"every probe predating 2026-09-08 targets a tool, a hook or a live endpoint &mdash; not one is a negative control over an app-fix suite"*. **That is false.** Twenty-one suites carry their controls INLINE, as arms inside the suite itself, and most predate today: `api/sd-data-dental-ledger-validation.test.js` runs **fourteen** &mdash; *"MUTATION (validator stubbed to null): dnt_payments {amount:-500} reaches the store"* &mdash; on the dental MONEY ledger; `tests/sairnbiz_server_backup.js` reverts each fix in lifted source; `api/sairndental/public-book-guardian.test.js` replaces `if(bkIsMinor(dob))` with `if(false)`. **I found it by running one of the suites I had just called unsupported.** A count is only as true as the shape you looked for, which is the lesson this row exists to record and which I then demonstrated on myself. **THE CORRECTED MEASUREMENT.** Controls exist in TWO shapes and both are legitimate: a separate harness (27 files) and in-suite arms (21 suites). Re-checking the ten-row sample with both shapes counted, **about half had real committed controls** &mdash; not eight of ten missing. Still unsupported in that sample: `tests/sv_storage_guard.js`, `tests/sairnlaw_hydrate.js`, `tests/sairndental_coverage_edit.js`, `api/sd-data-dental-settings-patch.test.js` (it MODELS the store but never breaks the fix), and `tests/sairnsenior_org_hydrate.js` &mdash; **which is one of my own rows from earlier tonight**, claiming three mutation probes that were run by hand and never committed. **WHAT SURVIVES OF THE ORIGINAL FINDING, AND IT IS WORTH KEEPING.** The claim in a row is still a fact about a past run rather than a property of the artefact, and the reader cannot tell which without opening the file. Both times the missing controls really were written this session they found a real defect &mdash; a payroll total reading `$0` for *"nothing priceable"*, and an assertion PINNING the defect its own failure message named. But the practice is in far better shape than the first version of this row said. **A SCANNER WAS BUILT AND DELETED, AND THAT CALL LOOKS BETTER NOW, NOT WORSE.** Three iterations still mis-classified known cases both ways &mdash; it called `api/sd-data.js` itself a harness, then called `tests/local_only_probe.py` not one &mdash; and it would ALSO have missed every in-suite control, so shipping it would have put this same wrong headline beside twenty rows with a machine's authority. **THE RECOMMENDATION IS UNCHANGED AND CHEAPER THAN THE BACKFILL**: a row either says *"N mutation probes, held by `tests/X_probe.py`"*, or *"N control arms, in the suite"*, or *"N negative controls, run by hand this session"*. All three are true sentences; the reader can then tell without opening anything. The backfill is now a handful of named suites rather than twenty.  **BACKFILLED 2026-09-08 (CC): all five now have committed controls** &mdash; `tests/suite_control_backfill_probe.py`, one table-driven harness rather than five near-identical files, **17 controls across the five suites, every one biting**, all five target sources restored byte-identical by sha256. The five rows now say which shape backs their number, and **their original counts are preserved rather than overwritten with mine** &mdash; *"two negative controls (run by hand at the time; 3 are now COMMITTED)"* &mdash; because what a past session RAN and what the repo HOLDS are two different facts, and flattening them would be the same error this row exists to record. **TWO CONTROLS CAME BACK SILENT FIRST AND BOTH TAUGHT SOMETHING.** Disabling SAIRNvet's early `_svUnreadable[key]` return changed nothing, because on the FIRST call that latch is still false and what actually refuses a corrupt value is the parse-verification block below it &mdash; **a control has to break what the arm exercises, not what looks like the guard.** And turning a failed SAIRNsenior read into `rows=[]` is invisible: an empty list adds nothing, so nothing is written, so *"leaves that resource alone"* still holds. The defect that arm really guards is a failed read WIPING local data, so that is what the control now injects. **AND THE FULL RE-SWEEP SAYS TEN ROWS, NOT FIVE.** The five above were a sample of ten; sweeping all 29 found five more. But those five name a SOURCE file rather than their suite (`law-auth.js`, `public-book.js`, `sairn_load_state_check.py`) and their controls exist under a name the row never mentions &mdash; a WORDING problem, not a missing-control one, and exactly what the convention above fixes. Two rows still name no file at all. | M |

Nobody is losing a working feature; there was never a working feature. **Next action: run `sql/stonedesk_public_surface_schema.sql`.** It is additive and idempotent, creates all five tables (`sd_public_shop`, `sd_quote_requests`, `sd_customers`, `sd_order_links`, `sd_public_rate_limits`), enables RLS with service-role-only policies, and its grants already include the `update` that the counter's `merge-duplicates` upsert needs at plan time — the exact verb whose absence took `sairncash_waitlist` down for hours on 2026-08-26. `tools/sairn_sql_preflight.py` reports it clean, **which is not a pass** — that tool compares against the repo's own CREATE TABLE statements, so a file written and never run looks PRESENT to it, which is precisely this case. **Confirm by re-probing the two endpoints after the run, not by the editor reporting success** | S |

Decide soft- vs hard-delete, then widen the gate — note the gate and the handler branch are separate, and registering one without the other yields an unreachable 400 (`api/sd-data.js` says so in its own comment). **The DB grant no longer pre-authorises hard-delete** — if this is ever built, the grant must be re-added deliberately for the specific tables chosen, which is the point. **AND IT DID NOT NEED TO BE. Michael's decision 2026-09-08: SOFT delete** — the record is marked and hidden, the row stays and stays recoverable, and a hard-delete path for a genuine erasure request is a separate action if it is ever wanted. **The marker lives inside the existing `data` jsonb and the write is an UPDATE, so no new privilege was needed and `sql/stonedesk_data_schema.sql` still grants select/insert/update and no delete** — asserted, not just intended. **THE VERB IS `soft_delete`, NOT `delete`, deliberately:** `delete` already exists here and means a real DELETE (the SAIRNcode Compliance-Admin branch issues the only `method: 'DELETE'` in the file), and two verbs that destroy different amounts of data must not share a name — a caller copying a working call from one family to another would silently get the other behaviour. This proposal adds **no** additional table-privilege change. **What is genuinely new is everything that is not a table privilege:** `usage` on schema `public`; sequences; routines (the five SQL functions `revoke all … from public` but name neither role explicitly); and widening `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES` from tables-only to all object types. **THE CASE FOR:** the key is public by design, so least privilege matters more on `anon` than on any private role; both roles are now proven unused by two independent methods; and the one feature that might have justified `anon` — a public intake form doing `INSERT` — is dead at both ends (see the row above), so there is no live use and no pending one. **THE CASE AGAINST, stated properly rather than waved at:** exactly one statement carries real risk — `revoke usage on schema public from anon, authenticated`. Its blast radius is **not knowable from this repo**, because Supabase internals (Studio, Realtime, PostgREST schema-cache reload) may touch `public` as those roles in ways no file here describes. Everything else is verifiable from the catalog; that one is not. Reversal is a single `GRANT`, so the cost is low, but the failure mode could be non-obvious and land somewhere nobody is watching. **RECOMMENDED: SPLIT IT.** Stage A — tables, sequences, routines, and the widened default privileges: no operational unknowns, fully verifiable, fold into the existing file. Stage B — the schema `usage` revoke alone, its own run, its own window, its own verification, so that if something does break the cause is unambiguous. **Do not run them together**; a combined failure would leave nobody able to say which half caused it. One further cost worth naming: if a public intake form is ever built the right way, `anon` needs `INSERT` on one table, and Stage B makes restoring that two steps rather than one. Small, acceptable, but it should be a known cost rather than a surprise. **STAGE A RAN AND VERIFIED 2026-08-26** — see the sweep row above. **STAGE B RUN PLAN, one statement, its own window:** `revoke usage on schema public from anon, authenticated;`. Before-state is already recorded by 3f (both roles hold `USAGE`). **Rollback is one line and should be in the clipboard before the revoke is run, not looked up afterwards:** `grant usage on schema public to anon, authenticated;`. **Verify immediately after, in this order:** (1) re-run 3f, expect zero rows; (2) a real `api/sd-data.js` read through the proxy — that path runs as `service_role` and must be **unaffected**, so a failure there is the signal to roll back at once; (3) load the Supabase dashboard's table view, which is the part no query can predict and the reason this statement is not sharing a run with anything else. **SEQUENCING RECOMMENDATION — run the `supabase_admin` check FIRST** (the row above, read-only, minutes). It is independent of Stage B, but if it comes back live then a second granting role is handing `anon` full CRUD on new tables, and closing schema `USAGE` while that door is open is the smaller of the two jobs. Knowing which situation you are in should come before choosing which to do next. **`supabase_admin` came back inert (100% `postgres` ownership, 251 tables), so that gate is clear and Stage B is written: `sql/anon_authenticated_schema_usage_revoke_stage_b_2026-08-26.sql`** — its own file, not a section, for the same reason the stages are split at all. Read-only except the one commented `revoke`; R6, a before-state, and a **Section 1 that re-confirms Stage A is still clean before widening** — if any table grant has appeared since, this statement would hide a real grant behind a schema-level block instead of removing it, so that check runs first. Rollback is in the header to be opened *before* Section 2. Section 4 carries Stage A's held cleanup **and settles the count-drift prediction**: take the table count, drop both baselines, take it again — it must fall by **exactly 2**. Expect **249, not 248** (248 measured 2026-08-25, plus 2 baselines, plus `rf_settings` which genuinely landed since); anyone expecting 248 will read a correct result as a discrepancy | M |

| **Platform** | **Load-state gates: every per-licence reference table now has a read-only check that its live content matches the seed** | Open — built and proven, but nobody runs them on a schedule | unassigned | Needs a decision on invocation, not more tooling | Closes the class of defect that had LAW-PINNACLE-2026 computing federal answer deadlines THREE DAYS LATE for a day after the fix was pushed — the seed was corrected, the licence was never reloaded, and nothing could detect it because all 270 SAIRNlaw seed rules are `version: 1` including the two the correction changed. **CORRECTED 2026-08-29, within the hour of writing this row:** it originally named `tools/sairnlaw_build_load_gate.py` as a generator. That tool and its generated SQL were **deleted in `8ac8bf6`**, deliberately and correctly — `tools/sairn_load_state_check.py` is now canonical for SAIRNlaw. **⚠ NAME CORRECTED 2026-09-09 (Fourth): this row said `tools/deadline_load_state_check.py`, and no such file exists in any clone** — the canonical tool is the same `sairn_load_state_check.py` that serves all four apps, and a session following the old name would have gone looking for a tool that was never there. Three reasons given, and the second is a critique of my design I have no answer to: it needs only a licence key so it runs from any clone and can gate CI; **a generated gate must be regenerated after every seed edit, and a forgotten regeneration is silently the same failure class the gate exists to catch**; and it covers CALENDARS as well as rules, where the SQL gate globbed `seed_*.json` only and missed `law_holidays` entirely — the table the 2026-08-29 sweep found 5 diverged rows in. **Still live:** `tools/sairn_build_load_gates.py` for the four non-SAIRNlaw apps (alf_compliance_rules 16, alf_payer_rules 6, dnt_cred_rules 6, rf_cert_rules 3, rf_contingency_rules 2), because no licence-key equivalent exists for them yet. Those inherit the same regeneration weakness and should follow SAIRNlaw's shape when someone has cause to touch them. Re-run after any seed change; the `sql/*_load_gate_generated.sql` files are derived, never hand-edited. **Both sides compare SUBTRACTIVELY** — `to_jsonb(row)` minus bookkeeping columns versus the seed rule minus its id — so a compute column added later is covered by default rather than skipped by a stale list, which matters because these tables keep `count`/`unit`/`state` in PROMOTED COLUMNS, not only in `data`. The gates also mirror the server write-path normalisation (`state` uppercased, `status` defaulted to `active`); without that they false-STALE 5 of 6 alf_payer_rules rows on first run. **Proven retroactively**, not trusted for being careful: the SAIRNlaw gate independently named the same 7 stale rule ids the compute-diff found, by a different method, and returned 0 missing / 0 stale / 0 extra against PINNACLE. **THREE THINGS STILL OPEN:** (1) `sc_anesthesia_base_units` has NO gate because it has **no seed file in the repo** — a source-of-truth gap, not a tooling gap; (2) nobody runs these on a schedule, so a defect is still found by someone choosing to look; (3) `version` remains uniformly 1 and carries no signal — the gates work without it, deliberately. See `SAIRN-BACKLOG.md` "Nothing can tell you a live licence is running stale rules". **THE INVOCATION DECISION IS NOW WRITTEN UP: `docs/2026-09-09-load-state-gate-invocation.md` (Fourth).** Its finding, which this row does not currently say: the gate's ONLY caller is `sairn_push_gate_hook.py` check 1, and that fires only when the outgoing commits touch a seed file — so *a seed change cannot ship unloaded, and a quiet licence is never checked*. Recommends a Vercel cron behind `CRON_SECRET`, on the ground that it is the only option where the four customer licence keys do not move to a new home; the alternative (GitHub Actions) needs them as repo secrets. Two things are Michael's to decide before anyone builds it: where the alarm goes, and whether it gates or only reports | M |

The fallback is reached ONLY on RPC 404; a 403 or 500 throws, so narrowing the EXECUTE grant can never silently reinstate the race. **The other two defects are fixed in code and need nothing run**: the four token-gated functions now honour the verdict they used to discard, and `legal-citator.js` refuses instead of spending when the budget read fails. | **SAIRNdental** | ~~`issueGfe()` enforces 45 CFR 149.610(c)(1) in the BROWSER ONLY — the server stores `status: 'Issued'` on an incomplete Good Faith Estimate~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (Hank)** — `api/_lib/dental-gfe.js`, 13 assertions, 12 negative controls | Hank | — | `sairndental.html`'s `issueGfe()` refuses to mark an estimate Issued while any federally required element is missing, and its comment says exactly why: *"an estimate handed to a patient missing a required element is a non-compliant document that looks compliant... the practice believes it has met the obligation and has not."* **That refusal is browser JavaScript.** The write goes to `api/sd-data.js`'s generic `DNT_RESOURCES` block, which validates `payload.id` and nothing else, so the server accepts `status: 'Issued'` on a GFE missing any of them. **Not reachable through the ordinary UI** — unlike the guardian gap, which was — so this is a hardening item rather than a live defect, and it is its own row because a federal disclosure rule deserves its own pass rather than a ride-along in a commit about guardians. **DONE, and three decisions worth knowing.** (1) **Only an ISSUED estimate is checked** — a Draft is allowed to be incomplete, which is what a draft is for, so the common write costs nothing and the two extra reads are paid only when a document becomes a representation to a patient. (2) **The patient record and the practice identifiers are read SERVER-SIDE**, never taken from the payload: half the required elements live on records the caller is not sending, and trusting a caller's copy of the practice's own NPI would make the check theatre. (3) **A read that could not answer REFUSES** (503 `GFE_CHECK_UNAVAILABLE`) rather than passing — an estimate that goes out incomplete cannot be recalled, a failed save can be retried. **The element labels are asserted identical to `sairndental.html`'s own `gfeMissing()`, scraped rather than retyped**, so a server rule that drifts from the one the practice sees fails the suite instead of being discovered by a practice. **Eligibility is still NOT decided server-side, deliberately** — whether a GFE is owed at all turns on 149.610(b)(1)(ii), a question only a person can answer, and `gfeEligibility()` puts it to the user rather than answering it | S |
| **SAIRNdental** | The generic `DNT_RESOURCES` write validates `payload.id` and nothing else &mdash; **SEVENTEEN resources, not fifteen** | Open &mdash; **11 of 17 CLOSED, SIX REMAIN &mdash; and TWO of those six are DELIBERATELY UNWRITTEN, so the real remainder is FOUR.** `dnt_referrals`, `dnt_providers`, `dnt_provider_hours` and `dnt_txplans` 2026-09-11 (Hank). `dnt_procedure_types` 2026-09-09 (Fourth). `dnt_denial` 2026-09-05 (Cody). `dnt_patients` (guardian) and `dnt_gfe` (45 CFR 149.610(c)(1)) 2026-09-04 (Hank); **`dnt_payments`, `dnt_charges` and `dnt_coverage_rules` 2026-09-04 (CC)** &mdash; see `api/_lib/dental-ledger.js` | unassigned | &mdash; | **COUNT CORRECTED, AND THE DENOMINATOR MOVED ONCE.** This cell has been wrong twice: as *"3 of 15"* while listing four, and as *"of FIFTEEN"* &mdash; `DNT_RESOURCES` holds **SEVENTEEN** since `dnt_supplies` and `dnt_vendor_orders` joined it on 2026-09-10. **Counted off `DNT_RESOURCES` in `api/sd-data.js`, never off this prose**, and the boundary assertion in `api/sd-data-dental-ledger-validation.test.js` carries the same instruction. **SIX WITH NO DOMAIN CHECK, AND THE LIST IS NARROWER THAN IT LOOKS:** `dnt_operatories`, `dnt_recall_outreach`, `dnt_supplies`, `dnt_vendor_orders` &mdash; plus `dnt_ar` and `dnt_revenue`, which **nothing writes**. **MEASURED 2026-09-11 off every `sdnData('write','<literal>',...)` call in `sairndental.html`, because a prose tally has been wrong here twice:** `dnt_ar` and `dnt_revenue` have **ZERO writers**, and the app states why in its own comments &mdash; *"deliberately no write to `dnt_ar`: a stored ageing row is stale the next \[time\]... charges and payments that really exist. `dnt_ar` stays registered and unwritten"*, and *"`dnt_revenue` has no client accessor."* **A validator for a resource nothing writes is dead code**, so they are recorded as deliberately-unwritten rather than counted as outstanding work; if either gains a writer, that is when it needs one. **`dnt_referrals` CLOSED 2026-09-11 (Hank), and the sharp shape is the DIRECTION.** `rReferrals()` renders `H(r.direction === 'incoming' ? 'Incoming' : 'Outgoing')` &mdash; one strict literal with an **unconditional else** &mdash; so a capitalised `'Incoming'`, an `'in'`, or a **missing** direction each display as **OUTGOING**, the opposite of the truth, on a clinical referral, **with no unknown state available**. Measured in node against that expression. **And an unrecognised status reads as PENDING:** the dropdown marks an option `selected` only on `===`, a `<select>` with nothing selected shows its FIRST option, and that same dropdown is what writes the status back &mdash; so a Declined referral displays as Pending and a user confirms the wrong value by touching the row. `patient_name`, `external_party`, `date` and `reason` are each required &mdash; all four are rules `addReferral()` already refuses on, `patient_name` is the record's identity because linking a real patient is optional, and the **reason** is what makes a referral defensible under the anti-kickback rules the panel's own header cites. **`dnt_providers` CLOSED 2026-09-11 (Hank), and it was the SCOPING one:** `dntLinkedProvider()` finds the caller's row by `linked_employee_id` and the result becomes the patient set a non-broad-read role may see. **Deliberately a small validator, because most of the risk was already closed** &mdash; the management-only role gate (2026-08-27, because anyone who can edit the roster can grant themselves a patient list) and the duplicate-link 409 were both already there. What was left: a **nameless provider**, which the app refuses and the handler did not &mdash; three render sites do `H(pv ? pv.name : '(unknown provider)')`, so a row that EXISTS with an empty name draws a **BLANK cell**, which is worse than the labelled *"(unknown provider)"* a MISSING record gives, because the row cannot then be identified to fix it. And a **non-string `linked_employee_id`**, which `===` against the token's string employee_id can never match, so a provider who genuinely IS linked reads as unlinked and every patient read answers 403 `PROVIDER_NOT_LINKED` &mdash; fail-closed, and a support incident with no diagnosable cause because the roster panel still shows the link. `role` is deliberately NOT validated, with a test asserting the permissiveness: the only reader is `p.role \|\| 'Dentist'`, which defaults around anything. **AND A LATENT AUTHORIZATION COLLISION FOUND ON THE WAY, REPORTED AS LATENT RATHER THAN LIVE because both auth paths were checked first.** An unlinked provider row carries `linked_employee_id: ''` (`saveProviderEdit()` stores `$('pv-edit-login').value \|\| ''`), so a session whose `employee_id` were the empty string would match the **first unlinked provider** by `'' === ''` and inherit its patient list. **Not reachable today:** `api/dnt-auth.js` refuses an empty `employee_id` on bootstrap and the signin path takes it from the stored row. **But that is an incidental bound, not a control** &mdash; it lives in another file for another reason and nothing in the scoping code depended on it deliberately. `dntLinkedProvider()` now refuses an empty session id outright, held by two arms in `api/sd-data-dental-provider-scope.test.js` (16/16) with a **proven negative control**: remove the guard and the empty-id arm FAILS while the real-id control stays green. **`dnt_provider_hours` WAS TAKEN NEXT BECAUSE IT WAS THE ONLY ONE FEEDING AN UNAUTHENTICATED, PATIENT-FACING SURFACE.** `api/sairndental/public-availability.js` reads every hours row for the licence and generates the booking slots a member of the public is offered. Nothing in that path validated a block, and `addProviderHours()` checked only that a provider was chosen and both times were non-empty. **EIGHT SHAPES MEASURED AGAINST THE REAL SLOT LOOP in node &mdash; a Monday, 30-minute appointments, good block 09:00&ndash;11:00 gives 4 slots. SEVEN ARE SILENT:** `start_time` of `"9"`, `"abc"` or absent, an end at or before the start, and a `day_of_week` of `"monday"` or `"Mon"` each give **ZERO slots** &mdash; so the provider reads as **fully booked to every patient, permanently, with nothing logged**. That outcome has already happened in production once from a different cause, and `public-availability.js` still carries the comment recording it: a malformed query *"made every availability request return zero slots regardless of real provider hours &mdash; found live during the end-to-end booking test."* **THE EIGHTH FABRICATES INSTEAD OF HIDING, and it is why this resource jumped the queue.** `Date.UTC(y,m,d,29,0)` rolls into the next day, so `end_time` `"29:00"` from a 22:00 start produced **FOURTEEN slots, the last at 04:30 the following morning** &mdash; appointments offered to anonymous callers at times and on a day the practice is not open. An `<input type="time">` cannot produce it; the handler accepted it. **ONE DAY LIST NOW, NOT TWO, and that is the structural half of the fix.** `public-availability.js` declared its own `DAY_NAMES`; the validator has to compare against exactly that list, and a second copy could drift from the reader it protects &mdash; invisibly, in the silent direction. `DAY_NAMES` now lives in the pure validator module and the endpoint imports it, with a test asserting the endpoint no longer declares its own (comment-stripped, per the comment-quoting rule). **STRICTER THAN THE BROWSER ON ONE RULE, DELIBERATELY:** `addProviderHours()` does not check that end is after start, so the app itself could create an inverted block &mdash; which yields zero slots, i.e. is already broken. **NO LEGACY-ROW COST, verified:** that function only ever pushes a new record, there is no edit path, and `removeProviderHours()` is local-only because `dnt_provider_hours` has no removal verb at all &mdash; one of the 324 in `tools/removal_path_baseline.json`. **DELIBERATELY NOT VALIDATED:** overlapping blocks for one provider and day (they duplicate a slot rather than invent a wrong time, and merging is a product decision about split shifts), a block shorter than the appointment length (legitimately zero slots; the length is per-request), and seconds (`HH:MM:SS` parses identically). **HELD BY 220/220, with ten new MUTATION arms** &mdash; with the validator stubbed to `null`, each bad row reaches the store, so every assertion is known red against the pre-fix behaviour. **One ripple handled rather than hidden:** the scope test that used `dnt_provider_hours` as the representative *unvalidated* neighbour now correctly refuses that payload, so it was moved to `dnt_recall_outreach` and the move is stated where it happened. **And a defect in my own new arms, caught by running them:** four refusal arms asserted *"refused and still wrote"* by counting ANY fetch &mdash; but a truthy `linked_employee_id` makes this branch legitimately **READ** `dnt_providers` first for the duplicate-link check, so they failed on a refusal that stored nothing. They now count **POSTs only**, and the comment says why, because the store is still the thing being protected. **And two more ripples on the referral pass, both stated where they happened:** the scope test that used `dnt_referrals` as the representative *unvalidated* neighbour, and the boundary assertion itself, now correctly refuse those payloads &mdash; both moved to `dnt_recall_outreach`. | M |
| **SAIRNdental** | **A referral with no linked patient is INVISIBLE to every scoped provider role** &mdash; the front desk can file a referral the treating provider never sees, and nothing says so | Open &mdash; **found and MEASURED 2026-09-11 (Hank)** while validating `dnt_referrals`; deliberately NOT fixed by the validator, because the form makes the link optional on purpose | unassigned | &mdash; | **Measured, not reasoned about.** `dnt_referrals` is in `DNT_PATIENT_SCOPED_RESOURCES`, and the scoped read filters `d[key] != null && dntScopeIds[String(d[key])] === true`, while `dntScopeIds` is built with `if (x.data.patient_id)` &mdash; so an empty id is **never a key in the map**. Run against the real expressions: a referral with `patient_id: ''` or with no `patient_id` at all is filtered out for every scoped role, exactly like another provider's patient. **IT FAILS CLOSED, WHICH IS THE RIGHT DIRECTION &mdash; there is no leak here.** The cost is the other one: the referrals panel offers *"Link to Existing Patient (optional)"*, so the front desk can file a referral **for** a patient without linking the record, and the treating provider then never sees it in their feed. **Nothing discloses that**, and the front desk's own view shows it fine, so the two roles disagree about whether a referral exists. **Deliberately NOT fixed in the write validator:** requiring `patient_id` would refuse a shape the app produces on purpose, and there is a test asserting a referral with `patient_id: ''` still writes. **The real options are a product call** &mdash; disclose it in the panel (the cheap, honest half, matching what SAIRNvet's additive-merge notice does), scope unlinked referrals by `internal_provider_id` instead, or make the link required and migrate. **Not a session's decision.** | M |
| **SAIRNdental** | ~~Two coverage rules for the same payer and procedure: which percentage applies is decided by ROW ORDER~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (CC)** on Michael's call to fix it rather than leave it separate | CC | — | `lookupCoverage()` uses `rules.find(...)`, so two matching rules meant the applied percentage was whichever sorted first — after `dntMergeById()`, insertion order. The write now READS the existing rules and refuses a genuine conflict with **409 COVERAGE_RULE_EXISTS**, naming the percentage already on file so the refusal is checkable against the rules table. Same shape as the `dnt_providers` `linked_employee_id` refusal in the same handler. **THE COMPARISON MIRRORS `lookupCoverage()` EXACTLY, and that is the point rather than a detail:** payer trimmed and lower-cased, `procedure_type_id` compared STRICTLY with no coercion. Looser than the reader and it refuses rules the app treats as distinct; tighter and it certifies "no conflict" while the reader still collides. A numeric `procedure_type_id` deliberately does not clash with the string form, because `lookupCoverage()` would not match them either. **FAIL-CLOSED, a deliberate divergence from the `dnt_providers` precedent it otherwise copies.** That one wraps its read in `if (dupR.ok)` and lets the write through when the check cannot run; this returns **503 COVERAGE_CHECK_UNAVAILABLE**, following the newer GFE precedent in the same file, because a uniqueness check that silently did not run is indistinguishable from one that passed. A 404/400 is NOT treated as a failure — the table simply does not exist, so there is nothing to duplicate and the write answers the standard `NOT_PROVISIONED`. **THE CLIENT HAD TO MOVE WITH IT, and that is the half that would have been missed:** `addCoverageRule()` wrote to localStorage BEFORE calling the server and toasted *"Saved on this device only — server sync not yet enabled for this app"* on any failure. With a 409 now possible that would leave the device applying a rule the server rejected, and every estimate computed there would differ from one computed anywhere else — while the message blamed a sync feature that is not disabled. Reordered server-first, same as `addProvider()`, and it now shows the server's own words via `dntLastErrText()`. **Cost, stated:** adding a coverage rule now requires the server; a network failure means the rule is not added rather than added locally. That is the trade `addProvider()` already made for the same reason. **16 new assertions**, including four payer spellings that must still clash, four shapes that must not, a CONTROL proving the identical payload is accepted when no rule exists, and proof that a 409 issues the read and no write. | S |

The 409 added earlier the same day made the attempt visible; this makes it possible. **The edit re-uses the existing rule's id**, so the write upserts on `(license_hash, coverage_rule_id)` and updates the row in place — the duplicate check already excludes the row being edited by id, so an edit cannot clash with itself. `created_at` is preserved rather than stamped with today. | **Tooling** | ~~`git_push_master_guard` denies on the SUBSTRING `master`, including inside a filename~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (Cody)** in `2c1ed4c0` — anchored on the destination refspec, live-verified in BOTH directions through the real hook | Cody | — | **CLOSED, and the fix was not where the row said it was.** The guard now splits the command on shell separators, finds the `git [global-opts] push` invocation in each piece, and compares only the DESTINATION half of its refspecs. Measured against the 27-case probe: the old rule wrongly DENIED 6 of 14 legitimate commands and wrongly ALLOWED 1 of 13 hazards — `git -C <path> push origin master` slipped the old regex entirely, so this is a net tightening rather than only a loosening. Three shapes are in the deny list specifically because a plausible version of this change would have let them through: separators are split on the raw TEXT rather than shlex's tokens (shlex splits on whitespace only, so `...origin master;echo hi` would arrive as one token comparing unequal); `--force-with-lease` is deliberately NOT in the consumes-a-value list, because listing it would make the parser eat the remote and never read the ref; and an unparseable piece falls back to the OLD blunt substring test rather than to allow, so a parse failure cannot become a hole. Three boundaries are stated in the module header rather than left to look like misses: a bare `git push` with no refspec is not inspected, the SOURCE half is not checked, and `archive/master` stays allowed. **THE HALF THAT MATTERED MORE, found only by trying to commit the fix: editing the repo copy did not change what runs.** The commit was denied by the OLD rule with the new code already on disk. `C:/Users/marsh/.claude/settings.json` — the GLOBAL file, not any clone's — still points four hooks at `C:/SAIRN/tools/`, and its copy of this guard was byte-identical to the pre-fix version. Both hooks run and a deny from either blocks. The external copy has been synced so live behaviour matches the reviewed code; **the duplicate global entries are left for Michael** (see the row below on the same directory) because that file governs every project on this machine. **Live-verified both ways after the push:** a command line containing `git push` followed by the word was ALLOWED and the push completed, and `git push --dry-run origin master` was DENIED. Held by `tests/run_push_master_guard_probe.py`. Original finding below. `tools/git_push_master_guard.py:28` was `re.search(r'git push.*master', cmd)`. **The `.*` spans the whole command string**, so any occurrence of the word anywhere after a push invocation trips it — including in an unrelated argument, a commit message, or a path. **Reproduced THREE times by accident, not theorised:** a diagnostic line that pushed to `HEAD:main` and separately named the file `git_push_master_guard.py` was DENIED with *"this command pushes to the stale 'master' branch"*, the only occurrence of the word being the guard's own filename; the command that first tried to ADD THIS ROW was denied for the same reason, because the row text quotes both halves of the pattern; and then the COMMIT of that row was denied too, because the commit message did. The row listed a commit message as a hypothetical before the third one made it an observation. **This is a false positive, not a hole, and the direction matters** — the guard cannot be talked into ALLOWING a push to the stale branch, only into refusing something harmless. That is why it was left alone rather than fixed inside an unrelated change. **Fix shape, not chosen:** anchor on the refspec rather than the line — match the word only where a ref can appear (`<remote> [+]<src>:<dst>`, or a bare ref), which is the same parsing `sairn_push_gate_hook.pushed_tip()` already does and could be reused. **Do NOT loosen it to a bare word match** — a commit message or a path would then decide, which is the same class of defect as the push gate's override matcher firing on a quoted mention (found and fixed 2026-09-03). Cost so far: three denied commands, each retried by routing the text through a file so no command line carries the pattern. Cost if ignored: a session hits it on a real push and reaches for an override it does not need, which is how a gate gets hollowed out | S |

So the key really does map to `anon`, and the denials are authorization, not a dead key. Without that control, nine `42501`s would have been indistinguishable from a revoked credential proving nothing.<br><br>**AND IT ANSWERS THE SHARPER QUESTION BY INFERENCE.** The real reason to worry was the row above: `supabase_admin`'s default ACL grants `anon`/`authenticated` **full CRUD** on tables it creates, and `license_keys` is the one table on the platform with **no tracked `CREATE TABLE`** — nobody knows which role created it. Full CRUD includes SELECT. **`anon` does not have SELECT here, so `license_keys` did not arrive with that default ACL.** That is the concern, closed by measurement.<br><br>**WHAT IS STILL UNMEASURED, stated so this row is not read as more than it is:** (1) `authenticated` — this clone holds no signed-in JWT, so that role was not probed at all; (2) the **non-SELECT** verbs for `anon` — `TRUNCATE`/`REFERENCES`/`TRIGGER` are known present on 158 tables from the row above and PostgREST exposes no verb that can reach them, so they are inert over HTTP but still ungranted work; (3) whether `license_keys` is one of those 158. **All three are answered by Section 2a of the SQL file when somebody runs it as `postgres`.** None of them is the disclosure risk I flagged.<br><br>**⚠ TWO OF THOSE THREE ARE NOW ANSWERED — this row went stale the same day it was written. Corrected 2026-09-09 (Fourth); see `docs/2026-09-09-license-keys-and-service-role-grant-state.md` §2.** Michael ran `sql/license_keys_grant_review_2026-09-05.sql` end to end as `postgres` (`e5a98af6`): **`anon` AND `authenticated` held nothing on this table**, which answers (1) for `license_keys` and answers (3) outright — it is not one of the 158. Only (2) survives, and only platform-wide: the non-SELECT verbs for `anon`/`authenticated` on 158 tables, which PostgREST exposes no verb to reach, so they are ungranted work rather than exposure. **Two independent methods agreed** — the outside publishable-key probe with no credential, and the inside `postgres` read — which is what retires the concern rather than leaving it "probably fine". As written the row still reads as three open exposures when it is one piece of tidy-up | S |

Nothing here can be edited or deleted."* **Mis-attribution is the failure that matters in a record like that.** **IT VERIFIES RATHER THAN REJECTS, AND THAT IS A CALL.** `law_matters` syncs, but a matter created on a device that has not hydrated is genuinely absent — so "not found" cannot distinguish a fabricated id from a real matter the server has not seen. Refusing would break the chat for the second case; coercing to `general` would destroy its attribution. **FOUR STATES, KEPT APART, because flattening them turns a disclosure into an accusation:** `true` confirmed **for this licence** (an unscoped lookup would stamp another firm's id as verified — worse than not checking); `false` this licence has no such matter, **not proof of fabrication**, and the panel offers both readings rather than alleging fraud; `null` the lookup itself failed, and "could not check" must not be stored as "checked and false"; `undefined` the record predates the check, passed through rather than coerced so months of existing entries do not all light up as failures. The panel renders three and warns on exactly one; `general` is never looked up and never marked. | **SAIRNlaw** | **"Which holidays actually count" — ONE bundled lawyer's question across four jurisdictions, not four separate ones** | Open — legal judgment | **Michael** | Needs a lawyer's read | **Bundled on Michael's direction 2026-08-25: do this as one research/decision pass, not one state at a time as each is seeded.** Every jurisdiction hits some version of it and the answers are likely to share reasoning. **(1) ILLINOIS** — statute (205 ILCS 630/17) vs court-observed (M.R. 5272) disagree **both ways**; M.R. 5272 omits Pulaski Day and Good Friday and adds the day after Thanksgiving. Confirm the statutory list is the right basis for 5 ILCS 70/1.11. Encoded that way; changes real dates. **(2) KENTUCKY — the blocker that stops the state being seeded at all.** RAP 6(A) and KRS 446.030 both say "legal holiday" and neither defines it; KRS 2.110 is titled "Public holidays", never uses the phrase, lists four days KY courts do not close for (Robert E. Lee Day, F.D. Roosevelt Day, Lincoln's Birthday, Confederate Memorial Day) and omits Thanksgiving. Encoding it fails **LATE**. **(3) TEXAS** — Tex. R. Civ. P. 4 and Tex. R. App. P. 4.1 both say "legal holiday" without defining it or citing Tex. Gov't Code 662.021, which is what the `tx` calendars encode; the link is the settled reading, not an express cross-reference. **(4) WEST VIRGINIA** — R. Civ. P. 6(a)(6) and R. App. P. 39(a) define it **differently within the same state** (Juneteenth), and one calendar per jurisdiction cannot serve both. **(5) ARIZONA** (added 2026-08-25) — Ariz. R. Civ. P. 6(a) says "legal holiday" and, on the text read, names no statute; the candidate A.R.S. 1-301 was not read. Same shape as Texas. **TWO JURISDICTIONS HAVE NOW ANSWERED IT THEMSELVES AND THEY ANSWER IT DIFFERENTLY, which is the strongest argument for asking this once rather than per state: WASHINGTON's CR 6(a) says outright "Legal holidays are prescribed in RCW 1.16.050" — an express cross-reference — and that statute then excludes its own decoys in subsection (7) ("may not be considered legal holidays for any purpose"); NORTH CAROLINA's Rule 6(a) instead asks whether "the courthouse is closed for transactions", so its statute is the wrong source entirely.** So the answer is not uniform and the question is really: which source does THIS rule's own words point at, and what do you do when they point at nothing? The common question: **when a court rule says "legal holiday" and points at nothing, does the general holiday statute supply the list, even where that statute is broader or narrower than what the courts actually observe?** Add the two Texas secondary-source rows to the same set while a lawyer is engaged **CATEGORY ADDED 2026-08-26, on Michael’s direction, covering MARYLAND and WISCONSIN together rather than as two new categories — they are the SAME SHAPE: a plausible-looking source that fails in the DANGEROUS direction.** Every jurisdiction above asks *which* list supplies “legal holiday”. These two ask something sharper: **the obvious source is not merely uncertain, it is affirmatively WRONG, and using it rolls deadlines LATE.** **(6) MARYLAND** — the intuitive path (Gen. Provisions § 1-302 + § 1-111) is wrong in BOTH directions at once: § 1-302 rolls only off Sunday, with no Saturday roll (→ EARLY), while § 1-111 adds Good Friday, Lincoln’s Birthday, Maryland Day and Defenders’ Day, four days that are NOT court holidays (→ LATE). Rule 1-203’s own committee note settles it — it **supersedes § 1-302**. Correct chain: Rule 1-203 → Rule 1-202(l) → **State Personnel & Pensions § 9-201**. **(7) WISCONSIN** — the wrongness is not which statute but **which TEST**. § 801.15(1)(b) rolls the last day unless it is “a day the clerk of courts office is closed” (a CLOSURE test), while the composed holiday definition in (1)(a) governs ONLY the sub-11-day intermediate-day exclusion. So the obvious move — drive the rollover off the holiday list — is the wrong basis, and because Wisconsin’s clerks of circuit court are COUNTY officers, any clerk open on a listed day means the rollover fires when the statute would not: **LATE**. The 1985 Law Revision Committee Note makes it worse rather than better, saying the amendment “will **permit** clerks to close their offices at these times” — permit, not require. **WHY THESE TWO BELONG WITH THE OTHERS AND NOT IN A ROW OF THEIR OWN:** the answer a lawyer gives to “when a rule says legal holiday and points at nothing, does the general statute supply the list?” is the same reasoning that answers “and what if the general statute is affirmatively the wrong one?” Splitting them would invite two inconsistent answers. **Both states are GATED-PASSED but deliberately UNSEEDED pending this** — see their own rows. Contrast the two that resolved safely: **MISSOURI**, where RSMo 9.010 is titled “Public holidays” (the identical lexical gap that helped kill Kentucky) but converges day-for-day with the state’s own schedule, so both readings agree; and **MASSACHUSETTS**, whose Rule 6(a) names its statute expressly. The distinction the lawyer needs to draw is CONVERGES vs DIVERGES, not NAMED vs UNNAMED. | L |

| **SAIRNlaw** | **Massachusetts SEEDED and LIVE — 11 rules; the gate's structural finding was resolved by disclosure, not deferred** | **Done 2026-08-25** (`b7d8150`) | — | — | Gate write-up kept at `docs/sairnlaw-massachusetts-deadline-seed-gate.md`; it PASSED and the seed followed in the same session. Rules 6, 12, 33, 34, 36 and 77 read verbatim through a real browser (every `mass.gov` URL 403s to curl and WebFetch, including `/doc/.../download` PDFs, while Playwright gets 200 — **a 403 there means "not a browser", never "page absent"**; same shape as NC's `nccourts.gov`). `effective_from` is real per rule. **THE COUNTY-SCOPED HOLIDAY LIST WAS RESOLVED THE VIRGINIA WAY:** the statewide Cl. 18 list is encoded and Suffolk County's Evacuation Day (17 Mar) and Bunker Hill Day (17 Jun) are DISCLOSED via `JURISDICTION_COVERAGE.ma`, because including them rolls thirteen counties LATE while omitting them can only run EARLY in Suffolk — and since the same clause requires Suffolk offices to stay "open for business and appropriately staffed" on both days, the omission may simply be CORRECT. Safe on both readings; the disclosure names Boston explicitly so it is not read as an edge case. **THE GENERATOR'S OWN ASSERTION CAUGHT A CARRIED-OVER ASSUMPTION, which is the reusable lesson:** the weekend-shift check copied from the VA/WV generators fired on 2026-07-04 — and the ASSERTION was wrong, not the data. Cl. 18 shifts only "when any of said days occurs on Sunday" and is silent on Saturday, so 4 July 2026 really is a Saturday legal holiday here. Harmless (R. 6(a) rolls off a Saturday anyway) but a both-ways shift carried across would have invented a Friday holiday and rolled deadlines LATE. The check now asserts no SUNDAY can appear, which is what the statute actually guarantees. **TWO FIRSTS FOR THE ENGINE:** the first GEOGRAPHIC coverage gap (WV's two lists split by body of rules; this one by place, and there is no field for place at all), and the first row that ABSORBS ITS OWN SERVICE EXTENSION — R. 33(a)(4)'s 40 days "shall be deemed to include the three day period allowed pursuant to Rule 6(d)", so it carries no `service_extension` deliberately; **do not "fix" it**. Also banked: R. 6(d) extends for ELECTRONIC service (opposite of FRCP 6(d) since 2016, wider than NC's mail-only 6(e)); R. 33 grants NO defendant floor where R. 34 and R. 36 both give 45 days, so there are two `later_of` rows not three; R. 12(a)(2)'s two limbs differ on whether the extension applies (notice of the court's action vs service of a paper) and are separate rows for that reason. **Remaining open, none blocking:** no appellate rows (Mass. R. App. P. is a separate body with its own time rule and needs its own read); no Rule 45 non-party rows; no backward rows, because R. 6(a) defines only a period that begins to run AFTER an act; and R. 33(a)(3)'s "whichever is later" second limb is unmodelled — it turns on whether an application is filed, which the engine cannot see, and because the rule takes the LATER the seeded 30-day date is a FLOOR, so the omission runs EARLY | S |

Missouri (~6.2M), largest unseeded after Tennessee failed. **Rules** free and official at `courts.mo.gov` with real per-rule currency (44.01 revised eff. **2025-01-01**, 43.01 eff. 2018-07-01, 103.08 eff. 2020-07-01). **Statutes are the strongest of any state gated:** `revisor.mo.gov` is the official Revisor, free, **HTTP 200 to plain curl**, no challenge, no terms gate, no publisher redirect, and it prints **per-section version history with effective dates** — the exact opposite of Tennessee, which failed on a publisher terms gate. **ACCESS QUIRK TO RECORD:** `courts.mo.gov/page.jsp?id=…` sits behind a **Cloudflare bot challenge** that neither curl nor headless Playwright clears, but the underlying **`ClerkHandbooksP2RulesOnly.nsf/…` document URLs are NOT challenged** and return full text — a challenge on `page.jsp` does NOT mean the rule is unavailable. Third distinct access mechanism after mass.gov and tncourts.gov. **THE HOLIDAY GAP IS KENTUCKY-SHAPED IN WORDING BUT NOT IN RISK:** Rule 44.01(a) says "legal holiday" and names no statute, and RSMo 9.010 is titled "**Public** holidays" — the identical lexical gap that helped kill Kentucky. It comes out the opposite way: Kentucky's KRS 2.110 lists four days its courts do NOT close for and omits Thanksgiving (fails **LATE**), whereas **RSMo 9.010's thirteen days match the State of Missouri's own 2026 schedule day for day** (verified against `oa.mo.gov`), including Lincoln's Birthday 12 Feb and Truman Day 8 May, and Thanksgiving is present. Both candidate readings converge, so encoding 9.010 cannot roll LATE on any listed day. **Add to the bundled lawyer's question as a NEW category** — rule says "legal holiday", points at nothing, obvious statute is titled something else but converges in practice — distinct from TX/AZ (convergence unknown) and KY (diverges dangerously). **ONE REAL DIVERGENCE, FAILS EARLY:** 9.010's shift is **SUNDAY-ONLY** (silent on Saturday, like Massachusetts, unlike VA/WV) yet the administration observes **Fri 3 July 2026** for a Saturday 4 July. Encode the statute and DISCLOSE the observed substitutes — encoding them instead would roll LATE. **DO NOT add a Saturday shift by analogy to VA/WV; that is the second state running where it would be wrong.** | L |

MN, VA and WV all need a wider computed span than they emit. **THE ONE INFERENCE IS DISCLOSED AS AN INFERENCE:** Indigenous Peoples Day is encoded on R. 6.01(d)’s “any day that the U.S. mail does not operate” limb rather than on the branch option, and the coverage summary says in terms that this is a reading and not a quoted holding and that it is **the only place in this jurisdiction where the engine could be LATE**.
