| **SAIRNlaw** | **STANDING GATE CHECK: the weekend-coverage question — run it on every future jurisdiction** | Open (reference, run every gate) | unassigned | — | **Promoted to a standing check on Michael’s direction 2026-08-26**, after four consecutive states hit it and gave four different answers. **THE OLD QUESTION WAS “does the computation rule name Saturday and Sunday?” THAT IS THE WRONG QUESTION.** Several jurisdictions put the weekend INSIDE the holiday list rather than beside it, so a rule that names only “legal holiday” may still roll weekends — or may not. **THE CHECK, IN ORDER:** (1) does the rollover clause name Saturday and Sunday expressly? If yes, done — `isWeekend()` is correct. (2) If not, what does it key on — a holiday STATUTE, or COURTHOUSE CLOSURE? (3) If a statute: **does that statute make BOTH weekend days holidays, EVERYWHERE in the state?** Statewide matters as much as both-days. (4) If closure: the holiday list is the WRONG basis for the rollover and encoding it risks rolling **LATE** — get the judiciary’s closure schedule instead. **THE FOUR ANSWERS SO FAR:** **LOUISIANA** — rule says “legal holiday” only; R.S. 1:55 makes **Sundays** statewide but **Saturday a holiday in enumerated parishes only** → `isWeekend()` rolls Saturdays where the law does not → **LATE → BLOCKED**, and it is why the weekend-flag engine row exists. **OKLAHOMA** — same rule shape, but 25 O.S. § 82.1(A) opens “**Each Saturday, Sunday**”, statewide, no county qualification → **safe**. **OREGON** — ORS 187.010(1)(a) makes “Each Sunday” a holiday (the Louisiana quirk) but ORCP 10 A names **“a Saturday or a legal holiday, including Sunday”**, so the RULE saves it → **safe**. **CONNECTICUT** — no holiday list exists in the Practice Book at all (five “holiday” mentions in 699 pages, none definitional); the rollover keys purely on **clerk’s-office closure** → weekends are right by accident of fact, but the statutory list is the wrong basis and would risk LATE → **the Wisconsin hazard**. **WHY IT IS WORTH ITS OWN ROW:** the failure is silent. Every one of these produces plausible dates; only Louisiana’s is wrong, and only because a proviso buried mid-sentence scopes Saturday to some parishes. Nothing in a green test run would surface it. **FIFTH ANSWER, 2026-08-27 — UTAH, and it is the first to stop at STEP 1.** URCP 6(a)(1)(C) names both days inside the rollover clause itself: “if the last day is a **Saturday, Sunday**, or legal holiday, the period continues to run until the end of the next day that is not a **Saturday, Sunday** or legal holiday.” No holiday statute has to be consulted for the weekend question at all and there is no statewide-coverage sub-question — `isWeekend()` is correct on the rule’s own words. **SIXTH ANSWER, 2026-08-27 — IOWA, AND IT BREAKS THE CHECK ITSELF.** Every previous state had ONE answer. Iowa has TWO, and which applies depends on WHAT KIND OF DEADLINE IT IS. The rules contain no counting mechanics at all — R. Civ. P. 1.1801 is one sentence pointing at **Iowa Code § 4.1(34)** (`first day` 0 hits, `legal holiday` 0 hits, `Sunday` 0 hits across 130 pages). § 4.1(34) sentence one rolls **ONLY SUNDAY**; sentence two rolls **Saturday + Sunday + clerk-closure + 11 named holidays** but is scoped to “**the commencement of an action … the FILING of a pleading or motion … or the perfecting or filing of an appeal**”. **Iowa’s answer and discovery deadlines are SERVICE deadlines** — R. 1.303(1) “shall **serve** … within 20 days”, and R. 1.509 says outright “**Answers shall not be filed**; however, they shall be **served**”. On the words, sentence two may not reach them → rolling a Saturday would be **LATE by two days**. **CONSEQUENCE FOR THE ENGINE CHANGE BELOW: a per-JURISDICTION weekend flag is NOT enough for Iowa — it needs PER-RULE.** Read the Iowa gate before building that flag. **SEVENTH ANSWER, 2026-08-27 — NEVADA, safe at step 1 like Utah.** NRCP 6(a)(1)(C): “if the last day is a **Saturday, Sunday**, or legal holiday, the period continues to run until the end of the next day that is not a **Saturday, Sunday**, or legal holiday.” **EIGHTH ANSWER, 2026-08-27 — ARKANSAS, safe at step 1.** ARCP 6(a) names both days: “unless it is a **Saturday, Sunday**, legal holiday, or other day when the clerk’s office is closed”. Its clerk’s-office limb appears TWICE — as a rollover TRIGGER and as the rollover TARGET (“the next day that the clerk’s office is open”) — but both are ADDITIONAL to the named days, so both omissions run EARLY. Minnesota’s shape, not Wisconsin’s or Connecticut’s. **Running tally: LA blocked, OK safe (statute), OR safe (rule), CT premise-fails, UT safe at step 1, IA SPLIT WITHIN THE STATE → BLOCKED, NV safe at step 1, AR safe at step 1** | S |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **Arkansas GATED — rules PASS and current to 4 June 2026, but the HOLIDAY STATUTE has no reachable primary source; one statute is the only blocker** | **Blocked 2026-08-27** ⚠ | unassigned | Ark. Code Ann. § 1-5-101 on an official source | Full write-up: `docs/sairnlaw-arkansas-deadline-seed-gate.md`. Arkansas (~3.1M). **RULES PASS AND STATE THEIR OWN CURRENCY** — the ARCP PDF opens “Rules of Civil Procedure (**current through June 4, 2026**)” and the collection index prints the same date per rule set, which only Nevada has matched. **THE PATH IS NOT OBVIOUS, RECORDED SO IT IS NOT REDISCOVERED:** Arkansas serves rules from a **Lexum/Decisia portal**; `arcourts.gov/rules`, `/court-rules` and `/rules-and-administrative-orders` all **404**, `arkansasjudiciary.gov` and `courts.arkansas.gov` **do not resolve at all**, and `opinions.arcourts.gov/ark/en/nav.do` returns 200 with the rule links **JS-loaded and absent from the HTML**. The working route is **`?iframe=true`**: `/ark/en/nav.do?iframe=true` → `/ark/cr/en/nav_date.do?iframe=true` → **`/ark/cr/en/16712/1/document.do`** (1.3 MB PDF). Browser UA required. **⚠ THE BLOCKER IS ONE STATUTE.** Rule 6(a) defines “legal holiday” by pointing outward, so no calendar without **Ark. Code Ann. § 1-5-101** — and **`arkleg.state.ar.us` answers the statute’s FTPDocument path with HTTP 200 and a 42-byte 1×1 GIF** (`content-type: image/gif`). **FOURTH DISTINCT “200 THAT IS NOT CONTENT” SHAPE IN THREE DAYS** — after Utah’s error-page body, Iowa’s inert dated URL and njcourts’ anti-bot interstitial — **and the most easily missed: a fetch checking only status and non-zero length passes it.** Justia has the section but is secondary and self-disclaims. **EVERYTHING BELOW IS FROM JUSTIA AND IS NOT PRIMARY-SOURCED.** **THE HOLIDAY BASIS IS A UNION OF TWO LISTS** — first jurisdiction needing both. 6(a): holidays designated **by the President or Congress OR by the laws of this State**, and the Reporter’s Notes say the recitation was deliberately omitted as redundant. **THREE TRAPS:** (1) **CHRISTMAS EVE, 24 DECEMBER, is a state holiday** — no seeded state has it, and it is ADJACENT to Christmas, so when the 25th is a Sunday you get the 23rd AND the 26th around a weekend; (2) **the state list has NO Juneteenth and NO Columbus Day but the UNION does** — a calendar from the state statute alone omits two days, one from the federal list alone omits Christmas Eve, **neither list is correct on its own**; (3) **“an employee’s birthday” is a listed holiday and is NOT A CALENDAR DATE AT ALL** — unmodellable, omitting it runs EARLY. Observation shifts agree between the two lists (Sat→preceding Fri, Sun→succeeding Mon), and Arkansas’s is GENERAL rather than enumerated, unlike NV’s and UT’s. **SHORT-PERIOD EXCLUSION IS FOURTEEN DAYS — A NEW NUMBER AND THE LONGEST IN THE PLATFORM** (six states use 7, three use 11, MN/UT/NV have none). Arkansas **did NOT follow the federal 2009 abolition** — it went 11 → 14 while FRCP went to zero, so do not reason from FRCP 6(a) despite the Reporter’s Notes calling Rule 6 “practically identical to FRCP 6”. **RULE 6(d) IS THREE *BUSINESS* DAYS** (engine already supports the unit — `ny_cplr_2103b` uses it) **AND E-SERVICE IS EXPRESSLY INCLUDED**, the opposite of NV/WV/NY/federal — a copied allowlist would UNDER-count. **✅ AND THE ANSWER CARVE-OUT IS EXPRESS, WHICH CONFIRMS THE FEDERAL FIX MADE THE SAME DAY:** 6(d) ends “**this subdivision shall not extend the time in which the defendant must file an answer or pre-answer motion when service of the summons and complaint is by mail or commercial delivery company in accordance with Rule 4**” — Arkansas says outright what FRCP 6(d) leaves to inference and what two federal rows had got wrong. Note it is NARROWER: it excludes the answer only for mail/commercial-delivery service. **SEEDABLE ONCE CLEAR:** 12(a)(1) has **four** periods — 30 days; 30 from first publication of a warning order; **60 days for a defendant INCARCERATED in this state** (a party-status period no seeded state has); 30 for cross/counterclaim — plus 6(c)’s 20-day **backward** motion row and 10/5-day response/reply rows, **both of which fall under the 14-day exclusion**. **ALL THREE DISCOVERY RULES CARRY THE SAME FLOOR WITH THE SAME NUMBERS** — later of 30 from the request and 45 from the summons — which is the Georgia `resolve_periods` shape, and Arkansas would be its **third real user**; unusual uniformity, where MO is 45/45/60, NC 45/45/60, MN 45/45/none, MA none/45/45. **UPDATE 2026-08-27 — TWO OFFICIAL SOURCES FOUND ON MICHAEL’S LEADS; THE BLOCK IS NARROWED, NOT CLEARED, AND ARKANSAS STAYS GATED.** **THE GIF IS A PATH PROBLEM, NOT A DOMAIN PROBLEM — CONFIRMED:** `/Home/FTPDocument` serves real PDFs for Acts from **2017R onward** (150–235 KB) and the **1×1 GIF for 2015R and earlier**, a clean cut-off at 2017; the **ACA is not served under ANY path shape tried**, `/Home/ArkansasCode`, `/ACA` and the code search all **HTTP 500**, and the site’s own Arkansas Law portal links to Acts, Bills and the admin-rules site but **NOT to the Code** — consistent with Arkansas licensing the ACA commercially. **The Arkansas Code does not appear to be published free by the State.** **✅ PRIMARY 1 — ACT 561 OF 2017** (arkleg, 191 KB PDF): § 4 amends § 1-5-101(a)(2) to strike Robert E. Lee’s Birthday, leaving “Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr.’s Birthday — the third Monday in January”; § 5 moves Robert E. Lee Day to § 1-5-106 gubernatorial-proclamation memorial days (second Saturday in October), which is **NOT a holiday and must not enter a calendar**. **✅ PRIMARY 2 — THE TREASURER OF STATE’S SHEET** (`media.ark.org/artreasury/holidays_2025.pdf`, on the State’s own CDN): “2025 State Holidays — Legal holidays by authority of **Act 304 of 2001 and Amended by Act 561 of 2017**”, enumerating ten dated days that match Justia exactly and **independently confirming CHRISTMAS EVE**. It also **omits “an employee’s birthday”**, supporting reading that limb as personal leave rather than a calendar holiday. **⚠ WHY IT STILL BLOCKS: § 1-5-101(b), THE OBSERVATION SHIFT, IS STILL UNSOURCED FROM PRIMARY** — only Justia has Sat→preceding-Fri / Sun→succeeding-Mon, and **the Treasury sheet CANNOT corroborate it because NO fixed-date Arkansas holiday fell on a weekend in 2025** (1 Jan Wed, 4 Jul Fri, 11 Nov Tue, 24/25 Dec Wed/Thu) — it never had to apply the shift. **That is the load-bearing piece:** the shift is what makes the Christmas Eve / Christmas pair behave, and they straddle a weekend in **2027** (24th Fri, 25th Sat) and **2028** (24th Sun, 25th Mon), inside any range that would be generated. Also open: **whether any act after 2017 amended the section** — every biennium from 2017R is fetchable but arkleg’s act search is JS-driven and returns nothing in static HTML, so the history could not be swept, and Justia (the source being checked) is the only thing saying it stops at 2017. **THE REMAINING TASK IS NO LONGER “FIND THE STATUTE” BUT “SOURCE § 1-5-101(b)’S SHIFT AND CONFIRM NO POST-2017 AMENDMENT”.** `codeofarrules.arkansas.gov` was also tried — its search is JS-driven and returns an identical shell for every query. **UPDATE 2026-08-28 — ONE OF THE TWO QUESTIONS IS CLOSED. ✅ NOTHING AMENDED § 1-5-101 AFTER 2017:** Justia’s 2024 code page carries the full amendment chain back to 1943 and ends at Acts 2017 No. 561 § 4 — the act already read from primary. **What is relied on there is the HISTORY LINE, not the substantive text**, and the distinction is deliberate: a publisher’s citation list is the part of a secondary source least likely to be wrong, while the text is the part that gets subtly mis-rendered. A primary confirmation was attempted and is **not cheaply available** — sweeping 2019R–2025R for `1-5-101` is ~1,000 PDFs per session, and the index that would collapse it is not published (**every `CodeSectionsAmended` path returns the same 42-byte 1×1 GIF**; the HTML page is JS-driven). **⚠ STILL OPEN: § 1-5-101(b), THE OBSERVATION SHIFT.** Michael searched and found **four independent secondaries — Justia across multiple years, FindLaw, USLegal, Encyclopedia of Arkansas — all quoting IDENTICAL language**, plus one genuine primary hit on `codeofarrules.arkansas.gov` that **cites § 1-5-101 without containing it** (same category already ruled out; a citation is not the text). **Four secondaries agreeing is not a primary source and the block stands** — but the residual risk has changed shape: the realistic failure is no longer “the language is wrong” (four publishers do not independently invent one sentence) but “the language is stale”, which the closed question above makes unlikely too. **High confidence, no primary source, and the gate does not seed on confidence** — the same standard that keeps Iowa and Connecticut blocked. **WHAT WOULD CLEAR IT, by likely cost:** (1) the print or LexisNexis ACA (Arkansas licenses its Code, so probably the only route to codified text, and not free); (2) **the act that enacted (b)** — Act 561 amended only (a)(2), so (b) predates 2017, and **arkleg serves nothing before 2017R**, putting Act 304 of 2001 out of reach on that host; a different official archive of pre-2017 session laws would settle it; (3) a court or agency document that **QUOTES (b)** rather than citing the section — the Treasurer’s sheet was the near miss, applying the rule without stating it in a year that never had to | L |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **Connecticut: SOURCES PASS but NOT seedable in the shape this engine models — the pleading rule is a CHAIN and the rollover has no holiday basis** | **Blocked 2026-08-26** ⚠️ | unassigned | Two structural questions, not source ones | Full write-up: `docs/sairnlaw-connecticut-deadline-seed-gate.md`. Connecticut (~3.6M); rules of court are the **Practice Book**, adopted by the judges of the Superior Court. **SOURCES ARE AMONG THE CLEANEST GATED:** the Judicial Branch publishes the **OFFICIAL 2026 Practice Book** as one free PDF on plain curl — 3.6 MB, 699 pages, a **real text PDF not a scan**, first page reading “OFFICIAL / 2026 / CONNECTICUT PRACTICE BOOK” — with per-section amendment histories giving real `effective_from`; `cga.ct.gov` serves the General Statutes free too. Nothing here is the blocker. **BLOCKER ONE: SEC. 10-8 IS A CHAIN, NOT A DEADLINE.** Pleadings “shall advance within thirty days from the **return day**, and any subsequent pleadings … shall advance at least one step within each successive period of thirty days **from the preceding pleading** or the filing of the decision of the judicial authority thereon”. So the *n*th deadline depends on the *(n−1)*th ACTUAL FILING DATE, indefinitely. **Every seeded jurisdiction computes ONE date from ONE caller-supplied trigger.** Maryland’s chained discovery floor was logged as the highest-stakes shape found in any gate because ONE LIMB depended on another rule’s output; **Connecticut’s entire pleading cadence is that shape as the PRIMARY mechanism**. Seedable only as “30 days from the preceding pleading”, with a trigger named so nobody mistakes it for “30 days from the return day”, and a readme saying plainly that **Connecticut has no single answer deadline to compute**. Two domain-scoped variants ride the same sentence: **summary process 3 days, mortgage foreclosure 15 days**. And the **RETURN DAY is a Connecticut-specific concept fixed by Gen. Stat. § 52-48 (NOT read)** — it is a date stated in the writ, **not derivable from a service date**, so it must be caller-supplied and a seed must not silently equate the two. **BLOCKER TWO: THE ROLLOVER HAS NO HOLIDAY BASIS AT ALL.** Running the new standing weekend-coverage check, Connecticut fails the question’s premise: **“holiday” appears FIVE times in 699 pages and none of the five is definitional** (two are professional-conduct commentary on gifts, one is juvenile detention). Sec. 63-2 and Sec. 7-17 both roll on **“a day when the office of the clerk … is closed”**. Weekends therefore come out right by accident of fact (offices are shut), but **encoding Conn. Gen. Stat. § 1-4’s list for the rollover would risk rolling LATE if a clerk’s office is in fact open on a listed day — the Wisconsin hazard**. Sec. 7-17 says offices close “during weeks which include a legal holiday” but **never says which days**. Correct basis is the **Judicial Branch’s published court-closure schedule**, making Connecticut ingest-not-derive alongside NC, NJ, MD and OK; plus a discretionary limb (closure authorized by the administrative judge “due to the existence of special circumstances”) that is unknowable → EARLY. **NO INTERMEDIATE-DAY EXCLUSION, STATED AFFIRMATIVELY:** Sec. 63-2 — “**Time shall be counted by calendar, not working, days.**” So the field is ABSENT, and unlike Minnesota (where it had to be inferred from a days-are-days rule) Connecticut says it outright. **NO SERVICE EXTENSION FOUND ANYWHERE IN THE PRACTICE BOOK** — zero hits for “days shall be added”, “shall be added to the prescribed” and “additional time after service”. All nineteen seeded jurisdictions have one. **Reported as an absence searched for, NOT as a conclusion**: the General Statutes were not searched, and that is the single most important remaining read, because assuming “no extension” wrongly computes EARLY on every mailed period. **AN E-FILING CUTOFF THAT MOVES THE FILING, NOT THE DEADLINE:** Sec. 7-17 deems a document e-filed **after 5 p.m.** (or at any time on a closed day) filed on the next business day — mechanically Missouri’s completion rule but applied to **filing** rather than service, so it does not change the DATE this engine reports, only that a filer has until 5 p.m. rather than midnight. Row note, not a mechanism. **ALSO NOT READ:** Gen. Stat. § 1-4; the discovery periods (Secs. 13-6, 13-9, 13-22/13-23, located but not read); and the Judicial Branch closure schedule itself. **UPDATE 2026-08-27 — PARTIALLY SEEDED, DELIBERATELY UNPROVISIONED (Hank).** The chain row is now live in `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_connecticut.json` — ONE row, `ct-pb-10-8-advance-from-preceding-pleading`, trigger `filing_of_preceding_pleading` (named at length so it cannot be read as `return_day`), 30 calendar days forward, new standard `ct_pb_63_2` (`impl: frcp_6a`, **no** `short_period_exclusion_days`), `effective_from` **2014-01-01** from Sec. 10-8’s own amendment line. **NO `ct` CALENDAR WAS CREATED AND THAT IS THE HOLD:** `rollOff()` consults the calendar BEFORE it looks at the weekday, so Connecticut refuses `NOT_PROVISIONED` on **every** date, not just weekends — the Wisconsin hazard cannot be walked into. `api/_lib/deadline-connecticut.test.js` (26 assertions) asserts that on a **Tuesday** landing as well as a weekend one and **FAILS IF ANYONE LOADS A `ct` CALENDAR**. No `JURISDICTION_COVERAGE` entry — that text rides on SUCCESSFUL computations, so one would be dormant (Guardian 0d); it goes in with the calendar. **RE-VERIFYING THE GATE AGAINST THE PDF FOUND THREE THINGS IT MISSED, one of which cuts the other way:** **(1) §3a, the only LATE-direction exposure —** the counting rule Sec. 63-2 sits under **RULES OF APPELLATE PROCEDURE**, and on de-hyphenated text `first day shall` occurs **exactly once in 699 pages**, there; `computation of time`, `computing any period`, `period of time prescribed` and `intermediate Saturdays` are **0 hits each**. There is NO trial-court counting rule. 63-2 arguably still reaches Superior Court (it governs “any documents … under these rules” and its own rollover names “the clerk of the **trial court**”) but that is not conclusive — and if it does not, the first-day convention is ASSUMED not sourced and computes **LATE**. Tolerable only because nothing computes; it now blocks the calendar alongside the closure schedule. **(2) §5a —** the no-service-extension finding survived a de-hyphenated re-run (0 hits, 5 patterns) AND got stronger: **Sec. 10-13 says “Service by mail is complete upon mailing”** — affirmative, not an absence, and mechanically Missouri’s completion shape. Its e-delivery limb carries an unknowable negative condition → EARLY. **(3) §4a —** Sec. 7-17 has a SECOND early limb §4 missed: e-filing **system-outage** tolling (nonoperational 30 consecutive min 9am–3pm, or ANY time 3–5pm). **NOT SEEDED and why:** the return-day first step (needs Gen. Stat. § 52-48; the return day is NOT derivable from a service date); the summary-process **3-day** and foreclosure **15-day** variants (real and quoted — held on a DOMAIN decision, `DOMAIN_LABELS` knows only `civil-litigation` and `appellate`, not on any doubt about the text); discovery, appellate and backward rows. **WHICH ITEM BLOCKS WHAT:** §3a and the closure schedule block the CALENDAR; § 52-48 blocks only the return-day row; a General-Statutes service extension blocks **nothing seeded** (this row runs from a FILING). 479/479 deadline tests pass. **⛔ HARD BLOCK, RESTATED EXPLICITLY ON MICHAEL’S DIRECTION 2026-08-27: DO NOT ADD A CONNECTICUT CALENDAR UNTIL THE FIRST-DAY-CONVENTION SOURCING QUESTION IS ANSWERED — DOING SO WOULD SILENTLY COMPUTE LATE.** The question in one sentence: **does Practice Book Sec. 63-2 reach Superior Court pleadings, or only appeals?** It is the sole source for “the last day shall, and the first day shall not, be counted” and it sits under RULES OF APPELLATE PROCEDURE. **This is a SECOND, INDEPENDENT blocker from the Wisconsin/closure-schedule hazard, and the more dangerous of the two** — every other disclosed Connecticut gap fails EARLY; this one fails LATE. **Answering the closure-schedule question alone does NOT unblock the calendar**, and shipping one on that basis is the specific mistake being guarded against: it would look like the hazard had been handled while the first-day convention was still assumed. Written into three places so it cannot be missed — the seed `_readme` opens with it, `ct_pb_63_2`’s comment block in `deadline-engine.js` carries it, and `deadline-connecticut.test.js` FAILS if a `ct` calendar appears at all | L |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **Oklahoma gate PASSED — has Louisiana’s rollover SHAPE but not its problem, and the calendar is NOT fully derivable** | **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28** | unassigned | — | Full write-up: `docs/sairnlaw-oklahoma-deadline-seed-gate.md`. Oklahoma (~4.1M), statutory civil procedure (Oklahoma Pleading Code, Title 12). **OSCN — the Oklahoma State Courts Network — publishes Titles 12 and 25 free on plain curl** with per-section Historical Data, and its title indexes list every section with its CiteID, so navigation is mechanical rather than guesswork. **IT HAS LOUISIANA’S SHAPE:** 12 O.S. § 2006(A)(1) rolls the last day only if it is “a legal holiday as defined by Section 82.1 of Title 25 … or any other day when the office of the court clerk does not remain open” — **Saturday and Sunday are NOT named**, exactly the structure that blocked Louisiana one gate earlier. **ONE CLAUSE SAVES IT:** 25 O.S. § 82.1(A) opens “The designation and dates of holidays in Oklahoma shall be as follows: **Each Saturday, Sunday**, …” — both weekend days, statewide, no county qualification. So the rollover reaches them and **the engine’s `isWeekend()` is correct here**, by a different textual route than any common-law state. **RECORD THIS AS A PATTERN, NOT A ONE-OFF:** three states running have had the weekend INSIDE the holiday list rather than beside it — Louisiana (Sunday only, parish-scoped Saturday → **blocked**), Oregon (Sunday in the list but Saturday also named separately in the rule → safe), Oklahoma (both, statewide → safe). **The gate question is no longer “does the rule name Saturday and Sunday” but “if it doesn’t, does the holiday statute cover both, everywhere?”** **EXCLUSION IS ELEVEN DAYS** (with TN/AZ/WI/AL), **and SIX SECTIONS ARE EXPRESSLY CARVED OUT of it** — §§ 765, 990.3, 1148.4, 1148.5, 1148.5A and 1756 count every intermediate day regardless of length. An *enumerated* opt-out, narrower than Louisiana’s open-ended “expressly excluded” and Minnesota’s open-ended opt-in, but still something a standard-level flag cannot express. None is in a first civil batch so it is recordable rather than blocking — **but seeding one later without noticing would apply an exclusion the statute removes, computing LATE**. The clerk-closure limb sits in BOTH the rollover and the exclusion and is a **partial-closure** test (“does not remain open … until the regularly scheduled closing time”), same breadth as Oregon’s; additional to the list rather than a replacement, so EARLY. **THE CALENDAR IS NOT FULLY DERIVABLE, FOR TWO INDEPENDENT REASONS.** (a) **The Christmas rule is the most complex encountered anywhere** — Dec 25, plus “the day before **or** after Christmas if Christmas is not on a Saturday or Sunday”, plus “the Thursday and Friday before Christmas if Christmas is on a Saturday”, plus “the Monday and Tuesday after Christmas, if Christmas is on a Sunday” — two or three holidays depending on the weekday, and **the general both-ways shift expressly does NOT apply to Christmas**. The before-or-after choice is simply not in the statute. (b) **§ 82.1(B) requires the Governor to issue an Executive Order EACH YEAR** specifying the dates — the **New Jersey annual-order shape layered on a statute** — and it is the only thing that resolves the Christmas choice. So Oklahoma is **ingest-per-year**, joining NJ, NC and MD, and is the first to be so for a **single day inside an otherwise derivable list**. The presidential limb (“the Governor **may** issue an Executive Order”) is discretionary and ad hoc on top: EARLY. **DO NOT ENCODE JUNETEENTH ON 19 JUNE.** Oklahoma’s is 25 O.S. **§ 82.4**, “**the third Saturday in June**”, a separate section — and § 2006 defines legal holiday by reference to **§ 82.1 only**. Practically it changes nothing because the third Saturday is already a holiday under “Each Saturday”, so it is **functionally invisible to deadline computation** — but encoding a 19 June holiday by analogy to the ten other seeded states would add a non-holiday and compute **LATE**. Also **no Columbus/Indigenous Peoples Day at all** (as in Oregon), and **the day after Thanksgiving IS statutory** (as in SC, unlike MN where it is a branch option). **SERVICE: +3 for mail, THIRD-PARTY COMMERCIAL CARRIER and electronic means** — ninth distinct answer on electronic service and the only one naming commercial carrier as its own category — period-lengthening, not Alabama’s federal after-expiry order. **BUT § 2006(D)’s proviso is a different TRIGGER, not a different amount:** where a summons and petition are served by mail, the answer runs from **the date of RECEIPT, or if refused the date of REFUSAL**, and takes **no +3**. Own row, or a caller supplying a service date computes EARLY. **AND THE ANSWER PERIOD CARRIES A PLAINTIFF’S ELECTION THE ENGINE CANNOT SEE:** § 2012(A) gives 20 days, but (A)(4) lets “the party requesting a summons to be issued … **elect** to have the answer served within **thirty-five (35) days** in lieu of the twenty” — a fact about what the requesting party chose, the same shape as Louisiana art. 1001’s 21-vs-30. **Two rows with unmissable names; guessing 35 where 20 applies computes LATE.** A defendant’s reservation of time adds a further 20 days while waiving defences — unseeable, but it only ever extends, so EARLY. **NOT DETERMINED:** the discovery periods (§§ 3233, 3234, 3236 — same source, effort remaining not a source problem); whether any of the six carved-out sections belongs in a civil batch; the current year’s Executive Order; and **25 O.S. § 82.2**, whose title suggests it bears on acts performable on holidays — § 2006 points only at § 82.1, but § 82.2 must be read to confirm it does not widen the definition **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28 (Hank) — 9 rules, `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_oklahoma.json`, new standards `ok_12_2006` and `ok_12_2006_d`, calendar 2026–2031 from `tools/gen_ok_calendar.py`. `deadline-oklahoma.test.js` 51/51.** All three gate conditions met. **(1) THE TWO FACTS THE ENGINE CANNOT DERIVE ARE FOUR SEPARATE ROWS, never one with a default** — the plaintiff’s 20-vs-35 election under § 2012(A)(4), and § 2006(D)’s proviso, which on MAILED process changes the **TRIGGER** to **receipt or refusal** rather than the amount. Trigger names carry both facts (`..._20_day_election`, `receipt_or_refusal_of_mailed_...`), a bare `service_of_summons_and_petition` matches **nothing**, and the test asserts all four exist and that none carries the extension. Guessing 35 where 20 applies is **fifteen days LATE**. **(2) THE SIX CARVED-OUT SECTIONS ARE RECORDED** (§§ 765, 990.3, 1148.4, 1148.5, 1148.5A, 1756 are excluded from the 11-day exclusion); none is in this batch, and the test asserts **no seeded row is under 11 days** so none reaches it. **(3) THE CALENDAR OMITS ONE DAY A YEAR AND DISCLOSES IT.** § 82.1(A) gives “the day before **OR** after Christmas” without saying which, and § 82.1(B) makes the Governor’s annual Executive Order the only resolver. **THOSE ORDERS ARE REACHABLE AND UNREADABLE** — sos.ok.gov publishes them, identifiable by title (**EO 2026-26 AMENDED**, 15 July 2026, for calendar 2027), but each is a **single-page scanned image with zero extractable text** (`/ProcSet [/PDF /ImageI]`, one `/Im1 Do`). Reading it needs OCR, which this project does not treat as verbatim. **Omitting reports EARLY; guessing would be wrong in BOTH directions at once** — adding a non-holiday (LATE) while still missing the real one. Affected years 2026, 2028–2031; **2027 is clean** (Saturday Christmas → the Thursday and Friday before, determinate). **THRESHOLD IS ELEVEN, not seven** — the test cross-asserts Oregon’s 7 against Oklahoma’s 11 so neither can drift into the other. **NO JUNETEENTH** (§ 2006 points at § 82.1 alone; Oklahoma’s is the **third Saturday in June** under § 82.4, already a non-day), **NO COLUMBUS DAY**, and **§ 82.2 was READ** to confirm its commemorative days stay outside — all three asserted by the generator. **The day after Thanksgiving IS a statutory holiday.** § 3236 turned out to need a **`resolve: later_of`** nobody had flagged: “The thirty-day response period shall not commence until an answer to the petition is filed”, so the clock runs 30 days from the LATER of request-service and answer-filing — seeding it flat would compute EARLY. **§§ 3233/3234 have NO defendant floor** (four phrasings searched, zero hits). Extension is +3 for mail, **third-party commercial carrier** (unique to Oklahoma) and **electronic means**; e-mail is NOT named and does not qualify. **Both tests proven to catch their defects:** encoding the omitted 24 Dec 2026 fails 2 of 51 including the arithmetic case, and dropping 11 to 7 fails the cross-assertion | L |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **Oregon gate PASSED — the Council-on-Court-Procedures wrinkle is REAL but is a CURRENCY problem, and ORCP 10 answers Maryland’s open ambiguity outright** | **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28** | unassigned | — | Full write-up: `docs/sairnlaw-oregon-deadline-seed-gate.md`. Oregon (~4.3M). **THE COUNCIL WRINKLE, CHECKED FIRST AND CONFIRMED REAL:** the ORCP are **not promulgated by the Supreme Court**. The Council on Court Procedures drafts and promulgates them, the **Legislative Assembly may then amend or repeal**, and they take effect as law the following 1 January — a two-stage lifecycle visible in the rules’ own citation lines, which carry **two amending authorities** (“CCP 12/6/14” alongside “2015 c.212 §8”). No other seeded jurisdiction has this. **AND THE COUNCIL’S OWN SITE DISCLAIMS ITS COPY:** “the first link is **static and may not include the most recent Council amendments nor amendments by the Legislature**”. Its static file is labelled **“Effective January 1, 2020”**, its “Amendments Made By Oregon Legislature” section is **empty and pending**, and its own news page records **2024 promulgations** (Rules 1, 14, 39, 55 and a new Rule 35) the ORCP page does not reflect. **So do NOT use the Council copy as the rule text** — take it from `oregonlegislature.gov/bills_laws/SiteAssets/ORCP.html`, the whole ORCP in one ~1.1 MB HTML file, **curl 200**, which is what was read. Standing consequence: the Legislature’s file is the TEXT and the Council’s promulgation record is the **forward channel** showing what becomes law next 1 January; both must be checked, in that order — the two-channel shape Maryland has, reached for a completely different structural reason. **IT DOES NOT HAVE LOUISIANA’S PROBLEM, AND THE DRAFTING IS WHY.** ORCP 10 A rolls off **“a Saturday or a legal holiday, including Sunday”** — Saturday named SEPARATELY, Sunday folded INTO the holiday definition. So although ORS 187.010(1)(a) makes “Each Sunday” a legal holiday (the same statutory quirk Louisiana has), Oregon’s rule rolls Saturday in its own right, statewide, and **the engine’s `isWeekend()` is correct here**. Worth stating explicitly because the two statutes look superficially alike and Louisiana was blocked on exactly this. **ORCP 10 A EXPRESSLY ANSWERS THE MARYLAND OPEN DECISION:** the sub-7-day threshold applies to the period **“(without regard to section B of this rule)”** — section B being the service extension — so **the 7-day test is measured BEFORE the 3 days are added**. Maryland’s Rule 1-203 leaves that identical question unresolved and it is logged as ship-disclosed-do-not-guess. Oregon does not decide Maryland, but it proves the question is a known one, that a drafter saw it coming, and **which way that drafter resolved it** — **carry this into the Maryland decision row as persuasive material.** **OTHER FIELDS:** exclusion is **seven**; the holiday basis is an express cross-reference to **BOTH ORS 187.010 and 187.020** (most states name one); there is a public-office-closure limb **broader than any seen** — it fires when the office is closed “before the end of **or for all of** the normal work day”, so a **partial-day** closure counts — and it is ADDITIONAL to the rollover rather than a replacement, so EARLY; no backward provision; and **ORCP 10 A disapplies itself to time limitations governed by ORS 174.120**, which was NOT read and must be before seeding. **ORCP 10 B IS THE BROADEST SERVICE ENUMERATION YET** — **+3 for mail, e-mail, facsimile AND electronic service** (eighth distinct answer on electronic service), period-lengthening not Alabama’s federal after-expiry order, and **“Except for service of summons” is stated in the rule itself**, removing the single most repeated inference in this engine — every other seeded state required deducing the Rule 4/Rule 5 scope split. **ORS 187.010 HAS A SELF-REFERENTIAL TRAP:** the shift is both ways, but the Sunday limb reads “Each time a holiday, **other than Sunday**, … falls on Sunday” — **without that carve-out every Sunday would make the following Monday a holiday**, because Sunday is itself item (1)(a). A blanket Sunday-shift generator would produce **52 phantom Mondays a year**. Being both-ways it also spills across the year boundary, so compute a wider span than you emit. ORS 187.020 adds Governor-appointed days and presidential days **only when the Governor also appoints** — note the conjunctive: a presidential day alone is NOT an Oregon holiday. Ad hoc, EARLY. **NO COLUMBUS/INDIGENOUS PEOPLES DAY AT ALL**, unlike AL, SC, LA, MN, MO, NJ, VA and MA. **OREGON HAS NO WRITTEN INTERROGATORIES.** Searching the entire ORCP for “interrogator” returns **four hits, all jury interrogatories** under Rule 61 C. There is no discovery-interrogatory rule at all — **every other seeded state has an interrogatory row; Oregon must have none, and that is the law rather than unfinished work**, which the seed readme has to say or the absence reads as a gap. **PERIODS:** ORCP 7 C(2) — defendant must **“appear and defend within 30 days”** (that phrasing, not “file an answer”), or 30 days from the date stated in the summons if served by publication; ORCP 43 B production **30 days with a 45-day defendant floor**; ORCP 45 B admissions **30/45 with silence ADMITTING**, plus a mandatory capital-letters warning on the request itself, the same device Missouri’s R. 59.01(a)(1) uses. Both floors run from **service of the summons**, a caller-supplied date, so Oregon does NOT hit the Maryland chained-floor gap **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28 (Hank) — 6 rules, `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_oregon.json`, new standards `or_orcp_10` and `or_orcp_10_b`, calendar 2026–2031 from `tools/gen_or_calendar.py`. `deadline-oregon.test.js` 52/52.** All four gate conditions met: text taken from the **Legislature’s** file not the Council’s static copy (asserted — every row’s URL is `oregonlegislature.gov`); **NO interrogatory row**, asserted so its absence cannot be read as unfinished work; the Sunday shift applied **only to holidays other than Sunday**, asserted by count in both the generator and the test; and **ORS 174.120 read** — it is the STATUTORY computation rule, it counts the same way but has **NO short-period exclusion at all**, so a statutory Oregon row must not use `or_orcp_10`. Not reached by any seeded row. **“EXCEPT FOR SERVICE OF SUMMONS” IS IN THE RULE’S OWN WORDS**, so the two 7 C(2) rows carry no extension and nothing rests on the inference that was got wrong federally. **THE EXTENSION REACHES E-MAIL, FAX AND ELECTRONIC SERVICE** — the opposite of NV/WV/NY/federal; the test asserts Nevada still adds 0 for e-mail on the same date Oregon adds 3, so if either ever matches the other one is wrong. **BOTH DISCOVERY FLOORS ARE `resolve_periods`** with different counts per limb (30/45), verified to pick the **45-day limb from the EARLIER trigger date** — exactly the case a plain `later_of` gets wrong — and to refuse `INCOMPLETE_TRIGGERS` on a partial set. The two floors use **different summons triggers** (43 B(2) from the summons, 45 B from the summons AND complaint) and the test asserts they stay distinct. **Test proven to catch a defect before being trusted:** injecting a phantom Columbus Day fails 3 of 52, including the arithmetic case. **The ORCP 10 A “without regard to section B” finding still belongs in the Maryland decision row** as persuasive material on the identical open question | L |

VA **1:7** carves itself down in Parts Five/Five A to briefs in opposition — already disclosed, and no VA appellate row carries the extension. WV **6(e)** points at Rule 5(b)(2)(F) but labels it with (G)’s text, giving opposite answers on consented electronic service — **both contested methods are REFUSED VISIBLY** rather than tie-broken, which is the best-handled case in the platform **✅ BUILT AND SHIPPED 2026-08-27, ahead of new coverage on Michael’s direction.** **THE MECHANISM:** a caller may now supply **`service_methods`**, an array holding the FULL SET of methods actually used. A rule opts in with **`requires_exclusive: true`**; `exclusivityOf()` returns exclusive / combined / unknown. Louisiana (~4.6M), a **civil-law** jurisdiction with a Code of Civil Procedure rather than rules of court, read fresh on the assumption that none of the accumulated pattern transfers — and that paid off immediately. `legis.la.gov` publishes the C.C.P. and Revised Statutes free on plain curl with per-article Acts history, so sources are fine. **THE BLOCKER: `isWeekend()` IS WRONG FOR MOST OF LOUISIANA.** C.C.P. art. 5059(A) rolls the last day **only “unless it is a legal holiday”** — it does NOT separately name Saturday and Sunday the way every common-law rule seeded does. La. R.S. 1:55(A)(1) then makes **Sundays** a statewide legal holiday but makes **Saturday a legal holiday ONLY in enumerated parishes** — Orleans, the city of Baton Rouge, the parishes of the 2nd and 6th congressional districts except Ascension, and the parishes of the 14th and 31st judicial districts. **Everywhere else a Saturday is an ordinary day and the deadline does NOT roll.** This engine rolls BOTH weekend days unconditionally for every jurisdiction, so Louisiana deadlines landing on a Saturday would roll in the majority of parishes where the law does not roll them — a date **LATER than the true deadline**. **This is the first jurisdiction where a core engine assumption is affirmatively wrong, and it cannot be fixed in a calendar file.** It is geographic like MA’s Suffolk County and AL’s Mardi Gras, but those were SAFE — omitting a county-only extra holiday runs EARLY. Louisiana inverts it: the engine would apply a holiday that does not exist in most of the state. **A coverage disclosure cannot fix a LATE-direction error**, the standing rule since Kentucky. **THREE OPTIONS, NONE CHOSEN:** (1) seed only the Saturday-holiday parishes as their own jurisdiction code and refuse the rest — honest, covers a minority of the state; (2) add a per-jurisdiction **weekend flag** to the computation standard (default `[Sat, Sun]`, `[Sun]` for `la`) — correct, and the largest, because weekend handling is shared by all nineteen seeded jurisdictions and needs a full regression pass; (3) refuse Louisiana and record why. **ART. 5059 ALSO INVERTS THE FRAMING:** “A legal holiday is **INCLUDED** in the computation … except when (1) it is expressly excluded, (2) it would otherwise be the last day, (3) the period is less than seven days.” Exception (3) reaches the same arithmetic as the engine’s existing 7, but (1) is a **per-article opt-OUT** a standard-level flag cannot express — the mirror of Minnesota’s opt-IN. And **“A half-holiday is considered a legal holiday”** is the **exact opposite of South Carolina**, gated one day earlier, whose R. 6(a) says a half holiday is NOT a holiday — and it is not academic, because R.S. 1:55 creates real half-holidays in **Sabine and Vernon parishes**. Art. 5059(D) carries a **second computation standard inside one article** — holidays excluded outright for administrative appeals, with named carve-outs for Revenue, Environmental Quality and Insurance — which must never reach civil litigation. **STATEWIDE HOLIDAYS NO OTHER JURISDICTION HAS:** Battle of New Orleans (8 Jan), **Mardi Gras** (statewide here, where in ALABAMA it is two counties only), **Good Friday** (full statewide, where Wisconsin needed a bespoke clause and South Carolina omits it), Huey P. Long Day (30 Aug), All Saints’ Day (1 Nov). **CONDITIONAL PERIODS THE ENGINE CANNOT SEE:** art. 1001 gives **21 days to answer, but 30 if the plaintiff served a discovery request with the petition** — a fact about what the plaintiff did, so these must be **separate rows with unmissable names**, never one row with a default (guessing 30 where 21 applies runs LATE); and art. 1458 gives interrogatories 30 days **but 15 in FAMILY LAW cases** unless served with the original petition — a domain-scoped period no seeded jurisdiction has, belonging in its own domain. **No 30/45 defendant floor appears at all**, consistent with reading this jurisdiction fresh. **CURRENCY CAVEAT:** the served text is updated only through the **2025 First Extraordinary Session**, with 2026 Regular Session changes on a separate list that any seed must cross-check — the Maryland Rules-Order shape; art. 5059 already shows Acts 2025 No. 250. **NOT DETERMINED, and the first is the most important:** whether **any** C.C.P. article adds days for service by mail or electronic means — art. 1313 has none, and assuming “no extension” without confirming is exactly the inherited assumption this fresh read existed to avoid; art. 1467’s admissions deadline; art. 1462(B)(2)’s exception; and which parishes comprise the named congressional and judicial districts — noting congressional districts are **redistricted**, so that membership is not stable over time | L |

Rules 6, 12, 33, 34 and 36 plus Ala. Code § 1-3-8 all read verbatim. **ACCESS NOTES:** `/library/rules` is a 404 — the working path is `/library/rulesofcourt` → `/library/CivilProcedure`; and the Code site is a JS drill-down (Title → Chapter → Section) so a guessed section URL fails on curl while a browser walks it. **CURRENCY IS THE FRESHEST SEEN:** Rule 6 carries **“Amended 3-26-2026, effective 4-9-2026”** — four months old — a reminder that even a year-old snapshot would be stale on the most load-bearing rule in the state. **FOUR THINGS THAT WOULD BE WRONG IF INHERITED FROM A NEIGHBOUR.** **(1) THE SERVICE ORDER IS FEDERAL AFTER-EXPIRY.** R. 6(d): three days “are added **AFTER THE PERIOD WOULD OTHERWISE EXPIRE** under Rule 6(a)” — `roll_then_add_then_roll`, where **the last eight states seeded are all period-lengthening** (NJ, NC, WA, NY, VA, MA, MO, MN, SC). The two orders differ whenever the unrolled last day is a weekend or holiday, and the error is **not** consistently in the safe direction. Electronic-filing-system service DOES get the three days, enumerated beside mail in the rule itself — the Massachusetts pattern, and the **seventh** distinct answer on electronic service. **(2) THE EXCLUSION IS ELEVEN DAYS** (“less than 11 days”), matching TN/AZ/WI, not the 7 of NJ/NC/WA/MA/MO/SC nor Minnesota’s absence. **(3) THE RULE NAMES ITS OWN ELEVEN HOLIDAYS** — a shape no seeded state has. Everywhere else the rule points at a statute or at nothing; R. 6(a)(4)(A) enumerates eleven in its own text and (B) adds “any other day declared a holiday by the President or Congress **or as prescribed by § 1-3-8**”. So the answer is a UNION of the rule’s eleven, the statute’s thirteen and the federal list — and the Committee Comment says so: “the net effect is the inclusion of all holidays whether state or federal”. **§ 1-3-8 adds two the rule omits — Confederate Memorial Day (4th Mon April) and Jefferson Davis’ birthday (1st Mon June)** — both still legal holidays via limb (B), so encoding only the rule’s eleven computes EARLY. **(4) THANKSGIVING HAS NO STATUTORY DATE:** § 1-3-8(a)(12) says “Thanksgiving Day, **as designated by the Governor**”, so unlike every other seeded state it is not derivable from the holiday statute and must come from the federal limb. **THE SHIFT IS THE CLEANEST YET** — § 1-3-8(b) shifts BOTH WAYS, unconditionally, for “any holiday”: no enumerated subset (contrast Minnesota’s five named days) and no “for the purposes aforesaid” caveat (contrast South Carolina). It WILL produce year-boundary spills, so the generator must compute a wider span than it emits — exactly what the Minnesota generator’s own assertion caught. **DISCLOSURES:** **Mardi Gras is a holiday in BALDWIN AND MOBILE COUNTIES only** (§ 1-3-8(e)(1)) — the Massachusetts Suffolk-County shape, omitting runs EARLY there and is correct elsewhere, and note it is a movable feast tied to Easter so derivable but not trivially; and R. 6(a)(3)’s weather/inaccessibility limb is ADDITIONAL to the rollover rather than a replacement, so EARLY. **ONE ITEM FOR THE BUNDLED HOLIDAY QUESTION WHOSE DIRECTION IS *LATE*:** § 1-3-8(f)(1) lets a state office stay OPEN on a state holiday on 60 days’ written notice — if a court did so and the engine rolls anyway, that runs LATE. Small, but it is the only unsafe-direction item here and should not be waved through. **PERIODS:** answer 30 days (publication excepted), cross-claim 30, reply 30, motion limbs 10 days from NOTICE of the court’s action and 10 from SERVICE of a more definite statement (the same notice-vs-service split that decides the extension in MO and MA); Rules 33, 34 and 36 all **30/45 with no outlier**, unlike Missouri’s 45/45/60, and every floor runs from a caller-supplied date so Alabama does NOT hit the Maryland chained-floor gap; R. 6(c)’s 5-day and 1-day motion periods are far under 11 and are the natural first rows to seed because they actually exercise the exclusion. **Also banked:** R. 6(dc) disapplies the exclusion entirely in **unlawful-detainer and eviction** actions in district court — a domain-scoped carve-out no seeded jurisdiction has, irrelevant to a civil batch but not to be lost if landlord/tenant is ever seeded; and the Committee Comment records that the rule **overrides § 1-1-4** on whether Saturdays are holidays | L |

South Carolina (~5.4M). Both the Judicial Branch and the Legislature publish free on **plain curl 200**, no paywall, challenge or terms gate. **ACCESS TRAP RECORDED:** the obvious `sccourts.org/courtReg/displayRule.cfm?ruleID=6.0&ruleType=RCP` returns **HTTP 200 and 458 KB** — but the body is a NAVIGATION INDEX of every rule in every body of rules, containing none of “Computation”, “intermediate Saturdays” or “shall not be included”. **A 200 with a large payload is not proof you fetched the thing you asked for.** The working path is `/resources/judicial-community/court-rules/civil/rule-6/`. **A CORRECTION ON THE RECORD, BEFORE ANYTHING IS BUILT ON IT:** a web search returned, as Rule 6(e), that “Service by e-mail will be treated the same as service by U.S. Mail … therefore, five days shall be added”. **That sentence is not in Rule 6(e)** — the actual rule reaches service “by mail or upon a person designated by statute to accept service” and says nothing about e-mail. I then read the **2022 Supreme Court order “RE: Service by E-Mail in the Trial Courts”** (App. Case 2022-000029, 6 May 2022) end to end: paragraphs (a)–(f), and **no time-extension provision whatsoever**. So on the two sources actually read, **whether the five-day mail extension reaches e-mail is UNRESOLVED**. Direction decides the default: omitting runs EARLY (safe), adding runs LATE — **so seed mail-only and disclose the open question; do not add an e-mail extension on the strength of a search result.** Third form of the Tennessee lesson — same instinct to check the service materials, opposite outcome. **FIRST STATE REQUIRING A STATE-PLUS-FEDERAL UNION:** R. 6(a) rolls off “a Saturday, Sunday **or a State or Federal holiday**”. Every seeded state keys on one list, or on two STATE lists (Wisconsin). This matters concretely — **Juneteenth and Columbus Day are federal holidays and are NOT in S.C. Code § 53-5-10**, so encoding only the state list would miss both and compute EARLY. **FIVE DAYS FOR MAIL, NOT THREE**, and the drafting Note says so: “the additional time … is increased from 3 to 5 days.” A three-day extension copied from a neighbour computes **two days EARLY on every mailed SC period**. It also reaches service on a **statutory agent**, which no other seeded rule does. **THE STATE LIST IS LONG AND DISTINCTIVE:** Confederate Memorial Day (10 May), the day after Thanksgiving **in the statute itself** (where Minnesota leaves it to a branch option), and a **THREE-DAY Christmas block, 24, 25 and 26 December** — no other seeded jurisdiction has any of these. **TWO CAVEATS ON THE SHIFT, both for the bundled holiday question rather than new categories:** § 53-5-30 shifts both ways but says “for all of the purposes aforesaid” in a section captioned about **presentment of bills, notes and checks**, so whether it reaches COURT deadlines is arguable on the text (a smaller cousin of the Maryland list-but-not-shift problem); and it shifts only “the holidays mentioned in Section 53-5-10”, i.e. the STATE half — the federal half has its own observance rules under 5 U.S.C. § 6103, so SC may need **two shift rules for two halves of one union**, which no seeded generator does. Also: short-period exclusion is **seven**; **“A half holiday shall be considered as other days and not as a holiday”** is an express carve-out with no analogue anywhere seeded (inert today, but the rule pre-emptively refusing a category, so do not drop it); no backward provision; R. 6(d)’s **two-day** opposing-affidavit period is under seven and is the natural first row to seed because it actually exercises the exclusion; and R. 6(b) names 50(b), 52(b), 59 and 60(b) as non-extendable plus “the time for filing notice of intent to appeal is jurisdictional”. **Remaining effort, not a source problem:** Rules 12, 33, 34 and 36 not yet read — same host, same URL pattern **✅ SEEDED 2026-08-28 (Hank) — 9 rules, `sql/sairnlaw_deadline_seed_southcarolina.json`, new standards `sc_rcp_6` and `sc_rcp_6_e`, calendar 2026–2031 from `tools/gen_sc_calendar.py`. `deadline-southcarolina.test.js` 48/48.** All three gate conditions met. **(1) THE STATE ∪ FEDERAL UNION IS BUILT AND ASSERTED** — first in the platform. **Juneteenth and Columbus Day are FEDERAL-only** and absent from § 53-5-10, and the test asserts both as SHAPE *and* ARITHMETIC; stripping the federal half fails **4 of 48**, including two date cases. **(2) FIVE DAYS, mail and STATUTORY AGENT only** — the latter unique among seeded rules — and **e-mail deliberately excluded**, with the whole unresolved question written into the standard’s comment and the seed readme. The search result claiming Rule 6(e) extends to e-mail **is not in the rule**, and the 2022 Supreme Court e-mail order was read end to end with **no time-extension provision at all**; adding the days would compute **LATE** if wrong, so the default omits them. **(3) BOTH § 53-5-30 CAVEATS RECORDED** — the “purposes aforesaid” scope question (the section is captioned *effect on presentment of bills, notes, and checks*), and the fact that it shifts only the STATE half; the federal half’s 5 U.S.C. § 6103(b) was **CHECKED to be identical**, recorded as a checked negative in case they diverge. **NEW FINDING THE GATE DID NOT COVER — GEORGIA’S “MAY” / “SHALL NOT BE REQUIRED” SPLIT IS HERE TOO, and it decides the row shape.** Rules 33 and 34 say a defendant **MAY** serve within 45 days — a PERMISSIVE alternative from a different event, so each gets its own **plain row** and the engine never chooses. Rule 36 says a defendant **SHALL NOT BE REQUIRED** — a mandatory floor, so it is a **`resolve_periods`** (30/45). Reading across would compute LATE on 33/34 and EARLY on 36, where silence ADMITS. **THE THREE-DAY CHRISTMAS BLOCK (24, 25, 26 Dec — unique) COLLIDES under the shift in 2026, 2027 and 2028**; the generator deduplicates and records the merge. **In 2027 Christmas Day itself is not an emitted date** — 25 Dec is a Saturday so it merges back onto the 24th, and 26 Dec moves forward to Monday the 27th. Also carried: **Confederate Memorial Day** (10 May, fixed) and **the day after Thanksgiving** in the statute itself. **NO BACKWARD ROWS** — Rule 6(a) has no backward provision, so 6(d)’s 10-day notice and 2-day affidavit periods are absent even though the two-day one is the only row that would exercise the 7-day exclusion. Rule 6(b)’s no-enlargement list is recorded for future rows | L |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **Colorado gate SPLIT — statutes PASS outright, RULES blocked behind an “I Agree” whose own terms exclude court rules** | **Blocked 2026-08-26** ⚠️ | **Michael** (one narrow decision) | A terms decision, or one email to OLLS | Full write-up: `docs/sairnlaw-colorado-deadline-seed-gate.md`. **Stated plainly: C.R.C.P. Rule 6 was NOT read** — nothing in that doc reports what it says. **STATUTES PASS with a channel Tennessee never had:** `leg.colorado.gov` publishes the full C.R.S. as free official OLLS PDFs on a plain curl, and **C.R.S. § 24-11-101 was read verbatim from one** (p. 252 of 3,111, Title 24) with no Lexis and no gate. Currency caveat disclosed: every page footer says “Colorado Revised Statutes 2024 **Uncertified Printout**”, so it is the 2024 edition and expressly not certified — the opposite of Wisconsin, which certifies itself with an as-of date. **RULES ARE THE BLOCKER.** The Judicial Branch publishes only `Rule_Change_YYYY-NN.pdf` files, not a consolidated current text; the old `Full_set_of_CRCP_and_Water_Rules.doc` now redirects to a site search, `/rules`, `/court-rules`, `/self-help/rules` and `/supreme-court/rulemaking` all 404, and the promising “Full Table of Contents.pdf” is the **Pattern Civil Jury Instructions** TOC, not the Rules. Reconstructing from base-plus-amendments is the **Arizona method**, and Colorado has a specific reason it would bite: **Rule Change 2011(18) was a wholesale “TIME CALCULATION CHANGES” overhaul**, so any pre-2012 Rule 6 carries a “less than eleven days” exclusion that may no longer exist — wrong on the single most load-bearing property in the rule. **THE TENNESSEE QUESTION AGAIN, ON MUCH BETTER FACTS.** The consolidated text is on the Colorado Legal Resources public-access site reached from the General Assembly’s own link, and unlike Tennessee **it LOADS** (CRS TOC renders; a “Colorado - Local, State and Federal Court Rules” link is present). Reaching content needs an “I Agree” modal, **and I did not click it**. But read the modal: “**The Terms and Conditions do not apply to the text and numbering of the statutes, constitutional provisions, or court rules in the content of the site.** Pursuant to section 2-5-118, Colorado Revised Statutes, persons … **may publish, reprint, or otherwise distribute the statutes**”. So on its own words the terms exclude exactly the material this engine needs, state law affirmatively permits redistribution, and the site is maintained under contract with the **Committee on Legal Services of the General Assembly** — where Tennessee’s AOC expressly DISCLAIMED control and the URL 403s to everything. Four differences, all favourable; record them as different facts rather than collapsing Colorado into Tennessee. **NEXT ACTION, narrower than Tennessee’s:** decide whether clicking “I Agree” on a public-access site whose terms expressly exclude statutes and court rules is acceptable here. **Weigh a cheaper option FIRST:** the same page says OLLS “can provide electronic copies of specified portions of the Colorado Revised Statutes without charge” — whether that extends to the court rules is one email, and would sidestep the question entirely. **BANKED FROM § 24-11-101 REGARDLESS:** **FRANCES XAVIER CABRINI DAY, the first Monday in October** — Colorado replaced Columbus Day with it in 2020, no other jurisdiction here has it, so it is the natural “prove the calendar is read” day (the role Patriots’ Day played for MA and Truman Day for MO) **and Columbus Day must NOT be carried across** from NJ/VA/MA/MO, all of which count it. The statute links itself to courts in its own words — holidays are treated as Sunday “**and also for the holding of courts**” — so Colorado does not join the bundled which-list question on the statute side. **THERE IS NO WEEKEND-SHIFT PROVISION AT ALL** in § 24-11-101: it says nothing about a holiday falling on Saturday or Sunday, where every seeded state has one somewhere (Sunday-only in MA/MO/WI, both-ways in VA/WV/MD) — **and importing one by analogy is the exact mistake already caught three times**, so whatever Rule 6 says would have to carry that weight instead. Wisconsin (~5.9M). Civil procedure is STATUTORY (chs. 801–847) like North Carolina, so all of it sits on the Legislature’s own free site and **everything needed returns HTTP 200 to plain curl** — no browser, no challenge, no paywall, no terms gate, no publisher redirect. **CURRENCY IS STATUTORILY CERTIFIED WITH AN AS-OF DATE ON EVERY PAGE:** “updated through 2025 Wis. Act 247 and through all Supreme Court Orders … in effect on August 5, 2026. Published and certified under s. 35.18.” Stronger than Missouri’s per-section version history or Maryland’s per-rule credits — nothing has to be cross-checked against a second source. **NO NEW ENGINE MECHANISM REQUIRED:** § 801.15(5) is VIRGINIA’s shape, not Missouri’s — mail +3 days, and fax/e-mail/e-filing **+1 day if the transmission is “completed between 5 p.m. and midnight”**, else +0. That is an AMOUNT that varies by clock, so it maps straight onto the existing `amount(method, ctx)` with `service_time`; the completion standard built for Missouri is NOT needed. **Fifth state, fifth answer on electronic service** (MA +3 in the time rule; TN service rule deems it mail → +3; MO +0 with a moved trigger; MD +0 and no cutoff; WI +1 on a clock). Found by the standing habit again, this time in reverse: § 801.14(2) — the service rule — has NO deeming and NO cutoff, because Wisconsin puts the cutoff in the TIME rule instead, the opposite of Missouri. **THE ONE THING TO SETTLE, AND IT IS THE DANGEROUS DIRECTION:** § 801.15(1)(b) uses TWO DIFFERENT TESTS in one subsection — the last day rolls unless it is **“a day the clerk of courts office is closed”** (a CLOSURE test, the North Carolina shape), while the carefully-composed holiday definition in (1)(a) governs ONLY the sub-11-day intermediate-day exclusion. **No seeded jurisdiction uses one basis for the rollover and another for the exclusion.** Wisconsin’s clerks of circuit court are COUNTY officers, so if any clerk is open on a listed day, encoding the list for rollover rolls **LATE**. (Closures not on the list run EARLY, which is safe.) The 1985 Law Revision Committee Note is exactly the wrong word for this purpose — the Good Friday/Dec 24/Dec 31 amendment “will **permit** clerks to close their offices at these times”, permit not require, which is why this cannot be waved through. **Three options, none chosen:** (1) treat the union list as a closure proxy and disclose the mismatch — needs checking that clerks actually close on every listed day; (2) seed only periods ≥ 11 days so the exclusion never fires, and disclose the rollover basis — reduces but does not remove the exposure; (3) refuse Wisconsin rollover entirely — safe, near-useless. **THE HOLIDAY DEFINITION IS A UNION OF THREE SOURCES** and neither list is a superset: § 801.15(1)(a) takes § 230.35(4)(a) OR § 995.20 “or both”, PLUS “a full day on Good Friday”. Only in 230.35(4)(a): **Dec 24 and Dec 31**. Only in 995.20: 3rd Mon Feb, **Juneteenth, Columbus Day, Veterans Day and TWO ELECTION DAYS**. And Good Friday is a FULL day only for time computation — 995.20 makes it just 11 a.m.–3 p.m. for worship, so encoding 995.20 alone would miss it. **Shift is SUNDAY-ONLY in both** (230.35(4)(a)(10) narrower still: only if Jan 1, Jul 4 or Dec 25 falls on Sunday) — **third state running** where carrying VA’s or WV’s both-ways shift would invent a Friday holiday and roll LATE. **County-scoped, same shape as MA’s Suffolk County:** 995.20 makes municipal election day a holiday “in every 1st class city” (Milwaukee) and lets counties of 750,000+ add holidays by ordinance — omitting runs EARLY, safe, same coverage treatment. **THE ANSWER RULE IS THE MOST BRANCH-HEAVY YET (§ 802.06(1)(a)):** 20 days default, 20 for a guardian ad litem from appointment, 20 for a cross-claim answer and for a reply, **45 for the state or a state officer/employee/agent**, and **45 “if a defendant in the action is an insurance company, or if any cause of action … is founded in TORT”**, plus 10 days after notice of the court’s action on a denied motion and 10 after service of a more definite statement. **The tort/insurer branch is the trap** — it turns on what the CLAIM IS ABOUT, not on service, which the engine cannot infer; getting it wrong reports 20 days when 45 is due (EARLY, so safe in direction but badly wrong for a user, and it is the branch a large share of civil filings actually fall in). Needs its own row with an unmissable trigger name, like Missouri’s three § 55.25(a) branches. **DISCOVERY IS ORDINARY:** §§ 804.08, 804.09 and 804.11 are all 30 days with a 45-day defendant floor, no outlier (unlike Missouri’s 45/45/60), silence ADMITS under 804.11, and every floor runs from **service of the summons and complaint — a caller-supplied date, NOT a computed one — so Wisconsin does NOT hit the Maryland chained-floor gap**. Short-period exclusion is **ELEVEN days**, matching TN and AZ, and Wisconsin explains why: a 1986 Judicial Council Note records the extension from 7 to 11 “to conform to that made in Rule 6 (a), F.R.C.P. in 1985” — **so the 7-vs-11 split across states is a VINTAGE ARTEFACT**, useful when gating the rest. **Not determined:** whether WI clerks of circuit court publish closure schedules per county or centrally (option 1 turns on it), and the § 801.18 e-filing subchapter was not read beyond 801.15(5)(b)’s reference. Secondary disclosables: the **“between 5 p.m. and midnight”** boundary at exactly 17:00 is genuinely ambiguous (Virginia’s “no later than”/“after” wording is not), and a transmission at 02:00 is NOT between 5 p.m. and midnight so adds nothing — a naive “after 5 p.m.” implementation gets that wrong | L |

Sources are a clean pass: complete current Rules free with **plain curl 200**, Maryland Code free on the General Assembly site, per-rule credits with real effective dates, and 76 free Rules Order PDFs giving an **independent currency channel that does not depend on the publisher** — used here to confirm the 228th Order’s 2026-10-01 amendment to Rule 2-422 is deadline-neutral. **NOT Tennessee:** the full text sits on `govt.westlaw.com/mdc`, but as the government slice hosted for the Judiciary’s own Thurgood Marshall State Law Library, with no sign-in, no click-through and no terms acceptance — it loads on a bare curl. `dsd.maryland.gov` Rules page is a 404, dead lead. `WebFetch` fails on mdcourts.gov while curl works — a FOURTH access quirk after mass.gov, tncourts.gov and courts.mo.gov. **THE TRAP, AND IT FAILS LATE:** the intuitive path (Gen. Provisions s. 1-302 + s. 1-111) is wrong in BOTH directions — s. 1-302 rolls only off Sunday (no Saturday roll -> EARLY) while s. 1-111 adds Good Friday, Lincoln’s Birthday, Maryland Day and Defenders’ Day, four days that are NOT court holidays (-> LATE). Rule 1-203’s own committee note says it **supersedes s. 1-302**. Correct chain is Rule 1-203 -> Rule 1-202(l) -> **State Personnel & Pensions s. 9-201**. Third state running where the obvious statute is the wrong one — add to the bundled lawyer’s question as its own category: *a state with TWO plausible holiday statutes where the general one is wrong*. **A FOURTH ELECTRONIC-SERVICE PATTERN:** MDEC e-service gets **zero** added days and Rule 20-205’s committee note *expressly negates* the mail extension; no cutoff hour found, so Maryland needs NEITHER the extension nor the completion mechanism for e-service — the simplest of the four, knowable only by reading Title 20. **HOLIDAY LIST CANNOT BE DERIVED** — s. 9-201(14) Governor/President days are arbitrary (Maryland gave TWO observed days for Christmas 2022), (13) election days fire only in even years, and MLK/Memorial/Columbus each carry “unless Congress designates another day”. Ingest per year from `mdcourts.gov/administration/holidays`, joining NJ and NC as ingest-not-derive. **Shift is BOTH WAYS** (SPP s. 9-204) — first such state in three; do NOT carry MA’s or MO’s Sunday-only across. Doctrinal gap flagged: Rule 1-202(l) cites only s. 9-201 (list), not s. 9-204 (shift), and s. 9-202 confines that subtitle to the Executive Branch — resolved against six verbatim archived Judiciary calendar entries showing both directions applied, not by inference. **BUILD ITEMS:** the exclusion is “seven days or less”, and because `short_period_exclusion_days` is compared with a STRICT less-than, Maryland’s value is **8, not 7** (same trap the engine already documents for Texas); backward counting is NOT the mirror of forward (Rule 1-203(b) counts ALL intervening days and rolls the latest day BACKWARD — first jurisdiction needing a per-direction exclusion flag); and Rule 1-203(a)(2) is a second non-holiday roll trigger (clerk’s office not open or closed part of the day) needing an EARLY-direction disclosure | L |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **DECISION: Maryland’s <=7-day / mail-extension interaction — ship DISCLOSED, do not guess** | Open — **decision made 2026-08-26** | **Michael** | Bundled lawyer’s question | Md. Rule 1-203(c) adds three days **“to the prescribed period”**, so a mailed 7-day period arguably becomes a 10-day period — which is “more than seven days” — which would **flip intermediate Saturdays, Sundays and holidays from EXCLUDED to COUNTED**, changing the date. **Two live readings, no committee note, no cross reference, and no controlling authority found.** (A) “the period of time allowed” in 1-203(a) means the period AFTER the extension -> exclusion lost -> EARLIER date. (B) it means the underlying rule’s period -> exclusion survives and the 3 days are appended -> LATER date. They diverge on any mailed period of **4–7 days**. **This cannot be resolved by picking the safe side**, because which reading is safe depends on the period — that is what distinguishes it from every other disclosed gap in this engine, all of which fail consistently EARLY. **Michael’s direction 2026-08-26: ship as a disclosed ambiguity rather than guessing.** If a mailed short period is ever seeded, the engine should REFUSE rather than pick — the same call already made for W. Va. R. 6(e), which returns `refused_contested_standard` rather than resolving a rule against itself. Belongs with the bundled lawyer’s question **PERSUASIVE MATERIAL ADDED 2026-08-26 from the Oregon gate — a drafter who saw this exact question coming and answered it.** **ORCP 10 A** applies its sub-7-day threshold to the period **“(without regard to section B of this rule)”**, section B being the 3-day service extension. So Oregon measures the 7-day test on the UNDERLYING period, BEFORE the extension is added — reading (B) in the Maryland framing. That does not decide Maryland (different state, different rule) but it establishes three things worth having when someone answers this: the ambiguity is a KNOWN one rather than a quirk of Maryland’s drafting, at least one drafter anticipated it, and the resolution that drafter chose was the one that PRESERVES the exclusion. Md. Rule 1-203 has no equivalent parenthetical, which is precisely why it is ambiguous — so the contrast is itself the argument that Maryland’s silence should not be read as implying either answer. | S |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **ENGINE GAP: a discovery floor that CHAINS OFF A COMPUTED DATE — highest-stakes shape found in any gate** | Open — **own row on Michael’s direction 2026-08-26** | unassigned | A design decision on rule chaining | **This is an engine gap, not a Maryland quirk.** All three Maryland discovery rules use one construction; Rule 2-424(b) verbatim: “Each matter of which an admission is requested shall be **deemed admitted** unless, within 30 days after service of the request **or within 15 days after the date on which that party’s initial pleading or motion is required, whichever is later**…”. **Why this is unlike every later-of row already seeded:** Ohio, Georgia, NJ, NC, WA, VA, MA and MO all measure a defendant floor from a date the CALLER SUPPLIES (service of the complaint, an appearance, service of process). Maryland’s second limb runs from “the date on which that party’s initial pleading or motion is **required**” — **the output of another rule** — and it is not a static offset: Rule 2-321 sets the answer at **30, 60 or 90 days** depending on how and where the defendant was served, and Rule 2-321(c) extends it further to “15 days after entry of the court’s order” on a Rule 2-322 motion or remand. So the floor is 45 / 75 / 105 days, or a date that depends on a ruling. **`resolve_periods` cannot express it** — its limbs take an event and a count, and neither limb can be “the result of computing rule X”. **Nothing seeded today handles a trigger that depends on another rule’s outcome.** **THE STAKES ARE WHY THIS IS ITS OWN ROW:** Rule 2-424 is self-executing — miss it and facts are **DEEMED ADMITTED** — so a floor guessed short computes **EARLY on the one rule where an early date silently forfeits the case on the merits**. Most dangerous single shape encountered in any gate so far. **Three options, none chosen:** (1) caller supplies the computed date via a named limb event — honest and buildable today, but a caller picking the wrong 30/60/90 branch produces an EARLY floor; (2) build real rule chaining so a limb can name another rule — correct, and much the largest; (3) refuse the defendant limb and seed only the plain 30-day row with an explicit disclosure that a defendant’s true deadline may be later — safe (EARLY), least useful. **Maryland should not be seeded piecemeal until this is decided** | L |

Rules 5.02, 6.01, 6.05, 12.01, 33.01, 34.02, 36.01 read verbatim. **WHAT FAILS IT IS ONE STATUTE.** Rule 6.01 does not define "legal holiday"; it expressly delegates to **Tenn. Code Ann. § 15-1-101**, so no statute means no calendar and every TN row refuses. **THE AOC'S OWN CODE PAGE IS THE DECISIVE EVIDENCE** (`tncourts.gov/Tennessee%20Code`, read verbatim): the LEXIS site "is not operated by, nor is it under the control of, The Tennessee Administrative Office of the Courts", and "**Lexis has changed its free version of the Tennessee Code. The page does not say that — it says the AOC does not control the host and that content now sits behind a **terms-acceptance click**. The verdict is unchanged and rests on firmer ground read literally: **an agent must not click an "I agree" button on anyone's behalf** (the same line already drawn for BAILII/AustLII and with CanLII v. Caseway AI in mind), and that line is reached before any content-quality question. `lexisnexis.com/hottopics/tncode` 403s to curl AND a real browser in any case, and `advance.lexis.com` renders "Page Unavailable". The only free full text reachable is the open-data `release76.2021.05.21` snapshot, **provably stale** — it lacks Juneteenth, added by 2023 Tenn. Acts ch. 337 eff. 2023-05-05. **AND ONE GAP RUNS LATE, which forecloses a Virginia-style disclosure:** § 15-1-101 grants all election days as legal holidays on its face while its own Compiler's Notes record Acts 1984 ch. 979 letting the governor DELETE holidays and his choosing the election-day ones. **DISTINCT FROM BOTH PRIOR REFUSALS, do not collapse them:** Kentucky has no free rules at all plus a holiday basis failing LATE; Arizona was a *completeness/staleness* problem later cleared on `govt.westlaw.com`'s free government edition — **that clearance does NOT carry here**, because reading a page that simply loads is not the same act as accepting a contract. **What would reopen it:** a human obtaining § 15-1-101 through the gate and supplying the verbatim current text plus the governor's standing designations under Acts 1984 ch. 979. | **SAIRNlaw** | **Banked from the Tennessee gate — three findings that would corrupt a seed if carried across from a neighbour** | Open (reference, no action) | unassigned | — | Kept as its own row because these are reusable regardless of whether Tennessee is ever seeded, and two of them are traps this project has already hit once in another form. **(1) THE SHORT-PERIOD EXCLUSION IS ELEVEN DAYS, NOT SEVEN.** Tenn. R. Civ. P. 6.01: "When the period of time prescribed or allowed is less than eleven (11) days, intermediate Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays shall be excluded." Every other seeded state with an exclusion uses 7 (NJ, NC, WA, MA, WV appellate); only Arizona also uses 11. Encoding 7 by analogy would be wrong on every TN period of 7–10 days. Rule 6.04's five-day motion notice is the row that would actually exercise it — seed that one first if TN ever clears. **(2) A TIME RULE THAT READS MAIL-ONLY MAY NOT BE.** Tenn. R. Civ. P. 6.05 adds three days for service "by mail" and says nothing about electronic service — but **Rule 5.02(2)(c)** deems an emailed document "a document that was mailed for purposes of computation of time under Rule 6", and **Rule 5.02(3)(e)** does the same for E-service. So email and e-service DO get the three days, via the SERVICE rule rather than the time rule, and reading 6.05 alone gives a wrong EARLY answer. Facsimile has no such deeming provision and gets nothing. Tennessee reaches Massachusetts' outcome by a completely different route. **Standing habit this should become: check the service rule for deeming provisions before concluding a time rule is mail-only.** **(3) THE CLEANEST STATEMENT YET OF THE BUNDLED HOLIDAY QUESTION, AND IT POINTS AT THE STATUTE.** Advisory Commission Comment [2011] to Rule 6.01: "The status of a day as a legal holiday is statutory; thus, for the purpose of filing papers in court, **it does not depend on whether the clerk's office is open for business** … state offices might be open on Columbus Day … in such circumstances, however, Columbus Day is still a 'legal holiday' for purposes of computing time periods under the rule." The **exact inverse of North Carolina**, whose Rule 6(a) keys on courthouse closure — and it means the TN state-employee holiday schedule (§ 4-4-105) is the WRONG source, in terms. Worth carrying into the bundled lawyer's question as the model answer a rule can give. Also banked: TN Rules 33.01, 34.02 AND 36.01 all carry a 30-day period with a 45-day defendant floor, so all three need `later_of` rows (Massachusetts has it on only two of three); § 15-1-101's weekend shift runs BOTH ways like VA and WV, unlike MA's Sunday-only; and Good Friday is a TN legal holiday, as in NJ | S |

It does not point at G.S. 103-4, and that matters enormously: 103-4 lists Robert E. Lee's Birthday, Greek Independence Day, the Halifax Resolves anniversary, Confederate Memorial Day, the Mecklenburg Declaration anniversary, Columbus Day, First Responders Day, Yom Kippur, Washington's Birthday and general election day, and the NC Judicial Branch closes for **none of them**. Encoding 103-4 would add ~9 spurious holidays and push deadlines **LATE**. The right basis is the NCAOC's published closure schedule — same treatment already given to Michigan's locally-modifiable MCR schedule, and the **inverse** of the standing Pennsylvania guidance in the engine header, which says to encode the statute; NC is the counter-example where the rule itself keys on closure, so that guidance must be read as conditional on what the rule's own test is. **(2) COVERAGE WILL BE 2026–2027 ONLY, not the 2026–2031 every other jurisdiction has.** NCAOC publishes dated schedules two years forward (read 2026-08-25 from `nccourts.gov/holiday-schedule`, which is JS-rendered and 403s to plain fetches — it needs a real browser). 2028+ must refuse NOT_PROVISIONED honestly, and the calendar needs re-running each year as NCAOC publishes. **(3) THE CHRISTMAS AND THANKSGIVING BLOCKS ARE ADMINISTRATIVE, NOT DERIVABLE** — Christmas is THREE days (2026: Dec 24, 25, 28; 2027: Dec 23, 24, 27) and Thanksgiving TWO, allocated by decision, not by any rule. Proof that a computed calendar would be wrong. Also already read: Rule 6(e) adds three days **"to the prescribed period"** (period-lengthening, NOT the federal order) and **mail only**; Rule 6(f) adds **five** days for Address Confidentiality Program participants but **only on periods of 10 days or less**. **Corrected 2026-08-25: the engine addition this clause predicted was BUILT (`73735ab`, `amount(method, ctx)` carrying `base_period_count`), so the base-period-length problem is solved — 6(f) is still not implemented, for an unrelated reason.
