Implemented as ONE widened gate rather than 32 edits — every site already calls `sdDemoCleared()`, and rewriting 59 call sites in a 2MB file is the bulk find-replace CLAUDE.md forbids. Consequences recorded rather than discovered later: the gate's NAME is now narrower than its behaviour (`sdDemoClearedByUser()` carries the original meaning for the Admin toggle, which would otherwise tell every real customer they had cleared demo data they never had); an UNLICENSED install now gets empty states too; and re-enabling demo mode off the demo licence no longer reports a success it did not have. `tests/demo_seed_licence_scope.js`, 15 assertions. **A separate omission found by deriving the list instead of reading it:** `SAFE_DEMO_KEYS` was missing `sd_it_tickets` and `sd_it_licenses` — their fallbacks are named `TICKETS`/`LICENSES`, so every scan looking for a `SEED`-named fallback walked past them, exactly as happened to `sd_fieldmap` in 2026-08-04; Clear Demo Data had been reporting success and leaving the IT panel seeded. **The figures the decision was made on:** the other 30 sites are purely local and hide nothing, but a first-run shop still sees invented figures presented as its own business — `sd_ap` computes four dollar KPIs (open, due, **overdue**, MTD paid) plus an aging table off six invented bills **dated 2024**; `sd_equipment` ~$471,000; `sd_bids` ~$293,000. The Slabs precedent says delete the fallback and show an honest empty state; doing that to 30 panels removes the demo from the product. **Recorded with the numbers so the call is made on numbers rather than on "~28 unaudited".** **NOT DONE and not claimed:** the follow-on's second half — whether the other fifteen apps carry the same shape | L |
| **StoneDesk** | ~~Procedural stone texture likely inflates `canvas.toDataURL()` PNG snapshots against an existing quota risk~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (Cody)** in `bf490b6d` on Michael's decision — JPEG q0.70 at full resolution, cap 50 → 20, live-verified end to end | Cody | — | **Measured in a real browser against the deployed app, not estimated.** `ct-canvas` is 640×480; `sdDrawSave()` stores `toDataURL('image/png')` in `sd_drawings`, capped at **50 entries**. Flat placeholder (no stone selected): **18.4 KB PNG / 25,114 chars**. Worst realistic textured case (U-shape, granite/dark): **49.9 KB PNG / 68,186 chars** — a **2.7×** inflation, so the premise holds. All twelve stone/tone combinations measured; granite is the most expensive by a wide margin (45–46 KB) and quartzite/light the cheapest (18.1 KB, indistinguishable from flat).<br><br>**THE CONSEQUENCE IS PLATFORM-WIDE, WHICH THIS ROW DID NOT ANTICIPATE.** localStorage counts UTF-16, so one worst-case entry costs **133 KB of quota**, and **50 × 133 KB ≈ 6.5 MB against a ~5 MB origin budget**. The 50-entry cap is therefore not a bound that protects anything. And `sairn.vercel.app` is **one origin shared by every SAIRN app** — measured live: **199 keys, 1.25 MB already in use** before a single drawing exists, `sd_slabs` alone 595,280 chars. A rep filling StoneDesk's drawing history does not degrade StoneDesk; it **starves every other SAIRN app on the same origin**, and their `st()` failures would surface as their own "could not save" messages, blamed on the wrong app.<br><br>**WHAT IS ALREADY FINE, checked rather than assumed:** the write is honest — `st()` returns false and `sdDrawSave()` already shows *"Could not save — storage may be full or unavailable"*, so this fails loudly, not silently. And `sd_drawings` is **local-only**: it appears in no resource registry and no sync list, so the 64 KB `MAX_PAYLOAD_BYTES` cap in `api/sd-data.js` is not in play. Both hypotheses were checked and both came back clean.<br><br>**THE DECISION, with measured options rather than opinions — all four numbers are quota cost per entry, worst case:** today PNG 640×480 = **133 KB**; JPEG q0.85 same size = **70 KB**; JPEG q0.70 = **49 KB**; PNG at half scale (320×240) = **58 KB**; JPEG q0.85 at half scale = **23.5 KB**. Lowering the 50-entry cap is the other lever and costs no fidelity. **Not changed, because every option trades something a customer sees** — JPEG blurs crisp line art, downscaling loses detail on a drawing an operator reads dimensions from, and a smaller cap silently shortens history. This row asked to measure before changing anything; the measurement was done first and the change followed.<br><br>**DECIDED AND SHIPPED 2026-09-04 (Michael's call): JPEG q0.70 at FULL resolution, plus the cap 50 → 20.** Downscaling was rejected deliberately — an operator reading a dimension off an old drawing is a **functional** risk, not a cosmetic one.<br><br>**THE VISUAL CHECK BEFORE SHIPPING CAUGHT WHAT THE ARITHMETIC COULD NOT, and it would have been a correctness regression.** `ct-canvas` is **transparent** — the pale field is CSS, not pixels — and JPEG has no alpha, so a bare `toDataURL('image/jpeg', 0.70)` **flattens it to BLACK**. Seen in the browser: the grid lines vanish entirely, dark grey on black, and the drawing comes back as something the rep never drew. Shipping on the numbers alone would have traded a size problem for a worse one. The snapshot is now composited onto the canvas's **own computed** background first, read from `getComputedStyle` rather than hardcoded, with a white fallback because falling back to *none* is the black field again.<br><br>**Result, measured on the deployed build by driving the real `sdDrawSave()`:** 68,186 chars → **29,123 chars** (0.43×), quota cost **133 KB → 57 KB** per entry, whole store **~6.5 MB → ~1.1 MB**. Side by side the edge colour-coding, the texture and the panel label are all legible, and the JPEG is if anything closer to what the rep sees on screen than the transparent PNG was. **The live origin was restored exactly** — `sd_drawings` removed again, 654,296 chars and 199 keys before and after. 15 assertions in `tests/stonedesk_drawing_snapshot_budget.js`, four mutations probed; the composite has one assertion per load-bearing part, because the fill must happen BEFORE the `drawImage` or it covers the drawing.<br><br>**~~FOUND WHILE HERE, NOT FIXED — `sd_drawings` is WRITTEN AND NEVER READ.~~ CLOSED the same day (Cody) in `726677f9` on Michael's decision to build the missing half rather than delete the feature.** A **Saved Drawings** list now sits on the Drawing Tool toolbar: each row shows the drawing's own snapshot, its name and date, and loads it back onto the canvas. **The decision that made it more than a list is a correctness one:** the store held a PICTURE and nothing else, so a "reload" could only have painted a raster onto a canvas whose model still described something else — shape, dimensions and cutouts would all be whatever was on screen, and the first recalculation would contradict the image. `sdDrawSave()` therefore also stores `dcSnapshotDrawingState()`, **the same snapshot the quote history already keeps**, and the loader is the same `dcLoadDrawingState()` with the same `schemaVersion` check — no second mechanism. **Entries saved before this are listed, greyed, and told why** ("saved before drawings kept their measurements"), because hiding them reads as data loss and enabling them would be a control that does nothing. The unsaved-work confirm moved into **one** `dcHasUnsavedWork()` used by both loaders, with a test asserting there is exactly one definition. **Deletion deliberately not added** — a list and a reload is what was asked for, the 20-entry cap already rolls entries off, and an unrequested destructive control is not mine to add. 32 assertions, four mutations; live-verified on the deployed build and the rendered panel photographed.<br><br>**`SAIRN-BACKLOG.md`'s 2026-08-13 entry is now partly STALE and is corrected here rather than left to mislead:** it predicted a *"5-15x"* inflation (measured: **2.7×**) and described `sdDrawSave()` as swallowing quota failures silently while claiming success. **That half was fixed at some point since** — `st()` returns a boolean the caller reads and the status line says *"Could not save"* on a refused write, both asserted so they cannot regress | M |
| **StoneDesk** | ~~Saved-quote Load: overwrite confirm fires on a merely-opened Drawing Tool tab~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (Cody)** in `7da9b31e`, live-verified | Cody | — | The gate asked `gN('da-len') > 0 \|\| gN('da-dep') > 0`. `initDrawPanel()` calls `selectDrawShape('straight')`, which **writes 96 and 25.5 into exactly those boxes**, so the test was true before the rep had touched anything. **The cost is not the extra click:** a confirm that appears when there is nothing to lose teaches a rep to dismiss confirms without reading them, and the next one will be real. **Dropping the dimension check would have been the wrong fix** — it would silently discard a rep's typed dimensions on a preset with no polygon, which is the case the old test was reaching for. `dcDimsEdited()` compares each box against the value THIS shape wrote, **captured as it is written rather than re-listed**, because the defaults live inline in `selectDrawShape()`'s if/else chain and a second copy would drift the first time one changed. Clearing a default counts as an edit; a shape whose sections default to 0 does not read as edited for sitting empty. 19 assertions in `tests/stonedesk_quote_load_state.js`, four mutations probed | S |
| **StoneDesk** | ~~`dcMode` doesn't reset to `preset` on a same-session Custom-Draw-then-preset quote Load~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (Cody)** in `7da9b31e`, live-verified | Cody | — | `dcLoadDrawingState()` restored the mode **in one direction only** — `if (state.dcMode === 'draw') setDCMode('draw')` — so loading a PRESET quote while the session was in draw mode left `dcMode` as `draw` with the freshly-restored `dcPoly` **empty**. `drawCTPreview()` dispatches on `dcMode`, so the canvas rendered the polygon view of nothing instead of the preset rectangle: **a blank drawing for a quote that has dimensions.** **Pricing was unaffected** — `isCustom` also requires `dcPolyClosed`, which was false — and that is exactly why it read as a display glitch rather than the state bug it is. | **SAIRNbiz** | **Benefits enrolment recorded a real employer cost that payroll ignored, and the same $520 was documented monthly but charged per pay period** | **CLOSED 2026-09-03 (Cody) &mdash; `pay_freq` is now a per-employee field and every payroll figure derives from it** | Cody | Nothing | **The decision that unblocked it: there is no single pay period.** Frequency varies by employee inside one company &mdash; hourly and salaried staff commonly biweekly, sales sometimes monthly, occasionally weekly, semi-monthly rarely and usually only by explicit agreement (Michael, 2026-09-03). So it is an EMPLOYEE field, not an app setting. **What changed:** `pay_freq` (Weekly / Biweekly / Semi-monthly / Monthly) on the employee record, editable in the modal and shown in both the employee and payroll tables, marked "default" until set. | **SAIRNbiz** | ~~No server-side persistence for anything except the employee roster &mdash; and the ledger implies more data integrity than actually exists~~ | **CODE CLOSED 2026-09-04 (CC) &mdash; ⚠ SQL PENDING, so the gap is NOT yet closed for a customer.** THE SCOPE, MEASURED OFF THE FILE RATHER THAN TAKEN FROM THIS ROW: `sairnbiz.html` writes **SEVENTEEN** localStorage collections through `st()`/`ld()`, and exactly ONE reached a server (`sb_emps`, via the bespoke `employees` branch). **NINE are now backed up** through one generic read/write pair in `api/sd-data.js` (`SB_RESOURCES`) plus `sql/sairnbiz_data_schema.sql` &mdash; 9 tables, which **must be run in Supabase before any of it does anything**. Until then writes answer 503 NOT_PROVISIONED naming the file and reads answer `provisioned:false`, and **nothing in the UI claims a backup exists**, deliberately. **Eight collections excluded on purpose, each with its reason in the registry** &mdash; `sb_emps` already syncs through the role-gated `employees` branch (folding it in would route the roster past that gate and into a differently-tenanted second copy), `sb_co`/`sb_cfg` are single objects not keyed collections, `sb_incidents` has no write path at all, and `sb_lic`/`sb_role`/`sb_seeded`/`sb_sync` are app state. **TWO DELIBERATE DIVERGENCES FROM THE SAIRNbuild PRECEDENT, both stated rather than drifted into.** (1) **A SESSION GATE.** `BLD_RESOURCES` gates on the licence alone and argues that correctly &mdash; those are the shared job record. These nine are payroll history, performance scores and PIP flags, and the licence key is a string `sairnbiz.html` itself documents as NOT auth. It is a SESSION gate, not a ROLE gate: accounting legitimately reads AP/AR and HR legitimately reads training. (2) **ID MINTING.** Four of the nine (`sb_exps`, `sb_perf`, `sb_hire`, `sb_bud`) store records with **no identifier of any kind** &mdash; copying the precedent verbatim would have silently backed up nothing for four of nine while the other five worked, the half-applied failure the `st()` hook exists to prevent, reintroduced through the data. Ids are minted and persisted, same derive-then-persist shape `sbNormalizeBills()` already uses here. The other five are NOT minted: their id is user-visible (an invoice number, a vendor code), so a missing one is reported instead of invented. **THE SILENT-CATCH HALF, WHICH IS THE SHARPER FINDING.** `st()` was `try{...}catch(e){}` and returned NOTHING, so every caller &mdash; `saveExp`, `saveBill`, `runPayroll`, `saveCertRenewal` &mdash; showed its success toast unconditionally on the next line. **A browser that could not store the record told the user it had**, and the LEDGER half of the same action still reached Postgres, so the journal entry survived while the record it was derived from did not. It now returns a boolean and says **"THIS DID NOT SAVE"**, once per key so a render-path failure cannot toast on every repaint. **AND THE LOGIN ORDER CHANGED, because the fix would otherwise have created a new defect:** a fresh device seeded Pinnacle demo rows and THEN merged the real backup, mixing sample invoices into a real company's books. A never-seeded device now hydrates first (it has nothing to paint, so nothing is delayed) and marks itself seeded if records came back; a device that already holds data still paints first. The blocking path is bounded at 6s so an unreachable server cannot hang the app. **It is a BACKUP, not two-way sync:** per-record last-write-wins, deletions do not propagate (`api/sd-data.js` has no delete path outside `sc_*`, platform-wide). **Nothing here is a ledger defect** &mdash; `sbGlPost()` already refuses to claim a post it did not get. | CC | ⚠ Michael: run `sql/sairnbiz_data_schema.sql` | 41 assertions + **6 mutation probes on the sync and 2 on the failure voice, all 8 bite**; registry/handler/client/SQL cross-checked in all four directions (9 declared, 9 handled, 9 synced, 9 tables); 268 resources platform-wide with zero cross-app collisions | L |
| **SAIRNbiz** | ~~`saveExp()` mints a ledger `source_id` that is stored nowhere &mdash; the journal entry points at a record that cannot be found~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (Cody)** in `ba55a4bc`, live-verified &mdash; and the identical site in `saveBill()` closed with it | Cody | &mdash; | **CLOSED.** The id is now minted **onto the record** in `saveExp()` and used as the ledger `source_id`, so an expense entry names a row that exists and every posting in the file keys the same way. **`saveBill()` had the same shape one function down and was fixed in the same change** &mdash; leaving one of two identical sites is how this codebase keeps rediscovering one defect. Its `blId` fell back to a minted `BILL-...` whenever the invoice number was blank and that string was never stored, **while the `bill_paid` entry for the SAME bill used `b.id`** &mdash; two entries for one bill, two different source ids, neither linkable to the other. Both now name the same record; the invoice number stays on the record as the human handle but is not the key, because it is optional and two vendors can issue the same one. **The id is minted at creation and deliberately NOT left to `sbEnsureIds()`**, which also mints an `EX` id: that helper runs inside the backup hook behind a licence check and two availability flags, so on an unlicensed or offline device it never runs &mdash; and offline is exactly when the local ledger is the only copy. Both sites reuse `sbEnsureIds`/`sbNormalizeBills` formats and collision loops, so the schemes cannot diverge and two saves in the same millisecond cannot share an id. Side effect worth naming: a new expense or bill now has its id **before the first render**, so the backup pushes it immediately instead of waiting for a normaliser pass. **The pre-existing ledger rows written under the old scheme still carry orphan ids** &mdash; they pointed at nothing before this change and still do; nothing was rewritten, and no migration is proposed here. 23 assertions in `tests/sairnbiz_ledger_source_id.js` driving the real `saveExp()`/`saveBill()` against a store holding **no licence key**, which is what proves the id cannot have come from the backup path; three mutations probed. Original finding follows.  `saveExp()` builds `exId='EX-'+String(Date.now()).slice(-6)` purely to fill `source_id` on the `sbGlPost()` call, and **never writes it onto the expense record**. So every expense journal entry carries a source id that matches nothing in `sb_exps`, and tracing an entry back to its expense is impossible from inside the app. The backup change now mints a **different** id (`EX<base36>-<n>`) onto the record itself, which does not close this &mdash; the two are unrelated strings. **Not folded into that commit on purpose:** changing what `source_id` means is a ledger-shape decision with existing rows already written under the old scheme, and it belongs in its own change with its own read of `/api/ledger`. The bill path has the same shape and is slightly better (`blId` falls back to the typed invoice number, which at least appears on the record). | S |

| **Tooling** | ~~Push-gate check 6 (the credential scan) reported itself UNCHECKED every time a push lost a race with another clone~~ | **FIXED 2026-09-04 (CC)** &mdash; root cause read out of the reflog, not reasoned; held by `tests/push_gate/redaction_base_probe.py` | CC | &mdash; | git hands a PRE-PUSH hook the sha the **remote has right now**, which by definition can be a commit this clone has never fetched. `git log <that sha>..<tip>` exits **128 Invalid revision range**, check 6 turned that into a bare RuntimeError, and the fail-open handler printed *"this push is UNCHECKED for credentials"* &mdash; so the push shipped scanned by nothing. **With four clones pushing concurrently this is the ORDINARY case, not an edge one:** it fires every time you lose a race. Observed live while pushing the SAIRNbiz work, then confirmed from `git reflog show origin/main` &mdash; the hook was handed `55a408a5` while this clone's origin/main was still `ae89eea5`, and `55a408a5` arrived on the NEXT fetch, after that push. **The fix falls back to a base this clone actually holds** (`@{u}`, then `origin/main`), which WIDENS the range &mdash; it can include commits already on origin &mdash; so the scan does more work, never less, and cannot miss a commit being sent. Fetching from inside a pre-push hook was rejected: slow, surprising, and it would make the gate depend on the network it exists to keep secrets off. It **says out loud** when it used a fallback base, because a scan over a range that is not exactly what ships must not read as one that was. The failure message now carries **git's own stderr** &mdash; the version that said only "failed" is what cost the diagnosis. **The sibling checks 1/2/3 were NOT affected, checked rather than assumed:** they read the same base through `outgoing_files()`, whose `git()` helper returns `''` on a non-zero exit instead of raising, so they already fell through. That is correct BY ACCIDENT and one edit away from being undone &mdash; making `git()` raise would silence every check on a raced push **with no message at all**, strictly worse than what was just fixed. The probe asserts it so that edit fails loudly. **Third wrong implementation of this check, and the first to fail in the ordinary case** rather than a contrived one; all three were found by driving it and missed by reasoning about it. | S |

| **Tooling** | A `PROBE` fixture commit from `tests/push_gate/check7_probe.py` can be stranded on HEAD and swept into the next ordinary push — **one reached `origin/main` and was the live tip** | **THE STRANDED-COMMIT HALF IS CLOSED 2026-09-10 (Fourth) — push-gate CHECK 8 refuses a PROBE-subject commit, `5959590c`, 11-assertion probe.** Both instances reverted (`86bf4b68`, and Cody's `c7295822`) | Fourth (check 8 done); **the probes still CREATE the commit — that half is open and needs a decision** | Whether to make the probes work on a detached HEAD, which would remove the hazard rather than catch it | **`b909dbee` "PROBE clean endpoint change" was not a change anybody made.** It is arm 1 of that probe — a one-line comment planted in `api/sb-auth.js` so the probe can assert a CLEAN change is not blocked by check 7. The probe pushes with `--dry-run` and unwinds with `git reset --mixed` in a `finally`, so **it cannot publish anything itself**; an interruption that skips the `finally` leaves the commit on HEAD, and the next ordinary `git push` from that clone carries it, at which point it looks like one more line of work. **FOUND BY HAVING THE SAME THING LOCALLY:** SAIRN-fourth opened 2026-09-09 ahead 1 on `PROBE undeclared public endpoint` (arm 3, `api/zz_probe_public.js`) with the file already deleted from the working tree, plus two duplicate probe fixtures left in `tools/reachability_exemptions.json` by `tests/reachability/live_mode_probe.py`. Mine was never pushed; origin's was. **Two instances of one failure, one caught and one not** — which is what makes it a pattern. **INERT, said plainly:** the content is a JavaScript comment, `api/sb-auth.js` is byte-identical to its pre-probe state after the revert, and `git log --grep '^PROBE'` finds exactly one such commit in the whole history, so this is the first occurrence. **The cheap mechanical fix is a push-gate check on the commit SUBJECT** (`^PROBE`), which is the house pattern — a rule that depends on remembering is the failure mode this repo keeps recording — but adding a gate check is a real change to a gate and wants its own review, which is why it was not bundled into the revert. Related and NOT the same: `cody` claimed `reachability-probe-restore-failure` on 2026-09-08, that claim has expired, and as of 2026-09-09 `SAIRN-cody`'s tree still carries `zz_reach_fixture.html` untracked plus modifications to `tools/run_all_tests.py` and `tests/suite_control_backfill_probe.py`. ~~**AND THE REACHABILITY RESIDUE RECURRED MID-SESSION 2026-09-09**~~ — **⚠ I RETRACT THAT, SAME SESSION, AND THE RETRACTION IS THE MORE USEFUL FINDING.** A fresh `zzDefinitelyNotAFinding` fixture did appear in `tools/reachability_exemptions.json` in this clone after the tree had been cleaned, and I recorded it as residue and ran `git checkout --` over it. **It was almost certainly a probe MID-RUN, not residue** — the post-push runner fires on every push, this session pushed four times, and every subsequent dirty file I watched (`sairnvet.html`, `zz_reach_fixture.html`, `api/legal-deadlines.js`) turned out to be a live probe that restored itself. So the cleanup may have clobbered a running probe, which is the same mistake in the opposite direction. **I have no evidence the reachability probe leaves residue at all, and the earlier version of this line asserted that it did.** **THE MECHANISM IS NOW PROVEN AND FIXED — Cody, 2026-09-09, `5b8570ce` + the follow-up.** It is the post-push runner, but not by interruption: it is CONCURRENCY. Nothing serialised the runs and `--hook` fires on every push, so **three concurrent `--hook` runs were observed alive in one tree** (09:05:38 / 09:06:06 / 09:06:21) plus two copies of `seam_check/run_probe.py` in the same second. Run A mutates; run B snapshots THE MUTATION as its "original"; A restores clean; B restores the mutation. **Both restored exactly what they read** — which is why it was never reproducible from a sequential run and why nothing ever went red. Fixed with a per-clone lock plus a per-probe refusal to snapshot an already-dirty target, now on **all seven** mutating probes, held by `tests/run_suite_lock_probe.py` (40 checks, driven in a throwaway `git worktree`). **The stranded-commit half of this row is still open and still yours** — a lock narrows that window without closing it, and a commit is residue no per-file restore can undo. **And the mid-run-vs-residue warning above is right and was earned twice:** `api/sd-data.js` turned up modified with `warn_days: gateWarn` deleted (delegation-probe arm 1) with the probe process already gone, so that one WAS stranded — checking for a live process is what tells the two apart. **⚠ AND DO NOT CLEAN A DIRTY TREE ON SIGHT, which is what this row would otherwise have you do.** Minutes later `sairnvet.html` showed a planted defect — the corruption branch of `st()` replaced with a bare `catch(err){}` — and it was **not residue, it was a probe MID-RUN**: `tests/suite_control_backfill_probe.py`, running under the post-push runner, which restored the file itself on the way out. `git checkout --` over it would have corrupted a live probe. **During a run, a mutated tracked file is indistinguishable from residue after one** — check for a running python process before deciding which you are looking at, which is precisely why `run_all_tests.py` snapshots `git status` before and after rather than judging the tree in the middle.<br><br>**— IT HAPPENED A SECOND TIME THE NEXT MORNING, AND THAT ONE WAS NOT INERT. —** `fab44663 "PROBE seam violation"` — check4_probe's arm 2 — reached `origin/main` at 09:23 on 2026-09-10 and deployed. It deletes `service_methods: body.service_methods` from `api/legal-deadlines.js`, **the field whose absence had already made SAIRNlaw run Florida five days late once**. A dropped field there does not throw: it arrives `undefined`, the engine takes its default branch, and the answer looks entirely reasonable while being wrong by days on a court deadline. **`api/_lib/deadline-endpoint-inputs.test.js` — named three lines above the deleted line as "the guard against a third instance" — failed exactly as designed** (`expected [], actual ["service_methods"]`), and the suite is wired REPORT-ONLY after a push, so it observed the breakage and could not stop it. Reverted by Cody in `c7295822`, **found because check 4 DENIED THEIR UNRELATED PUSH naming the field** — the gate catching it after it shipped, from the wrong side, as a refusal that reads like a false positive against work with nothing to do with it.<br><br>**MY OWN CONTRIBUTION TO THE EXPOSURE, said plainly:** repairing check4_probe's twelve-day silent SKIP on 2026-09-09 is what made it actually run, and running it is what strands this commit. The repair was right. Shipping it without the gate was not — I filed the gate as this row instead of building it, and it cost a live legal-deadline input the next morning.<br><br>**CHECK 8 CLOSES THE DOOR, 2026-09-10 (`5959590c`).** A push carrying a commit whose subject starts with `PROBE` is DENIED, in both hook modes, with the sha and subject named. **A dry run is exempt and that is correctness, not convenience** — it publishes nothing, and check4_probe and check7_probe each have an arm whose whole point is that a CLEAN change is ALLOWED through; denying those would leave the arm unable to tell *"check N allowed it"* from *"everything is denied"*. PreToolUse reads `--dry-run`/`-n` from the command text; **git hands a pre-push hook no dry-run signal at all** (verified, not assumed), so the two probes declare themselves with `SAIRN_PROBE_PUSH=1`, which suppresses **check 8 only** and is set nowhere else. **Its position is load-bearing:** immediately before check 1, so checks 2-7 still answer first and a planted seam violation is still denied BY CHECK 4 naming the field. The first version sat after check 1 and never ran at all — check 1 exits early on `if not apps` for any push touching no seed file, which is nearly every push. **Caught by the probe, not by reading.** `tests/push_gate/check8_probe.py`, 11 assertions, control first, all three exemptions proven against the same planted commit.<br><br>**WHAT CHECK 8 DOES NOT DO, so this row is not read as more than it is: it catches the commit at the door, it does not stop the probes creating it.** Making them work on a detached HEAD would remove the hazard instead; a detached HEAD left behind by a probe that dies is its own confusion, so that trade wants deciding rather than assuming | S |

| **Platform** | `api/sd-data.js` now makes an UNAUTHENTICATED database round trip on every junk-token request, and has no rate limit | ⚠ **BUILT AND MEASURED INERT 2026-09-05 (Fourth) — STILL OPEN, and the built limiter does NOT work.** Now in observe mode so it cannot refuse anyone | **Michael** — the fix belongs at the platform layer, not in this code | Decide: a Vercel Firewall rate-limit rule, or accept the amplification and delete the limiter — **and note 2026-09-05 that this now covers SIXTEEN endpoints, not one** | Before the reorder a junk token with a garbage resource was refused locally, for free. Now every such request costs one `license_keys` SELECT (`api/_lib/license.js:70-76`) before anything else, including the 413 size check. `grep` for `rateLimit\|rate_limit\|throttle` in that file returns nothing. The commit disclosed this as a latency and billing cost; the reviewer's framing is the one that matters — **a previously-free anonymous path became a database-amplification path.** Not a reason to revert: the trade is right and the alternative was the enumeration hole. It is a reason for a limiter, and `api/_lib/ai-rate-limit.js` was the shape suggested. **BUILT 2026-09-05, AND DELIBERATELY NOT IN THAT SHAPE — the deviation was raised with Michael before building and approved.** `ai-rate-limit.js` is Supabase-backed, one RPC per call, so putting it in front of this path would make every junk-token request cost TWO database round trips instead of one: it would measurably worsen the exact problem it was brought in to solve. `api/_lib/anon-rate-limit.js` is in-memory and per-instance, runs BEFORE `validateLicenseKey`, and costs nothing. **THE LOAD-BEARING PROPERTY IS THAT IT CAN NEVER REFUSE A WORKING CUSTOMER:** only a FAILED validation is recorded, so a valid key accumulates no count at any volume from any address — which is why this enforces by default where `ai-rate-limit.js` observes by default, since a refusal here can only land on a request that was already going to be refused one status earlier. An inactive licence (403), an upstream failure (502) and a missing bearer (no lookup happened) are all uncounted, each asserted. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO, stated in its own header rather than left to be discovered:** per-instance and reset by a cold start, so a spread flood is capped per instance and not globally; the address is `x-vercel-forwarded-for` first, which the platform sets, but a caller that can vary the address it presents rotates past it. Every degradation ends in "allowed", so the worst case is yesterday's behaviour and never a refused real request — it reduces the amplification factor of the ordinary case and is not a defence against a determined distributed attacker. The map is bounded at 5000 addresses, because a limiter that can be made to exhaust an instance's memory is a denial of service wearing a fix's clothes; its own probe caught that the first version settled at 5001 by pruning before the insert. **AND THEN PRODUCTION SAID IT DOES NOT WORK, which is the part that matters.** Live: 40 concurrent junk-token requests returned 40x 401 and NOT ONE 429, against a limit of 20 in 60 seconds. Nothing logged could say why, so the first fix was INSTRUMENTATION rather than a repair — a limiter you cannot observe is indistinguishable from one that does not run, this platform's most-repeated defect class, shipped by the session that keeps recording it. The instrumented burst settled it: every line read `addr_src=x-vercel-forwarded-for tracked=1`, so the address IS read and each instance tracked exactly one — and the counts climbed in a dozen PARALLEL sequences, the busiest reaching 10 of 20. **HORIZONTAL SCALE-OUT DEFEATS A PER-INSTANCE COUNTER IN PROPORTION TO THE ATTACK:** concurrency is what makes Vercel add instances, so a bigger flood spreads wider and every individual count gets LOWER. It fails hardest against exactly the case it was written for, and a slow sequential attacker cannot trip it either (~3.3s per request measured; 20 failures do not fit in 60 seconds). Its only realistic effect while enforcing was a rare FALSE refusal of a real customer behind a shared address who mistyped a key — all cost, no benefit — so **the default is now OBSERVE and it can refuse nobody**, held by two assertions. The counting and instrumentation are kept because the instrumentation is now the only thing measuring the amplification. **The author's argument for the design was half right and the conclusion was wrong:** a Supabase-backed limiter really would cost two round trips instead of one, but the answer was never an in-process counter on a platform that runs many processes. **THE DECISION, for Michael:** a Vercel Firewall rate-limit rule — the right layer, sees every instance, costs no database round trip — or accept the amplification and DELETE the limiter rather than leave dormant code that reads like a defence | M |

Severity HIGH by exploitability** — no credential, no skill, publicly documented URL, and the loss is direct spend on a live API key rather than data.<br><br>**WHY IT IS A DECISION AND NOT A FIX I SHOULD JUST MAKE: all 17 apps call this proxy with `headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' }` and nothing else.** Requiring a bearer licence key breaks every one of them until each is updated, and some AI surfaces may be reachable before a licence is entered. The cheaper half — **capping `max_tokens` server-side, and moving the limiter outside the `is_demo` branch so a client cannot opt out of it** — changes no caller and could ship immediately.<br><br>**THE OTHER 25 WERE READ AND ARE NOT THIS.** `api/sd-agent.js` is properly gated via `store.validateAndGate(licenseKey)` — a THIRD instance of the detector's bespoke-boundary limitation, not a defect. `public-book.js` is public by design and its rate limiter fails CLOSED. | **Platform** | **INTERIM FIX SHIPPED: `max_tokens` capped server-side, and the rate limiter taken out of the caller's hands** | **Live and verified on production 2026-09-05 (Cody) in `8fe970a9`.** ⚠ **NOT CLOSED — awaiting INDEPENDENT REVIEW**, per the standing rule for a live financial exposure | Cody (fix) / **unassigned (the review)** | — | Non-breaking; no caller changed.<br><br>**1. `max_tokens` reached Anthropic UNMODIFIED.** Now clamped to **4096** in `callAnthropic()`. **Measured, not guessed:** the largest any app's client sends is 2000, and `api/sd-agent.js` — the most demanding AI path here — sets its own `MAX_TOKENS` to 4096, so nothing legitimate is refused. **Clamped rather than refused**, so a large-but-reasonable request gets a shorter answer instead of an error. **The cap is in `callAnthropic()`, NOT the HTTP handler, and that placement is the point** — `api/law-auth.js:596` and `api/sc-ai.js:259` both pass a client-supplied `body.max_tokens` through the same function, so capping only the handler would have fixed the copy that is easiest to see and left two others open. **LIVE-VERIFIED:** a production request asking for 64000 returned `stop_reason: max_tokens` with `output_tokens: 4096`.<br><br>**2. The limiter lived inside `if (is_demo)`, and `is_demo` comes from the request body.** The one persistent cost control could be switched off by the caller. **A control a client can opt out of is not a control.** It now runs on every request. **Stated plainly because it would otherwise read as more than it is: the limiter is in OBSERVE mode and fails open, so this refuses nothing today** — what it does is make the control reachable on every path so enforcement cannot later be bypassed by a flag the caller sets. It also closes the file's own *honest scope* gap: non-demo calls were absent from the usage table entirely. **The demo contract is preserved exactly** (`{error:'demo_limit'}` at 200, which 17 apps parse); a refused non-demo call gets a real 429, because telling a paying SAIRNcash subscriber they hit a *demo* limit would be false.<br><br>**MY OWN FIRST VERSION OF THE CAP HAD THE COERCION BUG THIS PLATFORM ALREADY DOCUMENTS.** `Number(true)` is 1, so `max_tokens: true` produced a **one-token ceiling** that would have truncated a real answer to nothing. Found by probing the function rather than reading it. It is verbatim the trap `api/_lib/dental-ledger.js`'s `isPositiveMoney()` records — the lesson existed here and I repeated the bug anyway.<br><br>**22 assertions, negative-controlled both ways** — removing the cap turns 4 red, putting the limiter back inside `is_demo` turns 3 red — and the file was restored **byte-identical afterwards, sha256 compared rather than assumed**, which is the correction to my own earlier restore that silently did not happen | S |

The endpoint reads `Authorization`, validates the key when present, and logs the state per `app_id`. **That log is the point: Phase 4's gate is measured traffic showing every live app authenticated, NOT a checklist of files edited** — a file checklist cannot see the call site nobody found. **Verified live:** a call with no header and a call with a junk key both returned 200. `absent` and `invalid` are recorded as **different** states because absent may be a surface with no licence to send, which needs a different answer than a bug; `inactive` is a third state and is deliberately **not** refused even in enforce mode, since that is a billing question the app's own gate owns. **Fails OPEN on an unreachable licence store, in enforce mode too** — refusing on our own outage punishes the customer for it.<br><br>**DECISION (a) — SCALE. It `require`s `api/sd-data.js` and executes at module load. **The response body discloses nothing**, so this is not a data leak; it is twenty-plus anonymous code-execution and compute surfaces that exist purely by accident of naming. Files under `api/_lib/` and `api/_resources/` are protected by the leading underscore and are NOT affected — only the ones sitting directly in `api/`. **THE COUNT IN THE FIRST DRAFT OF THIS ROW WAS WRONG AND WAS CORRECTED BEFORE IT SHIPPED: 36, not 25.** The first pass counted only the files sitting directly in `api/`. `api/sairncash/` (3) and `api/sairndental/` (8) carry no leading underscore either, so they route identically — probed rather than reasoned about: `/api/sairndental/public-book.test` and `/api/sairncash/verify.test` both return 500, `/api/_lib/auth.test` returns 404. One of the newly-counted files is `api/sairncash/stripe-webhook.test.js`. **Fixed with a single `*.test.js` line in `.vercelignore`**, deliberately not scoped to `api/`: nothing outside a test runner needs a `*.test.js` on the server, and one rule is simpler than two. Verified first that no non-test file anywhere in the repo requires one, so nothing that ships can lose a dependency. The same commit annotates the rest of `.vercelignore`, which is residue from when the home directory was itself a checkout — `Documents/`, `AppData/`, `.bash_history` and the rest name nothing that exists in a real clone. Left in place rather than deleted (a deployment-config change with nothing to verify it against), but annotated so the next reader does not take them for evidence | M |

If it has not been applied, the app now accepts ~1.27 MB while the database still refuses at 64 KB — so every photo booking still fails, and the app-side guard supplies false confidence that it will not. **Settle it read-only, no debris:** `select conname, pg_get_constraintdef(oid), convalidated from pg_constraint where conrelid='public.dnt_appointments'::regclass and conname='dntap_data_size';` — expect `<= 1291059` and `convalidated = true`. **Not covered by any gate:** push-gate check 3 compares tables and columns against `db/schema_snapshot.json` and does not look at CHECK constraints at all, so constraint drift is invisible to it. | **SAIRNdental** | **No BAA exists, and a service agreement cannot substitute for one** — patient data makes SAIRN a HIPAA business associate the moment a real practice connects | **Open — blocks signing, not just shipping** 🚨 | **Michael** | Needs counsel, not a template | **Raised 2026-08-26 (Cody) while per-app service-agreement drafting was requested for the real dentist prospect.** A dental practice handling patient names, appointments and treatment records is a **covered entity** under HIPAA; SAIRN, by storing and processing that data on their behalf, is a **business associate**. 45 CFR 164.502(e) and 164.308(b) require a written **Business Associate Agreement** before the covered entity may disclose PHI to it. **The specific error this row exists to prevent:** a service agreement containing "HIPAA-trigger language" is **not** a BAA and does not satisfy the requirement. They are separate instruments with different required contents — a BAA must specify permitted uses, safeguards, subcontractor flow-down, breach notification, and return-or-destruction on termination. Drafting a good service agreement and believing the HIPAA question is handled is the failure mode. **Real, not theoretical:** `dnt_patients`, `dnt_appointments`, `dnt_charges` and `dnt_claim`-family tables are live and provisioned (confirmed in the 2026-08-26 provisioning sweep), so the storage this obligation attaches to already exists. **Same trigger already flagged for SAIRNcare and SAIRNvet** — treat as one platform-level piece of counsel work covering all three, not three separate efforts, the same way the trademark screen is one question and not thirteen. **UPDATE 2026-08-27 — the safeguards those documents describe now exist.** When this row was written, the drafts described role-based access and per-employee authentication that SAIRNdental **did not have**: its auth was `DEFAULT_PINS={owner:'1234',frontdesk:'2345',provider:'3456'}` compared in the browser, and all 13 `dnt_*` branches in `api/sd-data.js` ran with zero session checks — patient names and dates of birth were readable with a licence key alone, proven live. Fixed and live-verified the same day (`0248298`, `05bc516`): `sairndental_employee_auth` provisioned, `api/dnt-auth.js` shipping all seven actions, every dental branch session-gated, and the exact call that proved the gap now returning `401 NO_SESSION`. 26/26 live checks including bootstrap trapdoor, lockout, and the full deactivation lifecycle. **So the agreement and the DPA amendment now match what is deployed** — that dependency is cleared and should not block counsel any longer. **One caveat counsel should still see:** minimum-necessary tiering is deliberately **deferred** — all three roles can currently reach all patient data. That is stated as an open item in `api/dnt-auth.js`'s role header. It is a far narrower gap than no authentication at all, but a BAA reviewer may want it closed before signature. **Next action, unchanged in substance:** counsel, before any SAIRNdental agreement is signed or any real patient data is accepted. This is not a drafting task and no template in this repo can close it | M |

The only surfacing is a `console.warn` &mdash; **nothing reaches the user**. Worse, the toast still says *"no cross-device delivery yet"* on 2026-08-09 honest-language grounds that `sv_peerconsults` is localStorage-only &mdash; **that comment is now STALE**, so the feature states two contradictory truths. **(2) Hydration is additive-only**, so a CORRECTED dosing entry never reaches a second workstation; no disclosure exists anywhere in the file. That is exactly what `svSeedStore`'s `finally` exists to prevent, six lines above it | S |

Minting a unique number, syncing the counter, or prefixing per device all invent a uniqueness the paper does not have and change what is printed on a cheque. So the **number is untouched** &mdash; ordinary data, still what the user sees &mdash; and the **server key becomes a minted `CHK-<uuid>`** (randomUUID, with a fallback that is deliberately not `Date.now()` alone, which is what the sibling savers use and which collides across two devices in one millisecond). The on-show loader went into the **shadowed** `showPage` first and was moved to the one assigned to `window.showPage`; that copy now carries a note saying so. **SQL: the table did not change** &mdash; `check_id text` with `unique (license_hash, check_id)` was already right for a minted id, only what the client puts in it changed; both comments claiming it holds the cheque number are corrected. Held by `tests/mech_check_register_identity.js`, **20 arms, all passing**, driving the real `saveCheck`/`renderCR`/`loadCR` out of the HTML &mdash; arm 2 replays the OLD key against the same two saves and asserts the two devices really did collapse to one identity, arm 4 asserts a register with no repeat stays silent. Live-verified on `sairn.vercel.app/sairnmechanical` after deploy. | M |

| **SAIRNdental** | ~~`dnt_settings_obj` has FOUR writers under two backing variables — the same shape StoneDesk had, unchecked~~ | **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (CC)** — the collision is benign and now ACKNOWLEDGED with the trace; the real defect found in the same read was NOT the collision, and is fixed | CC | — | **Fourth reported it and left it because CC held sairndental. Traced by hand, as the row asked.** **THE COLLISION IS BENIGN AND WAS DESIGNED THAT WAY.** The three `rec` writers — `saveBookingSettings()`, `dnPersistWindows()`, `saveGfeIdentity()` — are one shape, not three: each does `Object.assign({}, <base>, {only its own keys})`, so no two write a different schema into the key. `dntSyncFromServer()` writes `serverSettings`, the server row, replacing wholesale, which all three rely on and say so in comments. Entered in `tools/key_collision_check.py`'s ACKNOWLEDGED map with that reason and the exact variable pair, so a THIRD writer or a rename re-reports it. `key_collision_check.py sairndental.html` is now **CLEAN**. **THE REAL DEFECT WAS THE MERGE BASE**, which is not a collision — the same distinction the `stonedesk:ai_memories` entry already records for its own key. The base was `settings()`, **this device's cached copy**, and `api/sd-data.js` stores `data: payload`, replacing the whole blob. So: workstation B saves an appeal window (server has `[X, Y]`); workstation A, last synced before that, saves the practice identity, merges onto its stale copy, sends `[X]`, and **Y is erased on the server** — after which B's next sync loses it. This row is explicitly practice-wide configuration a second workstation must see; the per-device merge protected panel-vs-panel and nothing else. **The base is now a fresh SERVER read**, and a read that FAILS returns null so the caller refuses rather than saving on top of a base it could not check. A device with no licence key keeps its local-only save — it is not talking to a server, so there is nothing to clobber. **AND A SECOND LIVE DEFECT IN THE SAME FUNCTION:** `saveBookingSettings()` wrote to localStorage BEFORE the request, and its 409 `SLUG_TAKEN` branch only toasted and returned — so the device kept a booking slug the server had **refused**, and the form and the patient-feedback link preview advertised a slug belonging to **another practice**. All three panels now write server-first through one shared writer, which also means the other two handle `SLUG_TAKEN` instead of reporting it as a generic failure. The stale *"server sync not yet enabled for this app"* message — on every failure path of all three — is gone; sync is enabled and that string hid the real reason. **21 assertions in `tests/sairndental_settings_merge_base.js`, driving the two extracted helpers rather than reimplementing them, and three mutation probes: reverting the base to `settings()`, moving the local write back before the server call, and treating a failed read as empty. Live-verified in both directions: six new markers present on the deployed page and both old-basis markers absent. **One of my own first-draft assertions was wrong and is recorded rather than quietly corrected:** I expected 500 committed + 400 actual against a 1000 budget to be *warning*; it is 90%, so critical, and the code was right. The lockout protection is the last-admin refusal, not a softer bootstrap | M |
| **Platform** | ~~**A live licence is API-unrecoverable: RF-PINNACLE-2026 has ZERO active owners, and all three recovery routes are closed**~~ **RECOVERED — re-verified against the LIVE roster 2026-08-29 (Hank), and this row was stale.** `roster` on `RF-PINNACLE-2026` now returns 5 rows and **TWO active owners**: `rf-verify-admin` (**role `owner`, not the `admin` its own seed file still says** — someone applied exactly the promotion this row proposed as recovery action 1) and `rf-verify-owner` (**`active: true`**, where this row recorded it `active: false`). `rf-verify-ui` remains `owner`/inactive; the two foremen are active. Read with a real signed session, not inferred. **`sql/sairnroofing_access_panel_verify_cleanup_2026-08-28.sql` HAS NOT BEEN RUN** — it deletes all five disposable accounts and every one of them is still live, so the recovery currently rests on a credential a cleanup script is queued to delete. Running it as written would recreate the exact zero-owner trapdoor this row describes. **The trapdoor ANALYSIS below is unaffected and still open** — it is about the missing recovery PATH across four apps, not about this one licence's state | **Recovered; the PATTERN row below stays open** — re-verified 2026-08-29 | unassigned | Needs one SQL statement; the API cannot fix this by design | **This is the bootstrap trapdoor landing in production, reached by ordinary verification work rather than by anything unusual.** Roster on `RF-PINNACLE-2026` has 5 rows and **zero active owners** — `rf-verify-owner` and `rf-verify-ui` are both `owner` with `active:false`, left that way by earlier Phase 4b and browser-check passes. The only active management account is `rf-verify-admin`, and SAIRNroofing's `PROVISIONING_ROLES` is `['owner']` only. **All three exits proven closed live 2026-08-28:** reactivate an owner → 403 `FORBIDDEN`; provision a new owner via `setup` → 403 `FORBIDDEN`; `bootstrap` → 409 `ALREADY_PROVISIONED`, because the existence probe deliberately does NOT filter on `active`. **THE DESIGN IS CORRECT AND THIS IS ITS COST, not a bug to fix by softening the probe** — making `bootstrap` fall back to "no ACTIVE rows" would auto-heal this and would also let anyone holding the licence key deactivate their way to a fresh owner account and seize the company. Recorded in `sairn-employee-auth-scaffold` §5 and in `api/sd-auth.js:304-308`, which names this as how SD-AUDIT-2026 was lost. **What makes it worth a row:** it is no longer hypothetical, it is sitting on a live demo licence, and the same shape can reach a paying customer. **Two separate actions.** (1) Recover this licence — one `update ... set active = true` on an owner row, or promote `rf-verify-admin` (whose PIN 481502 is already documented in `sql/sairnroofing_verify_admin_seed.sql`) to `owner`, which needs no new secret anywhere. `rf-verify-owner`'s own PIN was set live during Phase 1-2 and deliberately never recorded, so it is not loggable-in. (2) Decide whether any app should ship a guard that makes this unreachable — the Access panel added in `ce8193e` would have PREVENTED it by surfacing the server's `LAST_OWNER` refusal, but nothing stops the same state being created through the API directly<br><br>**NOT A ONE-OFF — CONFIRMED AS A PATTERN ACROSS FOUR OF THE FIVE APPS, 2026-08-28.** CC raised this while auditing tonight's auth builds and I verified it independently against the source rather than accepting it: `PROVISIONING_ROLES` is a SINGLE role in **SAIRNdental** `['owner']`, **SAIRNmechanical** `['owner']`, **SAIRNroofing** `['owner']` and **SAIRNcode** `['admin']`. Only **StoneDesk** has two, `['owner','admin']`. For all four single-role apps the `setup` gate is bound to that *same* role, so there is genuinely no second path back — RF-PINNACLE-2026 is simply the one where it has already fired. **ONE REFINEMENT TO CC'S FRAMING, worth stating precisely: StoneDesk's exposure is REDUCED, not absent.** Two provisioning roles mean an `admin` can restore a deactivated `owner` and vice versa, so it takes two deactivations rather than one — but if the last active `owner` AND the last active `admin` both go inactive, StoneDesk is locked out identically. The guard question is therefore "how many provisioning-role holders must remain active", not "does this app have two roles". **ONE MORE THING THE COUNT HIDES, verified from source 2026-08-28 (Hank): THE EXISTING GUARD IS PRESENT IN ALL FIVE AND IS PREVENTIVE-ONLY.** Every one of `dnt`, `mech`, `rf`, `sc` and `sd` already carries the last-one refusal — an `activeOwners`/`activeAdmins` `.length <= 1` check before allowing a deactivation. So the exposure is **not a missing guard, it is a missing RECOVERY PATH**: the refusal stops the last active provisioner being deactivated *through* `set_active`, and can do nothing about one that is already inactive — which is exactly how RF-PINNACLE-2026 got here, via verification passes that left rows `active:false` rather than via a refused call. **Consequence for the fix: a harder guard on the deactivation path would not have prevented this and will not prevent the next one.** The two halves are separable and only the second is unsolved — widening the pool (StoneDesk demonstrates it) versus providing a way back (nothing demonstrates it). **AND THE CONCRETE TRAP FOR WHOEVER BUILDS IT: SAIRNcode’s single role is `admin`, not `owner`.** Four of the five say `owner`; `sc-auth.js` says `admin`. A recovery path that names a role instead of reading `PROVISIONING_ROLES` misses SAIRNcode **silently** — the app answers normally right up until someone needs to recover. Scope note: exactly **5 of 16** auth files implement `set_active` at all; the other eleven have neither it nor a `PROVISIONING_ROLES` constant, so they are out of scope rather than unaudited. **This is the input to the harder-guard decision** (action 2 above), and it changes its scope: a fix aimed at one app would leave three with the same exposure. Candidate shapes, none chosen: refuse the deactivation that would take active provisioning-role holders to zero (the `LAST_OWNER` guard already exists but is unreachable by construction — see `sairn-employee-auth-scaffold` §7); or add a break-glass path; or widen `PROVISIONING_ROLES` per app, which is a real authorisation change and not a safety patch | M |
| **SAIRNroofing** | `set_active` and `set_certifications` parse a PATCH body their own helper never asked for — probable false 502 AFTER a successful write | **CLOSED 2026-08-28 — fixed by another session in `05bc516`, and independently re-confirmed live by me.** The row was raised 2026-08-27 as NOT live-verified ⚠️ | unassigned | — | **Code-shape finding with a probable runtime consequence, reported as that and not as a confirmed live failure.** `patchEmployee()` at `api/rf-auth.js:174-178` sends `headers` (`api/rf-auth.js:161` — apikey, Authorization, Content-Type) with **no `Prefer` header**. PostgREST answers a PATCH without `Prefer: return=representation` with **204 No Content and an empty body**. Both call sites then parse unconditionally: `const patched = await patchR.json();` at `api/rf-auth.js:482` (`set_active`) and `:393` (`set_certifications`). `.json()` on an empty body rejects, the outer catch at `:498-506` turns it into a 502 `'Upstream error — try again'` — **after the deactivation has already been written**. That is a silent-failure inversion: the action succeeds and reports failure, so an owner deactivating someone sees an error and may retry or assume it did not work. Contrast `api/sc-auth.js:407-411` and `api/sd-auth.js:401-405`, which explicitly set `Prefer: 'return=representation'`, and `api/leg-auth.js:297` / `api/law-auth.js:399,424,439`, which only parse on the failure path. **`recordFailure`/`clearFailures` use the same helper and are unaffected** — they never call `.json()`. **Next action: reproduce live against `sairn.vercel.app` before fixing** — the fix is one header or one `if (!patchR.ok)` guard, but the whole point of this row is that nobody has actually seen it fail yet. Not folded into the skill work, hence a row<br><br>**RESOLVED, and the resolution came from two directions.** `05bc516` ("set_active returned 502 on a mutation that had already landed") applied exactly the predicted fix at BOTH call sites — `api/rf-auth.js:489` for `set_active` and `:394` for `set_certifications` — parsing the PATCH body only on the error path (`if (!patchR.ok) { ... }`) rather than unconditionally, and its comment records it as proven live 2026-08-27 with both deactivate and reactivate returning 502 while the row changed correctly underneath. **That is the failure mode this row predicted from code shape alone and explicitly declined to assert without reproduction — the caution was right and so was the diagnosis.** Independently re-confirmed 2026-08-28 during the Access-panel round trip: eight consecutive `set_active` calls (deactivate, idempotent repeat, second-owner deactivate, two reactivates, and a probe pair) all returned HTTP 200 with a full JSON body and correct `remaining_owners`. No 502, and the underlying rows matched the responses on a follow-up `roster` read. Nothing further to do | S |

Even the narrower alternative under consideration removes exactly § 552.109 and § 552.6(b). **WHAT THIS MEANS FOR CODE:** current law is safe to encode and must NOT be a constant — exemption treatment wants a dated, configurable rule. **NEXT ACTION: re-check RIN 1235-AA51 and the FR Part 552 index before any release after October 2026.** Nothing to build now. | **Platform** | ~~The seed push gate reads seed files from the **working tree**, not from the commits being pushed~~ | **FIXED 2026-09-03 (Hank)** — 38/38 probe, 9/9 negative controls | Hank | — | `tools/sairn_push_gate_hook.py` calls `tools/sairn_load_state_check.py`, which reads `sql/*.json` **off disk**. So the drift it reports describes HEAD's working tree, not the refspec. **Reproduced, not theorised:** pushing `b351b49` — an engine-only commit touching zero seed files — was DENIED naming 13 missing New Hampshire rules, because the *later* seed commit was sitting in HEAD. This is the exact ordering the gate doc itself prescribes (deploy the computation standard, then load, then push the seed), so **the gate currently denies the workflow it documents.** Fix shape, not chosen: derive the seed set from `git diff` over the pushed range rather than from disk — but note that the disk read is also what makes the check honest about what will be live, so the two readings need reconciling rather than one replacing the other. **Workaround that is not a bypass, and should be preferred to the override:** check out the engine commit detached and push from there — at that commit no seed exists on disk, live genuinely matches repo, and the gate passes on its own terms | S |
| **Platform** | ~~`SAIRN_SEED_GATE=off`, the gate's own documented override, is **unreachable from any Bash tool call**~~ | **FIXED 2026-09-03 (Hank)** — the inline prefix now works; a quoted mention still does not | Hank | — | `tools/sairn_push_gate_hook.py:287` reads `os.environ.get('SAIRN_SEED_GATE')` — the hook runs as a PreToolUse hook inside **Claude Code's** process, so it inherits Claude Code's environment, not the shell's. Prefixing `SAIRN_SEED_GATE=off git push ...` sets the variable in a child shell the hook never sees; it was tried and the push was denied identically. The override therefore only works if it is set in the environment Claude Code itself was launched with, which is not what CLAUDE.md's push protocol describes and not what the denial message tells the reader to do. **Consequence worth stating: the escape hatch every other gate here relies on does not exist for this one in practice**, so a session that hits a false denial has no documented way out. Fix shape, not chosen: read the override out of the hook's `tool_input.command` string (the same payload it already parses for the push refspec) so an inline prefix works, and correct the denial message either way. Related to the row above — they were found together and the working-tree bug is what makes this one bite | S |
| **SAIRNlaw** | **DECISION: where an EARLY-direction omission gets disclosed — `JURISDICTION_COVERAGE` or the rule row's own note. Two seeded jurisdictions answer it one way and four answer it the other, and nothing says which is right** | **✅ DECIDED BY MICHAEL AND IMPLEMENTED 2026-09-01 (`a54fae9`), live-verified.** **Decision: `JURISDICTION_COVERAGE` is the contract** — it is the channel the majority already used and the one the audit proved reliably carries the disclosure. Utah and Nevada are migrated onto it; their suites' assertions of the ABSENCE are replaced by assertions of the entry. **Second decision, same day: a LATE-direction disclosure is its own category.** Alabama moves from `direction: 'early'` to `'late'` and gains a structured `late_exposure` block (authority / summary / why_not_refused / caller_action), and the table's header now documents **DISCLOSED-LATE** as a deliberate third category beside REFUSED and DISCLOSED. `direction` is **worst-case, not typical**, and says so — Alabama's other two gaps are EARLY, but a caller switching on the field is asking *can this be late*. **A SECOND ONE CANNOT ARRIVE QUIETLY:** `coverageTableDefects()` runs over the table at module load and throws on `'late'` without the block, the block without `'late'`, a direction outside early/late, or a missing required key — exported so the invariant and its test are the same code. `deadline-coverage-contract.test.js` carries 25 assertions including that a REAL computation for `ut`, `nv` and `al` returns the disclosure. **Live: Utah and Nevada now return `coverage.direction: 'early'` where they previously returned none, and Alabama returns `'late'` with the block.** 167 tests pass | **Michael** | — | **THE TENSION IS REAL AND IS BETWEEN TWO DELIBERATE POSITIONS, not between a rule and an oversight.** Utah's and Nevada's gate docs both say their omissions are *disclosable* — Utah §8 is headed "Not modelled, all EARLY, all disclosable", Nevada's says "Safe, disclosable" — and both seeds then declare NO `JURISDICTION_COVERAGE` entry, asserted verbatim by `deadline-utah.test.js:232` and `deadline-nevada.test.js:222` as *"its gaps are all EARLY and row-level"*. Alabama, Maryland, Missouri and Wisconsin took the opposite view and carry jurisdiction-level entries for omissions of the same EARLY class. **WHAT I CHECKED AND WHAT IT SHOWS:** "row-level" is a weaker claim than the assertion's wording implies — only **2 of Utah's 9 rows and 2 of Nevada's 10** carry any omission-flavoured `authority.note`, and the ones sampled explain rule STRUCTURE (which limb applies, why a row is separate) rather than disclosing the clerk-inaccessibility, hours-unit or filing-cutoff omissions the gate docs named. So a caller computing a Utah date is told nothing about them through either channel. **WHY THIS IS A DECISION AND NOT A FIX, and why I reverted rather than shipped:** adding the two entries is three minutes of work and it silently overrides a choice another session made and locked; it broke 16 suites, which is the mechanism working as intended. **The question for Michael is which channel is the contract** — if `coverage` is what a caller reads, Utah and Nevada need entries and their tests need updating; if the row note is, then four jurisdictions are over-disclosing and the gate docs should stop saying "disclosable" when they mean something else. **No date is wrong either way — every omission at issue runs EARLY** | S |
| **Tooling** | ~~The post-push deploy hook compares live against **LOCAL HEAD**~~ — **FIXED, and then fixed again for a second cause** | **CLOSED 2026-09-02 (Cody).** `63d67ad` changed the baseline to `origin/main`, fetched **after** the 60s wait so a push landing from another clone during the wait moves the baseline instead of registering as drift, and added a line saying whether THIS clone is in step — "deploy stuck" and "your clone is stale" need opposite responses. Verified against a real stale-HEAD scenario rather than a live check, because with everything in sync both baselines agree and the test discriminates nothing: with HEAD moved back one stonedesk commit, the OLD baseline reports drift and the NEW one still agrees with live. **Then it false-alarmed again** on 2026-09-02 and the second cause was different: it fired on a push containing only `sql/`, `tools/` and `tests/` while ANOTHER clone's real `stonedesk.html` change was mid-deploy — real drift belonging to someone else's push, attributed to one that could not have caused it. `0a17486` scopes it to pushes that actually shipped `stonedesk.html`, using `origin/main@{1}..origin/main`, a range deliberately WIDER than "what I just pushed" so it can only suppress a check that could not concern that file. **Both fixes were found by the diagnostic line the first fix added**, which is the argument for hooks that explain themselves. Original status: Open — **fired twice on 2026-09-01 (Hank**), both false** | unassigned | — | `tools/deploy_verify_notify.py` hashes `sairn.vercel.app/stonedesk` against `HEAD:stonedesk.html` ~60s after a push and reports a possible stuck Vercel webhook. **Both firings today were a RACE, not a stuck webhook**, and neither push touched `stonedesk.html`: live matched **`origin/main`** byte-for-byte on each occasion while local HEAD was one commit behind, because another session had pushed StoneDesk work seconds later. **Its recommended remedy is actively wrong in this case** — it advises pushing a trivial re-trigger commit, which would add a no-op commit to a shared branch to fix nothing. **In a four-clone setup a local HEAD is stale by default**, so this will keep firing. Fix shape, not chosen: fetch and compare against `origin/main` rather than `HEAD`, and say which ref was used in the message. **Diagnose it by comparing the three hashes** — `HEAD:stonedesk.html`, `origin/main:stonedesk.html` and live — before believing it; if live equals origin/main the deploy is fine | S |
| **SAIRNlaw** | `service_extension.order` is carried on 135 seeded rows in four different vocabularies and is **read by nothing** | **DECIDED BY MICHAEL AND DONE 2026-09-02 (`f0723f1`), reloaded and live-verified.** **Decision: DELETE the field platform-wide rather than build a validator for it** — the existing values are self-contradictory across rows, the same label sitting on standards whose sequences are opposite operations, so a validator could not fix what is already wrong without 135 rows being corrected by hand first. **129 keys removed from 22 files.** Confirmed unread first: every `.js`, `.py` and `.html` in the repo outside `node_modules`, `.git` and `archive/` grepped for a property access or key read of `order` — eight hits, all unrelated (a SAIRNcare pharmacy order, `opts.order` in `med-schedule.js`, StoneDesk's order-cart CSS and route-optimiser array, a Supabase `.order()` call, and a comment). **AND PROVEN INERT AGAINST DATES, WHICH A GREP CANNOT DO:** all 184 rules carrying a `service_extension` computed under all eight recognised methods across seven trigger dates chosen to land on weekends and holidays — **10,304 cells, zero changed, zero added, zero removed.** Reloaded: the load-state check reported exactly **129 stale entries** beforehand, matching the count removed, and MISSING 0 STALE 0 EXTRA 0 after at 407 rules. Live spot-checks return the same dates and extension states as before. **One process note worth keeping: the first removal pass left 17 stray carriage returns in four files** — the pattern matched the line feed inside a CRLF — and the files still parsed and every test still passed. The only symptom was four diffs of 300-400 lines instead of 14. **Caught by checking the diff SHAPE, not the test result.** | **Michael** | — | Grepped before adding a 136th: **neither `api/_lib/deadline-engine.js` nor `api/legal-deadlines.js` ever reads `service_extension.order`.** The sequencing that actually runs comes from the STANDARD's own `sequence` field — `add_to_period_then_roll` or `roll_then_add_then_roll` — which is declared once per standard and is genuinely load-bearing. **The row-level field is inert documentation, and the four vocabularies in use do not agree with each other:** `after_base_period` on 64 rows, `after_period_expires` on 30, `after` on 30, `add_to_period` on 11, and **49 rows carry none at all**. **THE RISK IS NOT THE REDUNDANCY, IT IS THAT NOTHING CHECKS IT AGAINST THE SEQUENCE THAT RUNS.** A row reading `order: 'after'` sits on standards whose sequence is `add_to_period_then_roll` — which adds the days INSIDE the period and rolls once — and on standards whose sequence is `roll_then_add_then_roll`, which is the opposite operation. Someone reading a seed to understand a date can be told the wrong thing by a field that looks authoritative and is not. **DELAWARE CARRIES NO `order` KEY, deliberately**, and its `_readme` says why rather than leaving the absence to look like an oversight. **Fix shape, not chosen — and it is a decision because both directions are defensible:** either delete the field from all 135 rows as noise, or make it real by having the validator reject a row whose `order` contradicts its standard's `sequence`. **What must NOT happen is a third state adding one by copying a neighbour**, which is how four vocabularies became normal | S |

## What this index does NOT cover, stated so the gap is known

- **Per-app handoffs were not read line by line.** Rows come from
  `SAIRN-BACKLOG.md` (section-by-section), `SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK.md`, and work
  done in-session on 2026-08-23. There are ~40 handoff files; an item that
  exists **only** inside one and never reached the backlog is not here yet.
  That is the first thing to improve on the next rebuild.
- **Sizing is judgment, not estimation.** S/M/L is a rough shape, not a
  commitment.
- **Owners are observed, not assigned.** `unassigned` means no session has
  claimed it in the active-work log (`SAIRN-ACTIVE-WORK.md` at rebuild time;
  now the three per-session files) — not that it is unimportant. Rows
  owned by **Michael** need a decision or an access level a session does not
  have.
- **⚠️ rows carry a number or claim this rebuild could not confirm.** They say
  so in Next action.

---

## Keeping it alive

This file rots the moment it stops being updated, and a stale index is worse
than none — it makes work look handled when it is not.

1. **When you close something, move the row** to *Recently closed* with the
   commit SHA. Do not delete it silently.
2. **When you find something new, add a row** in the same action as logging it
   in `SAIRN-BACKLOG.md`. Two places, one action, or they diverge.
3. **Re-derive, don't trust.** Before a planning decision, spot-check the rows
   you are about to rely on. This rebuild found one stale backlog entry
   (SAIRNlaw trust disbursement, listed open, actually shipped) out of 26 —
   assume a similar rate next time.
4. **Do not renumber or restructure the backlog to match this file.** The
   backlog is the detailed record; this is the index over it.
